The bridge TRANSPORT is clean and provably so (mTLS `CERT_REQUIRED` + `check_hostname`, TLS 1.2 floor, no plaintext fallback, `0o600` keys, fail-closed marshal). **String-literal injection is clean on ALL SIX TIERS**, verified by carrying a verbatim triple-quoted literal containing quotes, backslashes, CR/LF/TAB, `*/`, backtick-`${}`, `</script>` and format specifiers through every emitter with no breakout.

422. (private security advisories, see SECURITY.md) **FILESYSTEM CONFINEMENT AUDIT (2026-09-02).** The workspace jail's own docstring (`backends/python/revl_fs_workspace.py:126-127`) calls `resolve_within` "the single choke point every forward op AND every inverse routes through". It is not: three path families reach a syscall without passing through it, and the existing tests assert the choke point on exactly the paths that do. ✅ **ALL SEVEN FINDINGS FIXED ON THE PY TIER, AND F1-F4 NOW FIXED ON THE TS TIER TOO (see each finding's own marker).** F1-F4 landed with the path-family enumeration (`037d14aa`, `fix/fs-confinement-escapes`); F5/F6/F7 landed on `fix/422-fs-confinement-remainder` with item 431(b) settled alongside, since it is the same `O_NOFOLLOW` walk. Every finding was re-reproduced with executed output before it was touched. **The ts tier is closed** on `fix/422-ts-tier-confinement`, which PORTED the py shape rather than redesigning it: the same `PATH_FAMILIES` enumeration, the same table-driven totality wrapper, the same sidecar-restricted inverse sources, and the ts peer of the py AST guard scan (`backends/typescript/tests/fs_confinement_families.test.ts`, parsing the shipped module with the TypeScript compiler API), with every executed escape carried as a regression in `backends/typescript/tests/fs_confinement_escapes.test.ts`. Each of the four was RE-REPRODUCED against the shipped ts module before it was touched and refused after; run against the pre-fix module the new suite fails 18 of 25 and the family scan does not even load. **Item 429 self-host check, done and answered NO:** `selfhost/` is the compiler (lexer, parser, checker, lower, six emitters) and carries no host-ref extern form, no `@ts ref` resolution, no extern BODY handling and no filesystem code, so there is nothing here for it to mirror. `revl_fs_ts.ts` is runtime host support, not compiler behaviour, and the oracles compare emitted bytes and diagnostics, neither of which this touches. Deliberate no-port, recorded rather than skipped. **One residual carried forward, not one of these seven findings.** F7's `root_kind` half: item 410 classifies ANY search-path entry as install-origin, so `revl audit` still prints a `stdlib:` ref-provenance prefix for a `REVL_IMPORT_PATH` stand-in. Re-keying that decides the ref jail and the deploy-time root, so it is a 410 design change rather than an INFO fix; the shadow note now prints beside it.
    **F1 CRITICAL, ✅ FIXED ON THE PY TIER AND ON THE TS TIER.** ✅ py (`037d14aa`, `fix/fs-confinement-escapes`): both inverse SOURCE endpoints now route through `resolve_sidecar` (`backends/python/revl_fs_workspace.py:553`), which confines the source AND requires it to be a sidecar this workspace itself produced, so a witness naming an outside file no longer steals and destroys it. ✅ ts (`fix/422-ts-tier-confinement`): the SAME executed escape was confirmed on that tier first. `fsRestore` (`:359`) handed `w.preimage` straight to `replace()` and `fsUnrm` (`:376`) did the same with `w.garbage`, confining only the destination; run against the shipped module, two files outside the workspace root were renamed INTO it and destroyed at their original locations by a witness handed to the capability-free pure inverse (`outside/secret.txt exists: true` before, `false` after, the workspace file then holding the outside file's bytes). Closed with the py shape: a `resolveSidecar` guard (family 3) that confines the source AND requires it to sit in the matching sidecar directory, so an inverse consumes only a sidecar this workspace produced. After: both refused `EOUTSIDE`, both outside files intact. The unconfined `replace`/`remove`/`writeFile`/`mkdirOne`/`rmdir`/`snapshot` primitives the deprecated `globalThis.__revlFs` seam PUBLISHED are removed rather than deprecated, since an exported unconfined rename is this finding with no witness needed. Original finding: `stdlib/fs.rvl:121` `restore` and `:132` `unrm` guard only the DESTINATION; `os.replace(w["preimage"], target)` leaves the SOURCE unchecked, and `os.replace` is a rename, so the outside source is removed. Worse, both are `pub extern **pure** fn` (verified on the IR: `restore class=pure caps=None` beside `write class=witnessed caps=['fs']`), i.e. a capability-free, prompt-free filesystem primitive callable from every pure position by ordinary model-authored revl. Executed: an outside canary is moved into the workspace and deleted from its original location, by a component holding NO fs capability. `tests/test_fs_stdlib.py:161-177` asserts only the target endpoint. Second vector (unrun): witnesses are WAL-serializable and replayed by `revl recover` (`recovery.py:522`), so a forged WAL witness feeds the same primitive with no revl source.
    **F2 HIGH, ✅ FIXED ON THE PY TIER AND ON THE TS TIER.** ✅ py (`037d14aa`): `garbage_dir()`/`preimage_dir()` delegate to `_sidecar_dir_real` (`backends/python/revl_fs_workspace.py:461`) and return a RESOLVED path created inside the root. ✅ ts (`fix/422-ts-tier-confinement`): `garbageDir()` (`:144`) and `preimageDir()` (`:152`) were the unresolved `path.join` plus `mkdirSync({recursive:true})`, which succeeds on a pre-existing symlink for the same reason `makedirs(exist_ok=True)` does. Re-reproduced: with both sidecar names planted as symlinks, one `write` and one `rm` put `WORKSPACE SECRET` and `PARKED SECRET` in an attacker-chosen directory outside the root. Closed as on py: created NON-recursively, `lstat`-checked to be a real directory and not a link (so a dangling link is caught too, which realpath normalizes happily), returned only after `resolveWithin`, and `freshSidecar` re-resolves its directory, requires it to BE one of the two, and guards the leaf. After: both `Err(EOUTSIDE)` naming the symlinked sidecar, the outside directory untouched, and the target byte-identical because the refusal lands before any byte is written. Original finding: `revl_fs_workspace.py:140-153` `garbage_dir()`/`preimage_dir()` return an UNRESOLVED join and `os.makedirs(exist_ok=True)` succeeds on a pre-existing symlink. Executed: `write` exfiltrates workspace contents outside the root, and `write`-then-`rm` writes fully attacker-controlled bytes to an attacker-chosen outside directory. Also breaks abort, since the preimage the undo needs now lives outside the boundary. `tests/test_fs_stdlib.py:180-186` does a STRING prefix check on the unresolved path and passes.
    **F3 HIGH, ✅ FIXED ON THE PY TIER AND ON THE TS TIER.** ✅ py (`037d14aa`): the write opens the leaf and `fstat`s the fd, refusing `EMULTILINK` when `st_nlink > 1` (`backends/python/revl_fs_workspace.py:717`), so a multiply-linked target cannot be witnessed-written at all; the accepted residual and its justification are stated at `:113-128`. ✅ ts (`fix/422-ts-tier-confinement`): the module had no link-count check anywhere. Re-reproduced: an inside name hardlinked to an outside canary took the attacker's bytes through the boundary, and the undo then renamed the preimage over the NAME, so the inside file read as reverted while the outside file kept the payload, residue no discharge descriptor enumerates. Closed with the py tier's REFUSAL rather than a defence that cannot exist: `openConfinedWrite` `fstat`s the fd it opened with `bigint: true` and returns `EMULTILINK` on `nlink > 1`. After: `Err(EMULTILINK)`, both names still on the shared inode holding the original bytes, and Ok-conditional registration means no inverse and nothing to mislead. Same stated cost as py: a legitimately hardlinked file inside the workspace cannot be witnessed-written. Original finding: Hardlink write-through: realpath cannot resolve a hardlink, so an inside name aliased to an outside file is ALLOWED, the forward write mutates the outside file, and the undo's `os.replace` replaces the NAME and breaks the link, so the inside file looks reverted while the outside file keeps the attacker's bytes. Silent, unenumerated residue that the discharge descriptors report as clean. `hostfile.py:21-26` documents an accepted hardlink residual for the item-396 jail whose justification does NOT transfer here (the workspace writer is untrusted by premise); `revl_fs_workspace.py` never mentions hardlinks.
    **F4 MEDIUM, ✅ FIXED ON THE PY TIER; ◑ NARROWED, NOT CLOSED, ON THE TS TIER (node has no `*at()` syscall).** ✅ py (`037d14aa`): `open_confined_write` (`backends/python/revl_fs_workspace.py:670`) resolves, walks to the parent through directory fds with `O_NOFOLLOW`, opens the leaf with `O_NOFOLLOW` and only THEN `fstat`s the fd, so there is no check-to-syscall window and nothing is truncated by a refused open. ◑ ts (`fix/422-ts-tier-confinement`): NARROWED, NOT CLOSED, and said so rather than claimed as equivalent. Re-reproduced with a `worker_threads` swapper flipping the leaf between a real file and a symlink pointing outside: the write was diverted out of the root within 88 attempts. **Node cannot express the py fix.** Its `fs` exposes no `*at()` family at all (no `openat`, `renameat`, `unlinkat`, `mkdirat`, `fstatat`, and `fs.opendirSync` yields no usable dirfd), so without a native addon the directory-fd walk does not exist there. What IS expressible was done: an `lstat` walk from the root refusing a symlinked or non-directory component immediately before each syscall, `O_NOFOLLOW` on the leaf open, a post-open `(dev, ino)` identity check of the name against the fd, every byte written THROUGH the fd, and `confirmLanded` re-establishing the same identity afterwards. After: 3396 attempts over the same 8s window, zero diversions. Residual, named: the rename-based ops have only `fs.renameSync`, which is by name; POSIX `rename` does not follow a symlink at either leaf, so the exposure is an intermediate DIRECTORY component swapped between the chain check and the call, and a ts tier with the py tier's guarantee needs an `*at()` binding. **431(b) came along on ts, because the same fd discipline is what makes it expressible:** `fs.fstatSync(fd, { bigint: true })` and `fs.lstatSync(p, { bigint: true })` give dev/ino, so `confirmLanded` compares them on the leaf against the written fd, a parted name is `Err(ERACE)` registering no inverse, and `discardWrite` removes the created leaf ONLY while it still holds our inode. The `bigint` form is load-bearing: the default `Stats` carries `ino` as a double and would compare two different files equal. One casualty: `fs.copyFileSync(COPYFILE_FICLONE)` clones BY NAME, and reopening the target by name is the race the fd exists to avoid, so the ts preimage is copied through the fd and the O(1) APFS clone is lost. Original finding: The write TOCTOU is effectively deterministic, not the "narrowed window" the docs claim (`:26-29`): `write` does exists + isfile + makedirs + uuid4 + snapshot (whose first call loads libc via ctypes) between check and syscall. A competing thread won on essentially every trial (388 leak observations over 200 trials).
    **F5 LOW/MEDIUM. ✅ FIXED** (`src/revl/placement.py`, `tests/test_sandbox_placement_411.py`). `placement.py:485` `_fs_covers` does no path normalization, so `/scratch/../../etc/shadow` is COVERED by a `/scratch:rw` mount while the direct `/etc/shadow` spelling is refused. Bounded today (slice 1 launches no jail and the needs gate is advisory) but `_fs_covers` is what slice 2 inherits for deriving real mounts. **Reproduced** on the helper directly: `_fs_covers(['/scratch:rw'], '/etc/shadow', 'rw')` False beside `_fs_covers(['/scratch:rw'], '/scratch/../../etc/shadow', 'rw')` True, and the same escape through `/./`, `//` and a bare `/scratch/..`. **Fixed by REFUSING the non-canonical spelling where it enters** rather than normalizing it silently, on both sides, because the two sides are wrong in different ways: a silently normalized NEED leaves the author believing the gate checked the spelling they wrote, and a traversing MOUNT is worse the other way, since `/scratch/..` denotes `/` and normalizing would hand the runtime the whole filesystem from a line that reads like a scratch grant. Each refusal names the canonical spelling, which is the nearest allowed space (item 274). `_fs_covers` canonicalizes again as defense in depth for the slice-2 inheritor, and fails closed DIFFERENTLY per side: a non-canonical need is canonicalized then tested (which can only narrow), a non-canonical mount covers NOTHING (canonicalizing could only widen, and a defense-in-depth pass must never be the thing that widens a grant). Stated limit kept honest in the docstring: the test is LEXICAL, no symlink on the target host is followed (this layer cannot, the planning host need not even have the paths), so the ENVELOPE the runtime binds remains the boundary and this gate stays advisory.
    **F6 LOW. ✅ FIXED** (`backends/python/revl_fs_workspace.py`, `stdlib/fs.rvl`, `tests/test_fs_confinement_escapes.py`). A NUL byte in a path raises `ValueError` out of a witnessed body instead of returning `Err`, breaking fs.rvl's stated Fallible contract. `hostfile.py:189-196` handles exactly this for the 396 jail; the fs guard did not get the same treatment. **Reproduced** end to end on the real emitted `@py` bodies: all four of `write`/`rm`/`move`/`mkdir` raised `ValueError: lstat: embedded null character in path` (from `os.path.realpath`, whose `lstat` raises `ValueError` and not an `OSError`), so a caller handling the declared `Err` arm still crashed. **Fixed structurally, not one `except` per call site.** `refuse_unusable_path` rejects the NUL at family 1, where every caller-supplied path enters, so one check covers the four forward ops and every inverse endpoint; the message names what the author can do and states the whole allowed space, because a NUL usually means raw bytes or a length-prefixed buffer was pasted where a name belongs (item 274), and the NUL never survives verbatim into the message or a WAL witness. `_make_total` then wraps EVERY entry point `PATH_FAMILIES` and `READ_HELPERS` name, so an `OSError` or `ValueError` escaping any of them becomes an `FsOpError` carrying a code, a sentence and the offending path. **The enumeration is what decides**, reusing the structure the earlier 422 fix introduced rather than adding a parallel mechanism: a fifth entry point is total the moment it is listed, and `tests/test_fs_confinement_families.py` asserts the wrapping over the same table. Private helpers are deliberately NOT wrapped, their callers translate errnos on purpose, and the totality claim is about the surface a body can reach. **TIERS: py and typescript**, the only two tiers `stdlib/fs.rvl` has bodies for. The ts guard carries the same structure — `refuseUnusablePath` at family 1 plus `makeTotal` over `PATH_FAMILIES` + `READ_HELPERS` — landed with the ts confinement work on `fix/422-ts-tier-confinement` (`915fc5cf`) and pinned by `backends/typescript/tests/fs_confinement_escapes.test.ts` ("a NUL byte in a path is refused, not thrown as a raw TypeError"). **go, rust, java and wasm are NOT APPLICABLE**: every witnessed mutation in `stdlib/fs.rvl` and every inverse it registers carries `@py` and `@ts` bodies only, so a composition that uses one is refused at emit on those four tiers (``extern `restore` has no @go body — not portable to this backend``) and there is no fs guard there to be total about.
    **F7 INFO. ✅ FIXED** (`src/revl/compiler.py`, `src/revl/__main__.py`, `tests/test_stdlib_search_path.py`). `use "stdlib/fs.rvl"` is not identity-pinned: `compiler.py:41-45` searches `REVL_IMPORT_PATH` and importer-relative paths BEFORE `stdlib_root().parent`, so reading that import and concluding "confined witnessed fs" is unsound. **Reproduced both ways**, a `stdlib/` directory beside the importing file, and one `REVL_IMPORT_PATH` entry, each yielding `extern write class=pure caps=None` with a raw unconfined `open()`, against the real module's `witnessed[fs] caps=['fs']`, from a byte-identical import line. Sharper form found while fixing it and worth recording: in the search-path case `revl audit` PRINTS the stand-in's ref provenance as `"ts": "stdlib:stdlib/evil.ts#evilWrite"`, so the audit surface itself asserts a trust domain it did not measure (item 410 classifies any search-path entry as install-origin `root_kind="stdlib"`). **NOT fixed by refusing**: a local file winning outright is item 319's design and a vendored copy is item 389's stamped, drift-checked case, both tested, so refusal would break supported behaviour. What was unsound was the SILENCE. The compile now records which file each `use "stdlib/..."` actually resolved to, with its origin, additively on the IR as `stdlib_shadow` and on stderr beside the open-holes report (same channel, same reason: a piped IR document stays exactly the IR document). Identity becomes a measured fact of the compile instead of an inference from a spelling. ADDITIVE: absent for every composition that imports the shipped modules, so existing IR documents and audits are byte-identical. **Deliberately narrower than the finding's full surface**: it says the compiled `stdlib/` module is not the one this compiler ships, not that the substitute is hostile (a vendored copy is the common honest case), and it does not re-key item 410's `root_kind`, which decides the ref JAIL and the deploy-time root, changing that is a 410 design change, not an INFO fix. The audit's `stdlib:` prefix over a `REVL_IMPORT_PATH` stand-in therefore REMAINS, now with the shadow note printed beside it; filed as the residual below.
    **NEGATIVE RESULTS.** Path traversal and normalization against `resolve_within` are CLEAN (12 stacked `../`, absolute-outside, sibling `ws-evil`, NFD unicode, embedded newline: all refused or correctly contained). Symlink resolution is correct FOR THE ARGUMENT PATH. The INVERSE cannot be steered to write outside the root (`restore` created-branch, `unmove`, `rmdir_if_empty` all refuse an outside target) — F1 is about the SOURCE. Item 396 jails HOLD on both options against absolute paths, `..`, leaf and parent symlinks, and NUL; deploy-time ref pinning is re-verified at load and fails closed. The item-411 vocabulary gate fails CLOSED on every spelling tried. The stdlib ref root is NOT attacker-steerable by env var. Two by-design 411 admissions to state plainly: a capability with no `[sandbox.needs]` entry is admitted against any envelope, and a needs entry for an unreached capability is never vocabulary-checked; with slice 1 launching no real jail, nothing in 411 is a confinement boundary today.

424. ◑ (issue #110) DESIGN DONE 2026-09-02 (docs/design/424-dsh-language-gaps.md). SLICE B1 LANDED 2026-09-06 (merged f6c5b630: interposition today, measured) + SLICE C1 LANDED 2026-09-06 (merged 3b291219: `revl export client --lang ts`). **(a) IS NOT OPEN: item 426 answers it in full**, and the design note is a citation map rather than a second design: "stable identity" is 426 decision 1 (a declared label scoped to its origin), "patch semantics that target an id" is decision 2 plus the four operations, "re-admit the patched result" is decisions 3 and 5, and 426 §5.1 quantifies the cost (one compile of the patched rows, evidence in tests/test_manifest.py:44). 426 also CORRECTS this item's own "cost, not safety" line: the ADMISSION difference is cost, the ACTIVATION difference is a guarantee, because a `replace`/`remove` is blocked by G7 rather than by effort (426 §5.2). Five residuals (a) still needs: R1 activation of replace/remove, filed by 426 §5.3 and not blocking since 424(a)'s own story says "and restarts"; **R2 the `granted` clause has no surface** though 426 §9.3 Part 2 requires one, completed here as a row clause defaulting to EMPTY with `requires` a subset of `granted` and unwritable in a stack layer; **R3 placement authority, undecided in 426 and DECIDED here by citing 337**: placement is structure so the invocation overlay may not touch it and a stack layer may not write `place` at all, because a third party choosing which tier judges its row is 337's admission-theater one tier up; R4 extension by OBSERVATION is not extension by composition, which is why 424 filed (b) separately and 426 §11 puts interception out of scope; R5 re-realming from a layer, out of scope in 426 §2.3 and the reason (b) cannot be a pattern.
    **(b) DECIDED.** Measured first: `intercept` is already taken (Def. 30 metadata on a required key, refused on a provision at lower.py:8625), a DISTINCT-key wrapper compiles today but needs the provider re-keyed IN ITS OWN SOURCE (a fork by another name), and the SAME-key wrapper via a one-element `realms(...)` route compiles, admits, passes G4 and **never runs**: run.py:747 `if comp.get("routes"): self._install_router(...); continue` installs a `_Router` proxy and never plugs the fiber, so the provide body that IS the interception is silently discarded. G4's refusal of the naive wrapper names the crux twice: a seam cannot observe with effect unless the wrapped SERVICE declares the bound (wrong owner in a layered world, the argument 426 §8.3 already made for `reach`), and interposition RENAMES the capability because capabilities are requirement keys. Decisions: the unit is the EDGE spelled as a provision claim, so G2 gives the DSH waterfall property for free with no registry and no enumeration; the COMPOSITION places a seam and a seam never registers itself, so there is no hook table to grab; a seam is a SYNTHESIZED forwarding provider, so there is no `next()` to forget; three kinds, `observe` and `decide` (a `Deny` becomes the method's `Err`, admitted only on a `Result` method) and **`rewrite` REFUSED**, because argument substitution between describe and execute is roadmap 427 F2's approve-one-run-another shape and F2 is still unfixed, so its precondition is that the ticket be computed AFTER the seam chain; capability attribution through a seam is TRANSITIVE, not the local key name, or the panel reads an insertion as a full turnover; the observed record is `Untrusted[Value]` unconditionally, since funnelling typed args into a dynamic Value would launder the taint 424 itself calls revl's largest asymmetry; the observer never receives the inner handle, so a seam can suppress a call but never mint one; and non-first-party seams inherit 426 decision 6 unchanged.
    **THE ONE RULE CHANGE, flagged for the architect rather than taken:** the composition may widen an emission bound at ONE edge for a seam, which syntax-2.0 §4b.1 currently gives to the service author alone. Cost: transitive purity of a plain `fn` weakens to "effect-free except through capabilities declared at this edge". Compensations: written by the operator not the middleware, per-edge, and it emits a `seam:` token so 426 §8.5 cannot print `clean`. FALLBACK with no rule change: restrict seams to methods that already declare a bound, which covers the harness's F13 hook story in full (every tool call is an emission) and loses the general affordance.
    **(c) DECIDED.** revl already has three answers to "the call runs elsewhere" and typert is none of them (placement seam: one composition split, everything checked; federation contract: sovereign peers, a pinned consumer surface; extern: opaque, what `revl import openapi` generates). Decisions: a remote provider is a ROW whose provider is synthesized, so wiring stays local and remoteness is an ADMISSION fact not a wiring fact (the same rule interop-bridge §3 already states, "manifest data, not source text"); remotable requires a declared emission bound, which is G4 read at the client and not a new rule, with one extern per service so the capability is one name and the reach one bound; a transport failure is a WITHDRAWAL by default (reusing R2/R3 peer-death, which the bridges implement and the suite tests) with `on_failure(result)` as the opt-in on `Result` methods, and no spelling for swallowing it; **`@RemoteScope` is a REALM and there is nothing to build**, per 426 §2.3; one wire encoding, the canonical one in interop-bridge.md; the SERVER face is a transport not a design, since `revl serve --mcp` already projects `<prefix>.<key>.<op>`; a generated client carries 338's frontier field and no safety claim; **it does NOT re-admit the callee and must not imply it does**, because 337 requires the RECEIVER to re-compile from its own source and a client is the sender (no "verified remote" badge); every returned value is `Untrusted[T]`; and a reach bound is derived from host and port only, never from userinfo (421 F4's shape, and a client generator is exactly where a URL becomes a capability spelling).
    **THE RESULT THE THREE SHARE, which changes the cost estimate.** All three answers are one primitive: synthesize a provider from a service declaration and put it in the row table. 426 §3.2's `configure` row, (b)'s seam forwarder, (c)'s remote client, and item 60's `--mock-requires` (already SHIPPED) are four kinds of the same function. (b) and (c) are therefore not two compiler projects but two callers of one synthesizer plus 426 S1's row table, which is also why both wait on 426 S1 and on nothing else in 426.
    **Slices.** (a): 426's S1-S6 unchanged, plus A1 (the `granted` clause, folds into 426 S1, buildable today except the profile wiring) and A2 (placement authority, folds into 426 S2, buildable once the row table exists). (b): **B1 BUILT** (no language change: the distinct-key wrapper is documented as the sanctioned interposition pattern in `docs/composition-rows.md` with its re-key cost stated, and the same-key `routes` hole in `run.py` `_load` is PINNED as an executed test, `tests/test_424_b1_interposition.py`: the sanctioned wrapper observes each call and the routed seam's provide body is proven absent from the driver's `fibers`, realized in `routers` instead, so the day the driver stops discarding that body the test goes red); B2 the seam row and the synthesized forwarder, needs 426 S1+S2; B3 the declared bound, transitive attribution, the `Untrusted[Value]` record and the `seam:` token, needs B2 and 426 S5; B4 the `decide` kind, needs B2. (c): **C1 buildable TODAY** (`revl export client --lang ts` and `revl serve --http`, both projections of an IR already projected this way in another transport); **C2 BUILT 2026-09-02 in PR #250** (`src/revl/synthesize.py`, `tests/test_424_remote_row.py`); C3 taint and the reach derivation, needs C2. Exit test per slice in the note §1.3, §2.6, §3.3.
    **C2 BUILT (PR #250): the `remote` row, and what it refuses to promise.** `synthesize_provider(service, kind, params)` is the function §4 predicted, with `remote` as the kind that ships; it emits ordinary revl source through the in-memory source map `compile_files` already takes, so `_link` runs G2/G3/G4 over it exactly as over a file row and nothing in the synthesizer is on the trusted path. A consumer of a remoted key does not change by one character and the rename-invariant WIRING projection is byte-identical across the local and remote compositions, which is D-424c.1 executed rather than asserted. **Teardown: NO inverse is synthesized and a remote effect survives unwind** — `bracket` needs an infallible inverse (G5) and a network inverse is fallible by construction; `transactional` (243) needs a host-local inverse plus a witness, and the peer's own claim is not a witness. This LEAVES G7 INTACT rather than straining it: G7 is LIFO-complete over REGISTERED entries and a remote operation registers none, so there is nothing for it to walk. A `compensation` (247) stays the composing engineer's to write. **Withdrawal is cheap precisely because there is nothing to undo**: a remote row has a local fiber but it holds no acquired resource and registers no teardown entry, so disposing it is a pure unwiring and the LIFO replay that blocks 426 R1 is VACUOUS — the expensive half of that residual is the half a remote row does not have. Refusals: a plain `fn` service naming the method (G4 at the client, not a new rule); `on_failure(result)` on a non-`Result` method; two peers of one service in ONE realm naming both rows (two peers are two realms, D-424c.4, no new rule); a peer address carrying userinfo REFUSED OUTRIGHT rather than accepted-and-stripped, because stripping would leave a live secret in a composition document; a method outside the JSON-transparent subset naming the method and the type; and the `ts` tier, because an `emission` method emits a SYNCHRONOUS ts function and a network round trip is not synchronous. There is no third `on_failure`. Two divergences from the sketch, both recorded in code: ONE EXTERN PER METHOD rather than per service (the property D-424c.2 names — one capability, one reach bound — holds either way, and per-method externs keep every argument's declared type at the crossing), and the wire is the placement bridge's own `{"key","method","args"}` envelope over HTTPS rather than a new one. `remote`/`at`/`host`/`through`/`on_failure` are CONTEXTUAL keywords: KEYWORDS is untouched and the self-host lexer needs no sync, the same discipline 426 S2 chose for `configure @db with { ... }`. **Declared and not yet wired: the RUNTIME half of `on_failure(withdraw)`.** The withdrawal cascade is armed by the placement bridge's monitor connection (`backends/python/bridge.py:723` `watch(on_lost)`, fired on monitor EOF at `:747`), a seam-client mechanism a synthesized row does not join — measured, not assumed. The declaration, the check, the IR contract and a fault-raising body land; a test pins them so the day the runtime lands, something says so. **Item 439's next slice is UNBLOCKED**: slice 1 routed around the row table because a provider spelled `remote` needed C2, and the row, the admissibility check, realm addressing and the `on_failure` contract are now the seam a transport binding plugs into. Taint (D-424c.9) remains C3 and the generated header says so in the artifact itself.
    **Dependencies:** 426 S1 BLOCKING for B2/C2/A1/A2, 426 S2 for B2/A2, 426 S5 for B3, 426 decision 6 / 425 F1 for B2's non-first-party seams. 337 and 338 are CITED and not extended (R3 and D-424c.7/.8). 249 taint is spent by both (b) and (c) rather than worked around. 294 closes (b)'s same-token residual and is a named blind spot, not a blocker. 350 is noted: a `remote` row's address is exactly the class of value 350 says is read before the composition exists, so it should arrive through 350's `boot` component rather than a second mechanism. **THE ORIGINAL RECORD: REVL-SIDE GAPS FROM THE DEEPSEEK-HARNESS REFERENCE COMPARISON (2026-09-02).** A full crawl of DSH's reference (45 subsystem pages, 5 concepts, 3 generated catalogs, 6 Cordis API pages, 5 cookbook pages; 54 model-facing tools, ~70 plugin packages, 51 durable event types) against revl + revl-harness. Most gaps are harness-side and belong on that roadmap. THESE THREE are language-side, because they are composition-semantics questions revl must answer before the harness can build them.
    (a) **ROW-ADDRESSED LAYERED COMPOSITION, RE-ADMITTED (the biggest structural gap, and it is not close).** DSH's composition is DATA: a bundle ships config rows, a profile names an ordered stack of bundles, a profile patch targets rows BY ID, a home patch layers on top, CLI overlays land last. A third party ships a bundle; a user overrides one row and restarts; nobody forks. revl's composition is a flat file list swapped WHOLE, so there is no row identity and therefore nothing to patch. This is NOT a soundness conflict: the resolution is that a patched composition must be RE-ADMITTED, which is exactly what `truc assemble` already does (resolve, admit, compose). The difference is cost, not safety, and the harness already hot-swaps through the gate. Exit: give composition rows stable identity, define patch semantics that target an id, and re-admit the patched result. Without this revl has no third-party extension story: someone else can only extend a revl composition by forking it. Relates to item 136 (truc, landed but unused by the harness).
    (b) **INTERCEPTION WITH DECLARED REACH.** DSH's waterfall middleware lets any loaded plugin register on `tools/pre-execute` and observe every tool call. By revl's lights that is an undeclared authority grab, and G4 exists to bound exactly that reach. But the ergonomic affordance is real and revl currently offers nothing in its place: components are wired by direct service call, so a third party has no place to stand. Exit: an interception surface whose reach is DECLARED and admitted like any other, so a middleware's authority is bounded and auditable. revl is right on safety here and DSH is right on ergonomics; the compromise is unbuilt. Prerequisite for the harness's hook story (its F13).
    (c) **TYPED REMOTE CLIENT GENERATION.** DSH's `typert` gateway generates a host and a typed client from `@Remote`-decorated classes (`POST /api/<ns>/<method>`). revl already generates typed boundaries from `service` declarations (`export_wit.py`, `import_openapi.py`), so the decorator codegen is redundant, but the CLIENT face is missing: there is no generated typed client and no `@RemoteScope` equivalent. Small extension of machinery that already exists.
    **Also recorded, not a gap but a standing advantage worth defending:** revl can wrap DSH and put an audited boundary around it (`revl import cordis` produced a real working `dsh_gateway.rvl` from DSH's own plugin inventory, marked honestly as G8 trusted-not-checked with every operation defaulted to `emission` because untyped TS carries no reversibility claim). The reverse is not available. And the single largest asymmetry in revl's favour is TAINT (item 249, complete): DSH has no analog across all 45 subsystems, so a web result flowing into an outbound send is invisible there. That capability is currently UNSPENT by the harness, which makes `web_search`/`web_fetch` behind taint the best available showcase of revl's differentiator.

425. (private security advisories, see SECURITY.md) **MCP SERVER AUDIT (2026-09-02).** The approval and effect-class machinery (items 245/246/344/294/251) is internally SOUND and held under attack. The exposure is one tier up: the MCP authoring verbs treat the driving agent as a TRUSTED HOST-CODE AUTHOR, so the whole gate rests on the agent's own effect DECLARATIONS, which are never enforced against the arbitrary Python in an `@py` body.
    **F1 HIGH, ✅ FIXED 2026-09-02** (`8033ca6f`, `fix/mcp-host-body-trust-and-path-jail`). The DECISION was taken the way this finding demanded, not left to which verb was called: the driving agent is NOT a trusted host-code author. Source arriving over the transport compiles under the item-329 untrusted-author profile with the default CLOSED (`revl mcp serve --author-trust untrusted`), which refuses a declared or reached host block; `--author-trust trusted` restores the old behaviour and stamps every class-(c) ticket with the unreviewed host bodies a yes also runs, so exploit (iii) survives in NEITHER world; `--provider MODULE.rvl` is the operator-written host code the agent may compose (Gate.propose's granted-providers map) and `--grant SERVICE` turns on the item-329 reach allowlist. A `.rvl` file inside a sanctioned root is not agent-authored, so files still compile unprofiled, and the structural half of the profile runs once before dispatch so the verbs that compile through their own module (gauntlet, quarantine, repair, canary, edit, restore) are covered too. Original finding: `mcp/server.py:54-69` (`_compile`), `:242` (`_tool_load`), `:859` (`_tool_check`), `:272` (`_tool_swap`) pass NO profile; `run.py:706` then `exec`s the compiled body; `mcp/approval.py:121-205` classifies from declared extern facts and never inspects body contents. Three executed exploits: (i) an inline `@py` body executes arbitrary Python via `revl_load` + `revl_call`, default session, zero prompts; (ii) with the approval policy ON, a body declared `pure` (classified "not a boundary crossing", `approval.py:182`) runs arbitrary side effects with NO class-(c) prompt; (iii) **approve-one / run-another** with the policy fully engaged and the operator saying yes: the ticket shows `capabilities: ['notify']` while the `@py` body reads and exfiltrates a `.env` (`AWS_SECRET_ACCESS_KEY`, `DB_PASSWORD`). The defense EXISTS and is simply unwired: the item-329 untrusted-author profile refuses exactly this shape. THE DECISION this forces: either MCP agents are untrusted authors (wire the profile, default closed, operator-controlled, following item 334's `Gate.propose` precedent) or they are trusted (in which case item 246's per-call prompt gives far less assurance than its ticket implies, and the ticket must stop misrepresenting what will execute). Exploit (iii) must not survive in either world.
    **F2 LOW-to-MEDIUM, ✅ FIXED 2026-09-02** (`8033ca6f`, then the SECOND CARRIER on `fix/425-mcp-tail`). The argument jail closed the first carrier; a `use` path written INSIDE transport-carried source was still the same oracle, because the compile follows it (`_ModuleLoader.resolve_use` joins it to the importing directory and reads what is there). Reproduced against the jailed build: `revl_check {"source": "use \"/etc/passwd\" as p ...", "modules": {"m.rvl": ...}}` returned `{"file": "/etc/passwd", "line": 1, "message": "revl has no `#` comments"}` — existence, first token and line number for any path on the machine — and `use "../secret.rvl"` compiled a file outside the root clean. Item 422's lesson exactly: a guard on the destination that does not cover the source is not a guard. FIX: `_jail_refusal` also refuses an absolute or upward-traversing `use` path in transport-carried source (`_transport_use_escapes`, same door and same refusal shape, purely syntactic so it opens and stats nothing and cannot be the oracle it closes). BOUNDARY: transport-carried source only, which is the premise `_compile` already runs on — a `.rvl` file on disk inside a sanctioned root was put there by a human, so `root/app.rvl` importing `../lib/x.rvl` is the OPERATOR's composition layout and still compiles, as do `use "stdlib/str.rvl"` through the operator's search path and any path the caller also supplies in `modules` (that resolves in memory and never reaches a filesystem). Evidence: `tests/test_mcp_authoring_trust.py` section (4b) — four refusal tests plus three pinning the legitimate shapes. Every caller-supplied path argument is jailed to the operator-sanctioned roots, defaulting to the directory the server was started in (`src/revl/mcp/server.py:173-240`). Paths resolve through realpath BEFORE the check and nothing is opened or stated first, so the refusal is not itself an oracle. The `persist.py` hypothesis was executed and closed with it: `revl_snapshot`/`revl_restore` returned an arbitrary file's whole content and are jailed on the same seam. Original finding: No path jail on `files`: `revl_check`/`admit`/`plan`/`audit`/`load`/`tools` accept absolute paths and `../` traversal. Non-revl content is not returned (the parser rejects at token 1), so it is a filesystem existence + first-token + line-number ORACLE over the entire filesystem, plus full compiled structure (summary + G8 boundary) for any path that IS valid revl, none of it confined to an operator-sanctioned directory. `persist.py:89` (`revl_snapshot`/`revl_restore`) opens a caller-supplied path and is a second, untested path surface (hypothesis).
    **F3 MEDIUM, ✅ RESOLVED (was ◑ half closed), AND THE MARKER `folded into the item-416c fix` WAS WRONG.** The 416c fix (`0dd4c040`) redacts a resource dimension the author declared `Secret[T]`, which is exactly the half this finding says is NOT sufficient. ✅ closed: a `Secret[T]` resource param binds to the redacted placeholder before the spelling reaches the ticket, the WAL or the MCP wire (`approval.bind_resource_scope`, `src/revl/mcp/approval.py:552-560`). ✅ THE OTHER HALF IS CLOSED TOO, on `fix/425-mcp-tail` (`14f0e89f`, merged `89ea9bd6`): the ticket now carries `resourceScopesFromCallerArgs` and a `resourceScopeDurability` sentence naming the durable log, and `--approval-record-values {bound,withheld}` makes the durability an operator decision. The full record, including what the fix deliberately is NOT, is at the end of this block. ◑ WAS STILL OPEN, verified in source on `2d2fa07f` and reproduced before the fix below: an UNDECLARED resource param is bound exactly as before, by design, so a sensitive value passed through a PLAIN `Str` parameter named `path`/`host`/`table` is still persisted verbatim; and `Session.replay_forward` (`src/revl/mcp/session.py:4073-4074`) still copies raw `item["args"]` into its result, which is how it reaches the model's context. Original finding: Class-(c) runtime ARGUMENT VALUES leak verbatim into the outstanding ticket, `_approval_records`, and the `approve_ticket` grant record written by `wal.record_approval_granted` (`approval.py:329-359` `bind_resource_scope`, `:423-434` copied into the ticket body after the hash; `session.py:2612-2615`, `:2963-2966`; and `:3858-3868` where `replay_forward` copies `exc.ticket` and raw `item["args"]` into its result, which is how it reaches the MODEL's context). Scope note that matters: the landed `Secret[T]` redaction (`ad995a74`) is NOT sufficient here, because it keys off the declared type. A sensitive value passed through a PLAIN `Str` parameter named `path`/`host`/`table` (the `cap_order._REGISTRY` resource kinds) is bound into the capability spelling and persisted untouched. **RESOLVED 2026-09-02 on `fix/425-mcp-tail`; 425 F3 and 427 F5 are ONE residual, recorded on both so it cannot be refiled into a gap again.** Reproduced end to end first (plain `Str` `path` param, canary `/var/secrets/tenant-9f2c/prod-db-dump.sql`): the canary appeared in the ticket, in `_approval_records`, and literally on disk in `~/Library/Application Support/revl/approval-wal/revl-approval-<session>.wal`. WHAT THE FIX IS NOT, and why: redacting harder is unavailable here. Blanket-redacting the ticket makes it unanswerable (an operator who cannot see the target cannot decide, and on a fresh call the value is the CALLER'S OWN, just sent over the same wire the ticket goes back on, so hiding it protects nothing); and a placeholder in the LEDGER is worse than the leak, because every distinct target folds to one shape and a rule minted over it would cover all of them, which is the over-authorization `mint_standing_grant` already refuses a placeholder-scoped grant for. Redaction also cannot be applied to one sink only: every consumer compares SPELLINGS, so it is bind-time-or-nothing (which is why 416c is at `bind_resource_scope`). And the verbatim record is not incidental, it IS item 251's N1: `test_n1_ledger_records_the_bound_host_not_a_hash` and `test_an_ordinary_host_still_renders_verbatim_in_the_wal` assert it deliberately. WHAT WAS ACTUALLY WRONG: the promotion was INVISIBLE and unbounded. A call's arguments reach the ticket only as `argsDigest`, EXCEPT at a parameter whose NAME happens to sit in `cap_order._REGISTRY` (`path`/`host`/`table`), whose runtime VALUE is lifted out, bound into the capability spelling, and on a yes written verbatim to a durable cross-session plaintext log. Nothing told the author that naming a parameter `path` changes where its values end up, nothing told the operator that a yes persists this one, and nothing bounded it. FIX, two parts. (1) The ticket stops understating what a yes means (the item-425-F1 `unreviewedHostCode` move): `bind_resource_scope` now reports whether a bound dimension came from a CALLER ARGUMENT or from an author-written LITERAL, and a caller-valued crossing carries `resourceScopesFromCallerArgs` plus `resourceScopeDurability`, which names the durable log, the `argsDigest` asymmetry, and both escapes (item 274). (2) The durability becomes an explicit OPERATOR decision, `revl mcp serve --approval-record-values {bound,withheld}` (`Session.approval_record_values`, carried across `fork`): `withheld` records a caller-supplied valuation as `None`, the resource-bearing-but-UNRECORDED shape `distill._project_resource` already fails closed on, and drops its bound spelling back to the bare token in `classCCapabilities` too, since the distiller reads an inline-parameterised entry in PREFERENCE to the `resourceScopes` map so withholding one channel alone would leave the value in the other. Verified: canary absent from the WAL file on disk under `withheld`, present under `bound`. THE COST, stated: under `withheld` a series of approvals whose target came from a caller argument no longer folds into a distilled standing rule. Author-written LITERAL targets are recorded under both modes and distill unchanged, so a composition that names its destinations in source pays nothing. DEFAULT WAS `bound` (what shipped), deferred to item 251's owner. **SETTLED 2026-09-02 on `fix/427-clock-and-record-default`: the default is now `withheld`.** Reasoning, because the flip is a real trade and not a tidy-up. The two mistakes are not symmetric. Recording is IRREVERSIBLE and SILENT — the value lands in a plaintext cross-session file on disk, it belongs to the caller rather than to the operator who said yes, and which values land there is decided by whether a parameter happens to be NAMED `path`/`host`/`table`, which is not a property anyone chose. Withholding is neither: its entire cost is that approvals whose target came from a caller argument no longer fold into a distilled rule, and re-serving with `--approval-record-values bound` gets that back. Everywhere else in this machinery the unset default is the fail-closed one; this applies that rule here. The blocking objection was that flipping would SILENTLY disable N1, and that half is fixed rather than accepted: `distill.Reason.RESOURCE_SCOPE_UNRECORDED`'s refusal now names this flag, so the operator who loses a fold is told why and what to change. The ticket's `resourceScopeDurability` sentence also became per-mode (`ClassMap.build_ticket(record_values=...)`) — a disclosure describing the other session's behaviour is worse than none, since it trains an operator to skip the field. Blast radius of the flip, measured: exactly three existing assertions changed, all of them the "records the caller value" ones, and each now opts into `bound` explicitly; nothing in the auto-approve matcher, the ticket's live decision surface or the author-literal path moved, because `_distillation_ledger_fields` only governs the LEDGER copy. THIRD OPTION WEIGHED AND REJECTED (negative result): recording a SCOPED DERIVATIVE rather than the raw value — a per-segment keyed hash preserving the equality and longest-common-prefix joins `_resource_join` needs — does not work here. The distilled artifact is a rule a human READS and applies (`AutoApproveRule.to_dsl()`), and a rule spelling `fs.write(path="/9f3a/ab12")` cannot be reviewed; the auto-approve matcher must re-derive the same tokens at every later crossing, so the key has to outlive the session and sit beside the log it is protecting; and path/host segments are low-entropy enough that a keyed hash over them is a dictionary attack, not a redaction. The fold and the plaintext are the same fact, which is why this is a posture and not a transform. Both behaviours are now pinned by tests, so the default is a choice rather than an accident. NEGATIVE RESULT on the `replay_forward` half: it is NOT a cross-principal disclosure. A snapshot carries SOURCES, not recorded calls, and `restore`/`resume` boot a fresh runtime with a fresh recorder (`persist.restore` -> `Session.load`), so every step a session can replay was recorded by a call that session's own driver made and the raw args it echoes are that driver's own. Pinned by `test_a_restored_session_replays_no_earlier_principals_arguments`. The one shape not constructible from the MCP surface is a REPL-origin step (`replay_forward` has a branch for it) reaching a Session timeline; if a path is ever added that imports recorded steps from another principal, that echo becomes a real leak and needs step provenance to fix. Evidence: `tests/test_416c_approval_and_model_leaks.py`, five new tests under the 425 F3 / 427 F5 heading; docs/harness-gate-guide.md "What a yes also writes down".
    **F5 MEDIUM, ✅ FIXED 2026-09-02** (`9f0cc641`, `fix/ticket-overmint-and-call-approval`). `approve_ticket` is idempotent against a ticket that already minted: a duplicate call returns the SAME entry instead of appending a second unconsumed ledger row, so a legitimate resend still succeeds and an attacker gets no extra fireable approval. Same commit as 427 F7. Original finding: `session.py:2924-2972` `approve_ticket` neither retires the outstanding ticket nor rejects a duplicate mint, so N approvals of one ticket append N unconsumed ledger entries and one human "yes" buys N crossings. The per-ENTRY single-use guarantee holds; it is not a per-TICKET guarantee.
    **F4 LOW (fails closed), ✅ FIXED 2026-09-02** (`9f0cc641`, `8033ca6f`, then the SECOND SURFACE on `fix/425-mcp-tail`). The shape, not just the one verb: `ApprovalRequired` is not a `SessionError`, and `revl_call` was not the only broad `except Exception` sitting on `Session.call`. `ComposedServer._call_tool` (`src/revl/mcp/composed.py`, the fourth quadrant — a booted composition serving its OWN operations) still swallowed it. Reproduced against a live cordis session under `approval_policy = "auto"`: `tools/call app.ops.shout` returned `{"ok": false, "raised": true, "message": "ApprovalRequired: approval required for a class-(c) crossing"}` with no ticket and no hash. Fail-closed there too (the emission did not fire), and worse than on `revl_call`, because that wire advertises only the composition's operations, so with no hash to relay the crossing could never be approved at all. FIX: the two-step renderer moved to `approval.two_step_payload` beside the exception it renders, so the two surfaces cannot drift apart again, and each supplies its own item-274 "what you can do instead" (this one names the operator's `revl_approve` on the compiler server, since there is no approve verb on its own wire). No fail-open: the dedicated arm sits BEFORE the catch-all and only turns the refusal into a relayable result. Evidence: `tests/test_mcp_serve.py::test_a_class_c_crossing_returns_the_ticket_not_an_opaque_fault` (runtime-free) and `::test_a_live_class_c_crossing_surfaces_the_real_ticket` (cordis). `_tool_call` re-raises `ApprovalRequired` past its broad `except Exception` (`src/revl/mcp/server.py:642-645`) so the class-(c) ticket two-step renders the way `_tool_load`/`_tool_swap` already did, and `handle()`'s dedicated arm is live for that verb. Original finding: `server.py:258-270`: `ApprovalRequired` is not a `SessionError`, so the broad `except Exception` converts it to a generic diagnostic with no ticket or hash. The emission does not fire, but a class-(c) call through `revl_call` can never be APPROVED because the model never receives a hash, and `handle()`'s dedicated `except ApprovalRequired` is dead code for that verb.
    **F6 MEDIUM, ✅ FIXED 2026-09-11 ON THE MISSING UPPER BOUND AND ON THE `NaN`/`Infinity` ADMISSIONS; THE REFUSAL WORDING REMAINS DELIBERATELY UNCHANGED AND PER-CALLER TOKENS ARE STILL OPEN** (`fix/425-f6-lease-ttl-bound`). No lease on any path was bounded ABOVE: `LeaseBook.claim` validated only `ttl <= 0` and `LeaseBook.reinstate` only `now >= expiry`, so a lease could be minted for ~31 years and the module had no `MAX_TTL`. `expiry` and `holder` arrive TOGETHER in the caller-supplied snapshot document `persist._restore_leases` rehydrates, so an operator holding only the `restore` grant — a DIFFERENT grant from `swap` (`operator.py:51-54`) — could re-seat a fence over a name no operator token matches. `check_swap`'s exemption needs `lease.holder == me` and `release` is holder-checked, so no real operator was exempt from that fence and none could lift it: a veto over `revl_swap`/`revl_repair` for the whole workspace under `leases enforced` that only a process restart cleared. The module's own property ("a walked-away agent never wedges the workspace") was thus true for every input EXCEPT the one arriving over the wire, and the same hole was open on `claim` itself, whose `ttl` is a wire argument. `MAX_TTL = 86400.0` closes it on BOTH minting paths — `claim` REFUSES a longer `ttl`, so a caller learns its lease is not what it asked for, while `reinstate` CLAMPS, because a rehydrate's holder may not be around to re-claim — which makes the documented invariant total instead of input-dependent and bounds the worst case to a delay that self-clears on the schedule a real claim could have asked for. Three adjacent holes on the same untrusted path close with it: `not (ttl > 0)` and `not (expiry > now)` in place of `<=` and `>=`, because `NaN` and `Infinity` are literals every permissive JSON reader accepts — a `NaN` TTL previously passed `ttl <= 0`, so `claim` reported SUCCESS for a lease that was never live (`now < nan` is false) and a co-agent could believe it held a name it did not, and a `NaN` expiry did the same through `reinstate`, while an `Infinity` expiry now clamps like any other over-long one; `acquired` is normalized to a real instant no later than its own expiry so a non-finite one cannot reach `revl_state`; and `_restore_leases` verifies `meta["leases"]` is a LIST, its "a malformed record is skipped, never fatal" contract having to hold for the container too. The wire schema advertises the bound (`revl_lease`'s `ttl` gains `maximum`/`exclusiveMinimum`) and `docs/component-leases.md` states it in the verb and persistence sections. Regression: `tests/test_mcp_leases.py` (6 new: a claim past the horizon is refused and exactly the horizon accepted; a non-positive, `NaN` or `Infinity` TTL mints nothing; a restored lease clamps; a non-finite expiry is clamped or dropped; `acquired` is normalized; a forged fence self-clears and cannot be released by hand) and `tests/test_mcp_edit_gates.py` (2 new, driven through `revl_restore`). The six #847 restore-fence invariants are unchanged and re-run. Advisories: GHSA-5h82 reports this fence direction (a forged FOREIGN holder, not the self-holder exemption #847 closed) and names the missing clamp as its Fix direction item 2; item 1's "drop the lease or restore it inert" alternative was NOT taken, because dropping loses the cross-operator fence the persistence section promises and an inert record is what `verified: False` already makes it. Item 3 is half closed — the why-trace detail already says "re-seated from a snapshot — not claimed in this session" for an unverified lease, but the refusal SENTENCE keeps the ordinary foreign-holder wording, deliberately, because #847's test `test_a_restored_lease_still_fences_everyone_else` asserts that path is indistinguishable from a real claim. Item 4 (per-caller tokens, item 39) is untouched, so the advisory stays DRAFT rather than closed.
    **NEGATIVE RESULTS (on record as attacked and held).** Session/generation confusion: candidate-hash invalidation HOLDS (an approval minted before a semantically-changed swap does not fire afterward; the re-issue re-prompts with a new hash), per-entry single-use HOLDS, and `swap` replaces `self._tickets` so stale hashes do not survive a generation change. Input robustness: wrong types, missing required fields and deeply-nested input all fail CLOSED with no transport crash and no fail-open. Approval replay: a CONSUMED ledger entry cannot be reused; the only over-fire is F5's over-mint.
    **COVERAGE.** Driven end to end: check, load, call, swap, approve, timeline, fork, replay_forward, inspect_step, fmt, explain, scaffold, state (plus admit/plan/audit/ship/tools statically through the shared `_compile`). NOT separately driven, mostly funnelling through the same `_compile`/`Session.call` chokepoints: snapshot/restore, repair/gauntlet/quarantine, resolve, canary, query_*, live_query, history_*, lease, distillation_*, revoke, rollback/undo/unload, commit/commit_confirm/abort, edit, fork_confirm, step_back, replay_bisect. A follow-up pass should drive those, especially the three with their own path or candidate-source surfaces.

426. ◑ (issue #111) DESIGN DONE 2026-09-02 (docs/design/426-composition-layers.md, which SUPERSEDES two independent designs kept beside it: 426-composition-layers-truc-first.md and 426-composition-layers-composition-first.md. Run as competing framings deliberately, because the open question "does a patch address a file or a component" is exactly where they diverge). S1/S2 LANDED (the composition row table + the composition fold: layers, four operations, peer refusal) and S3 INCREMENTAL ADMISSION LANDED 2026-09-06 (merged 85aed59e: incremental admission over the resolved layer delta). RESOLVED: identity is a declared label scoped to its origin (`<origin>::@<label>`) with the `(key, realm)` claim set as what G2 checks, so uniqueness is free and a label survives both a rename and an upstream surface addition; a patch addresses the PROVISION CLAIM, never a file (compile_files merges every module before _link sees anything, so a file-addressed patch names an object the checker does not model) and never a component name (that is an implementation detail upstream may rename); an address resolving to nothing is a REFUSAL, never a no-op; resolution is a PURE FOLD that never calls the gate (per-op admission makes remove-then-add succeed while add-then-remove fails on G2 at an intermediate state) and the resolver is not on the trusted path, so `_link` runs G2/G3 unchanged and a resolver bug can only over-refuse; PRECEDENCE NEVER CHOOSES A PROVIDER, conflicts refuse and only the operator's site layer resolves by naming both sides; incremental admission rides `admit_into`, which already is patch admission at the verdict level, with the honest limit that activation is ADDITIVE-ONLY because replace/remove are blocked by G7 (teardown LIFO plus consumer re-resolution), making configure-only patches the highest-value narrowing; non-first-party rows always compile under the item-329 untrusted-author profile, so the row is the unit of confinement as well as identity; distribution is truc's and semantics are the composition's, and when they disagree THE COMPOSITION IS SOURCE OF TRUTH while the lock is the integrity proof; and the composition becomes a checked first-class declaration in revl, which SHRINKS the bootstrap floor and subsumes placement.py's [processes]/[config.X].
    **The synthesis result neither input had: closing one critical PROMOTES the other.** Design A found the authority panel is computed from the attacker's own DECLARATIONS (unenforced against `@py` bodies, roadmap 425 F1: declare everything `pure` and the panel prints zero additions). Design B independently found the panel is blind to CONFIG (a layer that is only `configure @db { url: "postgres://attacker.example/app" }` changes no token, no reach and no wiring edge, so it prints `authority-drift: clean`). Once A's fix removes the unchecked body, the ONLY remaining way for a layer to gain authority is to steer host code already present, and the only lever is config; and B's fix does nothing about a `pure`-declared exfiltrating body, which changes no config and so passes the unchanged-config conjunct. BOTH fixes are load-bearing. The panel therefore leads with a TRUST BASIS line (MEASURED / MEASURED-with-first-party-bodies-trusted / CLAIMED), always prints its BLIND SPOTS, forfeits the word `clean` under `--trust-host-code`, and keys crossing tokens by ROW LABEL rather than component name (else a rename reads as a full authority turnover) with a value DIGEST on config tokens (else one `--accept` in a CI file accepts every future value of that field forever, which is roadmap 427 F3's standing-grant shape reintroduced).
    **NEW CRITICAL the merge introduces, ❌ STILL UNFIXED, RE-VERIFIED IN SOURCE ON `837c7035` (2026-09-05; re-confirmed `compiler.compile_files` at `src/revl/compiler.py:557-560` still takes exactly ONE `profile: AdmissionProfile | None` for the whole merged program):** "non-first-party" is not a checked fact anywhere in the merged model, and the mechanism that would enforce it is whole-compile. A layer writes `from "path"` and under the composition-declared model the path is a fact the LAYER writes, so it picks its own trust level and the profile decision evaporates. Worse, `compile_files` (`compiler.py:381`) takes exactly ONE profile for the merged program, while the pure-fold decision wants one call for the whole delta and the confinement decision wants mixed trust per row: two calls reintroduce the intermediate-state G2 nondeterminism the fold exists to kill, and one profile either refuses the project's own legitimate externs or drops the profile, with the pressure entirely toward dropping it because that is the direction that makes tests pass. Fix (designed, not built): trust class becomes a property of the DECLARING DOCUMENT (a stack layer is non-first-party whatever its `from` path names, and the path must resolve inside its own vendored directory); `compile_files` takes `profiles: dict[rootpath, AdmissionProfile]` with the link running once unprofiled, for which the seam ALREADY EXISTS since `check_no_extern` is per-root at `compiler.py:212-214`; and the two genuinely whole-compile flags take the join in the safe direction.
    **Slices:** S1 the row table (buildable now, depends on nothing, pays for itself by turning the harness's two measured composition bugs into compile errors); S2 the fold (no truc, layers are files in a directory, where determinism is testable); S3 incremental admission (nothing new, evidence in tests/test_manifest.py:44); S4 the confinement split (waits on 425 F1's decision plus the per-root profile split, the largest compiler change, everything answering critical A is downstream); S5 the panel (waits on S4 and 428 F3, not worth shipping before S4 since without it the headline IS critical A's green screen); S6 distribution (waits on 428 F3 alone).
    **Dependencies:** 428 F3 (the optional truc pin) is BLOCKING and 425 F1 is BLOCKING for the confinement decision, though 425's fix wires the profile at the MCP verbs, a different call site, so this needs a NEW wiring rather than that branch landing. 428 F5 and F2 wanted; 428 F1/F9 not on the path since this design signs nothing. Also found while merging and not in the brief: 421 F1 matters, because `lower.py:9487` `_cap_keyed` corrupts exactly the G8 surface `audit_report` reads, so until it lands the panel must not print a capability label on a crossing reached through a spawn handle. **COMPOSITION ROWS, LAYERED PATCHES, AND ADMITTED EXTENSION (the resolution to item 424a; DESIGN-FIRST).** Item 424(a) records the gap: DSH's composition is DATA (bundles ship config rows, profiles stack bundles, patches target rows BY ID, layers resolve bundles then profile then home then CLI), so a third party ships a bundle and a user overrides one row without forking. revl's composition is a flat file list swapped WHOLE, so there is no row identity and nothing to patch. This item is the resolution, and the claim is that revl can do this STRICTLY BETTER than DSH rather than merely catch up.
    **Why it is not a soundness conflict.** The apparent conflict is that a patch inserting an arbitrary row would defeat G2/G4. It does not, because a patched composition is a CANDIDATE, not a live composition: it must be RE-ADMITTED, which is exactly the pipeline `truc assemble` already runs (resolve, admit, compose). The difference from DSH is cost, not safety, and the harness already hot-swaps a re-admitted composition through the gate in `ship_files()`.
    **The design, in five parts.** (1) Give composition entries STABLE IDENTITY, so an entry can be addressed without being replaced wholesale. (2) Define patch operations against an id: add, replace, remove, and set-config. (3) Define layer resolution in DSH's order (bundle stack, profile patch, home patch, invocation overlay), producing a candidate composition deterministically. (4) RE-ADMIT the candidate; a refusal is DATA with a why-trace naming which rule the patch broke, not a crash, and the running composition is untouched (the no-blip discipline the swap path already has). (5) On admission, hot-swap to the new generation.
    **Why this beats DSH's model rather than imitating it.** DSH's layering is UNVERIFIED: a home patch can silently break an invariant and the operator discovers it at runtime. revl's layering is ADMISSION-CHECKED, so a patch that widens authority, races a provision key (G2), or reaches farther than declared (G4) is refused BEFORE anything runs, with a navigable trace. Better still, revl already computes authority drift (`revl audit`, `revl diff`, `composition_diff`), so an operator can be shown "this third-party bundle widens your fs capability to /etc" BEFORE applying it. DSH cannot offer that at all, and it is exactly the property that makes third-party extension safe rather than merely convenient. The ecosystem story and the safety story stop being in tension.
    **Open questions for the design pass.** What is the identity of a row (a name, a content hash, a declared id) and how does it survive a source edit? Does a patch address a FILE or a COMPONENT (revl's unit is the component, DSH's is the config row)? How do bundles interact with `truc`'s existing package identity (item 136) rather than duplicating it? Can a patch be admitted INCREMENTALLY (re-admit only the affected subgraph) or must the whole composition be re-admitted, and what does that cost on a large composition? And what is shown to a user approving a third-party bundle: the authority DIFF is the obvious answer and it should be the headline of the UX.
    **Sequencing.** This is the largest single unlock for the harness's ecosystem story and it is a language-side question, so it lands here before the harness builds bundles on top. Relates to 136 (truc, landed but unused by the harness), 424 (the gap record), and 424(b) (interception with declared reach, the other half of a third-party extension story).

427. (issue #174) **APPROVAL AND POLICY AUDIT (2026-09-02).** The classification FOLD itself is sound and resisted a shape battery. Every bypass found routes AROUND the fold (a stale map) or LIES TO it (a binding with no dataflow to what executes), rather than evading its edges. That reframes the risk: the checks are largely fine, the plumbing feeding them is not. Triage order the auditor recommends: F1, then F6, then F2/F3/F4, then F5/F7/F8.
    **F1 CRITICAL, ✅ FIXED 2026-09-02** (`7f1c63ef`, `fix/admit-classmap-bypass`). `_wire_turn` now builds the post-admission composition BEFORE anything is plugged, runs the activation and lease gates over the turn's own components against it, and after the plug installs the class map, the distilled auto-approve rules, the cache index and the owner's reach-closure candidate hashes, in the same order `load` and `swap` install them; a refusal happens before the plug, so the running composition is untouched. `Gate.admit` routes through the approver seam, so an activation ticket raised at wire time reaches the embedder instead of escaping the facade. The two sub-findings below closed with it: the unresolved-classification branch REFUSES rather than reading `None` as class none, and `ClassMap._provider_of` no longer folds the per-`(key, realm)` provider map to the first match, so an ambiguous key resolves to `None` into that now-closed branch. Reproducers in `tests/test_admit_approval_gate.py` (406 lines). Original finding: `session.py:2170-2225` `_wire_turn` mutates `self.ir["components"]/["services"]/["manifest"]` and `driver.ir` and rebuilds NOTHING, while `load` (`:475`) and `swap` (`:923`) both rebuild the class map. `classify_call(turnKey, m)` then finds no provider and returns `None`, and `:2649` treats that as "not a boundary call" and the crossing FIRES. Executed: a granted class-(c) tool called directly raises a ticket; the same crossing through an admitted turn returns None with prompts/ledger/grants all unchanged and the sink written. Sharper variant: a crossing in the turn's ACTIVATION body fires at wire time, because `_enforce_activation_gate` is not run on admit either. Through the public `revl.gate.Gate` facade with an approver that DENIES EVERYTHING, the approver was never consulted and the sink was written. The item-329 profile is not a mitigation: the turn needs no host code, it forwards to a GRANTED provider. Also corrupts the roadmap-248 auto-approve measurement, since bypassed crossings never enter the denominator.
    **The fail-open behind it:** `:2649` treats "I could not resolve this" identically to "this is not a boundary call". It must refuse. Second route to a wrong answer on the same branch (hypothesis, misclassification demonstrated but no crossing fired): `approval.py:229-236` `ClassMap._provider_of` collapses the `(key, realm)` provider map and returns the FIRST match, so `classify_call` reports class `None` for a key whose other-realm provider is class (c). The fold should refuse an ambiguous key rather than pick one.
    **F2 HIGH, ✅ FIXED 2026-09-02** (`6e65eebc`, `fix/427-approval-fold-bypasses`). The binding is now a DATAFLOW fact instead of a positional coincidence. `bind_resource_scope` takes the calling SCOPE, and a resource dimension binds only from a proven source at the extern call sites inside it: a `{kind: lit}` string argument binds to that literal (it is what executes, whatever the caller passed), and a `{kind: name}` argument naming a provide-method parameter the scope never rebinds (`let`/`assign`/`for`/`effect`) binds to the caller's arg at THAT parameter's index. Everything else refuses: a computed expression, a rebound name, an extern reached THROUGH a pure fn (`facts["externs"][...]["through"]`), disagreeing call sites, and a token whose class-(c) origin inside the reach closure is any scope other than the one the caller's args land in (`binding_scope`, over a new per-scope classC origin map and `closureScopes`). A refused token keys BARE (wide but true) and the ticket carries `resourceScopeRefusals[token]`, a sentence naming the fix the author can enact per item 274: forward the provide-method's own parameter into the extern's resource parameter, or pass a string literal. Four canary shapes now agree shown-vs-executed (`tests/test_427_approval_fold_bypasses.py`); before the fix, the literal-destination and helper-indirection shapes showed the caller's `api.stripe.com/SEKRIT-CANARY-APV` while posting to `attacker.example`, and a benign `(body, host)` vs `(host, body)` position swap showed `host="payload"` while posting to the canary. The original finding: `approval.py:329-359` `bind_resource_scope` indexes the CALLER's positional args by the DECLARING EXTERN's parameter list. They coincide only when the provide-method passes its parameters straight through in the same positions, and nothing checks that: there is no dataflow from the service arg to the extern arg. Executed: the ticket, the ledger and the distilled rule shape key all read `http_post(host="api.stripe.com/SEKRIT-CANARY-APV")` while the host body posted to `attacker.example`. The operator narrowed the grant to exactly what they were shown, and it auto-approved. This is approve-one-run-another on the exact axis item 251 section 6 N1 introduced to prevent it. `_auto_rule_covers` (`session.py:3124`) and `_grant_covers` (`:2936`) both consume the fiction.
    **F3 HIGH, ✅ FIXED 2026-09-02** (`6e65eebc`, `fix/427-approval-fold-bypasses`). `mint_standing_grant` now reads `ticket["classCCapabilities"]` (the spellings the operator was shown and the exact set `_find_standing_grant` enforces) so the string minted is a string the operator read, and the mint and the gate can no longer disagree about which cone was consented to. Naming the bare token explicitly is now a WIDENING of the ticket and is refused with a message pointing at the proactive `revl_approve(capability=...)` mint for a genuinely wider cone. The regression asserts identity, not equality: the minted `capability` and the record written to the WAL are the SAME `str` object as `ticket["classCCapabilities"][0]`. The original finding: `session.py:3269-3276` `mint_standing_grant` reads `ticket["capabilities"]` (the worst-class fold, BARE tokens) while the operator is shown `ticket["classCCapabilities"]` (the resource-bound spellings). So `revl_approve(hash=..., uses=N)` with no explicit capability mints against the bare token, which tops the whole cone. Executed: shown `http_post(host="api.stripe.com")`, minted `http_post`, and a call to `evil.example` auto-approved. Contradicts `_grant_covers`'s own docstring, which says a grant for `fs.write(path="/tmp")` must NOT cover bare `fs.write`.
    **F4 HIGH, ✅ FIXED 2026-09-02** (`6e65eebc`, `fix/427-approval-fold-bypasses`). `candidate_hash` now folds in the semantic entries of the externs AND pure functions the closure components reach (`_reached_host_code`, a fixpoint over both lowered call encodings), keyed as `{components, externs, functions}`. So a swap that rewrites only an `@py` body recomputes a different hash and every token pinned to the old closure fails CLOSED at the existing `continue` in `_find_standing_approval` / `_live_grant_for`: not found, one fresh prompt, no error and no silent pass. CORRECTED 2026-09-29: this holds for an extern the closure CALLS by name, not for one it reaches only as a function value. `_reached_host_code` still walks called names, so for `let g = charge` then `g(n)` it reaches nothing, and a swap that rewrites only `charge`'s `@py` body leaves the hash identical (reproduced on `67fc027b7`): issue #1545, PR 1546 in review. Reach is per-component over all its scopes, the same granularity the component entry already had, so the hash still does not depend on which method the call entered and two compiles of the same source still agree (both properties are pinned by tests). WHAT THIS INVALIDATES: standing grants and single-use tokens are session-scoped in-memory state, so nothing durable is revoked and no existing session breaks at upgrade; what newly stops carrying is a token held across a swap that changes ONLY an extern body, a pure fn body, or an extern's declared params/class/capabilities without touching the component entry. Those crossings now re-prompt, which was the point. Candidate hashes recorded in an older WAL are audit text and are never matched against. The original finding: `approval.py:301-310` `candidate_hash` is taken over `ir["components"]` minus `source`; `ir["externs"]` (the `@py` host bodies that ARE the crossing) are not in it, though the docstring claims a swap of "changed behaviour, same names" recomputes a different hash. Executed: two IRs differing ONLY in the extern host body's destination produce an IDENTICAL hash, and a grant minted against one survives a swap to the other with no prompt. Affects `_find_standing_approval`, `_live_grant_for`, and item-310 cache entries.
    **F5 HIGH, ✅ RESOLVED (was ◑ half closed), AND THE MARKER `folded into the item-416c fix` WAS WRONG** (same defect as 425 F3, same evidence). ✅ closed by `0dd4c040`: a resource dimension declared `Secret[T]` binds to the redacted placeholder before the ticket, the WAL and the MCP wire. ✅ THE OTHER HALF IS CLOSED TOO, on `fix/425-mcp-tail` (`14f0e89f`, merged `89ea9bd6`) — one residual wearing two item numbers, recorded on both so it cannot be refiled into a gap again; the full record is on 425 F3. ◑ WAS STILL OPEN on `2d2fa07f`, reproduced before the fix below: an UNDECLARED resource param binds verbatim by design (`src/revl/mcp/approval.py:552-560` says so), so a sensitive value through a plain `Str` `path`/`host`/`table` param still reaches all three sinks, and `replay_forward` (`src/revl/mcp/session.py:4073-4074`) still echoes raw `item["args"]`. Original finding: Runtime argument values reach the ticket, the MCP wire response AND the durable WAL file, confirmed end to end in all three sinks. `cap_order` canonicalization refuses values containing `=` or `"`, which narrows but does not close it (dots, slashes and spaces pass). Note `session.py:2568` explicitly contrasts this with the `argsDigest` hash: the digest was the safe choice and the structured projection deliberately un-hashed it. **RESOLVED 2026-09-02 on `fix/425-mcp-tail`; 425 F3 and 427 F5 are ONE residual, recorded on both so it cannot be refiled into a gap again.** Reproduced end to end first (plain `Str` `path` param, canary `/var/secrets/tenant-9f2c/prod-db-dump.sql`): the canary appeared in the ticket, in `_approval_records`, and literally on disk in `~/Library/Application Support/revl/approval-wal/revl-approval-<session>.wal`. WHAT THE FIX IS NOT, and why: redacting harder is unavailable here. Blanket-redacting the ticket makes it unanswerable (an operator who cannot see the target cannot decide, and on a fresh call the value is the CALLER'S OWN, just sent over the same wire the ticket goes back on, so hiding it protects nothing); and a placeholder in the LEDGER is worse than the leak, because every distinct target folds to one shape and a rule minted over it would cover all of them, which is the over-authorization `mint_standing_grant` already refuses a placeholder-scoped grant for. Redaction also cannot be applied to one sink only: every consumer compares SPELLINGS, so it is bind-time-or-nothing (which is why 416c is at `bind_resource_scope`). And the verbatim record is not incidental, it IS item 251's N1: `test_n1_ledger_records_the_bound_host_not_a_hash` and `test_an_ordinary_host_still_renders_verbatim_in_the_wal` assert it deliberately. WHAT WAS ACTUALLY WRONG: the promotion was INVISIBLE and unbounded. A call's arguments reach the ticket only as `argsDigest`, EXCEPT at a parameter whose NAME happens to sit in `cap_order._REGISTRY` (`path`/`host`/`table`), whose runtime VALUE is lifted out, bound into the capability spelling, and on a yes written verbatim to a durable cross-session plaintext log. Nothing told the author that naming a parameter `path` changes where its values end up, nothing told the operator that a yes persists this one, and nothing bounded it. FIX, two parts. (1) The ticket stops understating what a yes means (the item-425-F1 `unreviewedHostCode` move): `bind_resource_scope` now reports whether a bound dimension came from a CALLER ARGUMENT or from an author-written LITERAL, and a caller-valued crossing carries `resourceScopesFromCallerArgs` plus `resourceScopeDurability`, which names the durable log, the `argsDigest` asymmetry, and both escapes (item 274). (2) The durability becomes an explicit OPERATOR decision, `revl mcp serve --approval-record-values {bound,withheld}` (`Session.approval_record_values`, carried across `fork`): `withheld` records a caller-supplied valuation as `None`, the resource-bearing-but-UNRECORDED shape `distill._project_resource` already fails closed on, and drops its bound spelling back to the bare token in `classCCapabilities` too, since the distiller reads an inline-parameterised entry in PREFERENCE to the `resourceScopes` map so withholding one channel alone would leave the value in the other. Verified: canary absent from the WAL file on disk under `withheld`, present under `bound`. THE COST, stated: under `withheld` a series of approvals whose target came from a caller argument no longer folds into a distilled standing rule.
