Author-written LITERAL targets are recorded under both modes and distill unchanged, so a composition that names its destinations in source pays nothing. DEFAULT WAS `bound` (what shipped), deferred to item 251's owner. **SETTLED 2026-09-02 on `fix/427-clock-and-record-default`: the default is now `withheld`.** Reasoning, because the flip is a real trade and not a tidy-up. The two mistakes are not symmetric. Recording is IRREVERSIBLE and SILENT — the value lands in a plaintext cross-session file on disk, it belongs to the caller rather than to the operator who said yes, and which values land there is decided by whether a parameter happens to be NAMED `path`/`host`/`table`, which is not a property anyone chose. Withholding is neither: its entire cost is that approvals whose target came from a caller argument no longer fold into a distilled rule, and re-serving with `--approval-record-values bound` gets that back. Everywhere else in this machinery the unset default is the fail-closed one; this applies that rule here. The blocking objection was that flipping would SILENTLY disable N1, and that half is fixed rather than accepted: `distill.Reason.RESOURCE_SCOPE_UNRECORDED`'s refusal now names this flag, so the operator who loses a fold is told why and what to change. The ticket's `resourceScopeDurability` sentence also became per-mode (`ClassMap.build_ticket(record_values=...)`) — a disclosure describing the other session's behaviour is worse than none, since it trains an operator to skip the field. Blast radius of the flip, measured: exactly three existing assertions changed, all of them the "records the caller value" ones, and each now opts into `bound` explicitly; nothing in the auto-approve matcher, the ticket's live decision surface or the author-literal path moved, because `_distillation_ledger_fields` only governs the LEDGER copy. THIRD OPTION WEIGHED AND REJECTED (negative result): recording a SCOPED DERIVATIVE rather than the raw value — a per-segment keyed hash preserving the equality and longest-common-prefix joins `_resource_join` needs — does not work here. The distilled artifact is a rule a human READS and applies (`AutoApproveRule.to_dsl()`), and a rule spelling `fs.write(path="/9f3a/ab12")` cannot be reviewed; the auto-approve matcher must re-derive the same tokens at every later crossing, so the key has to outlive the session and sit beside the log it is protecting; and path/host segments are low-entropy enough that a keyed hash over them is a dictionary attack, not a redaction. The fold and the plaintext are the same fact, which is why this is a posture and not a transform. Both behaviours are now pinned by tests, so the default is a choice rather than an accident. NEGATIVE RESULT on the `replay_forward` half: it is NOT a cross-principal disclosure. A snapshot carries SOURCES, not recorded calls, and `restore`/`resume` boot a fresh runtime with a fresh recorder (`persist.restore` -> `Session.load`), so every step a session can replay was recorded by a call that session's own driver made and the raw args it echoes are that driver's own. Pinned by `test_a_restored_session_replays_no_earlier_principals_arguments`. The one shape not constructible from the MCP surface is a REPL-origin step (`replay_forward` has a branch for it) reaching a Session timeline; if a path is ever added that imports recorded steps from another principal, that echo becomes a real leak and needs step provenance to fix. Evidence: `tests/test_416c_approval_and_model_leaks.py`, five new tests under the 425 F3 / 427 F5 heading; docs/harness-gate-guide.md "What a yes also writes down".
    **F6 MEDIUM, ✅ FIXED 2026-09-02** (`7f1c63ef` with F1, plus the follow-up `c0b6e9be`). `Session.load` opens the approval WAL for EVERY policy-enabled session, not only one whose composition carries a typed-approval edge, so consume-before-fire is a durable spend rather than a dict mutation. The follow-up was needed because opening the log for every policy session exposed a latent recorder defect: a timeline binds the WAL at activation, so a log opened after a component is live was never seen by it and was durable in name only. `Recorder.open_wal` now rebinds every live timeline, `WriteAheadLog` gained `is_open`, and a CLOSED log reads as absent instead of as a write target. Original finding: `session.py:647` is the ONLY site opening the approval WAL, gated on `_typed_approval_active`. `load` REFUSES a policy session without recording, saying "without a WAL there is no durable approval spend and no audit join", and then never opens one for a composition lacking typed-approval edges. Executed: after three approvals and two fires, `recorder.wal` is None and zero granted/consumed records were written. So `_consume_approval` and `_consume_grant` document consume-before-fire as crash-safe BECAUSE the spend is durable, and it is a dict mutation. It is also what makes F1 invisible after the fact.
    **F7 MEDIUM, ✅ FIXED 2026-09-02** (`9f0cc641`, `fix/ticket-overmint-and-call-approval`). `approve_ticket` was not idempotent: one ticket hash minted unlimited single-use approvals. Fixed as an idempotent no-op returning the existing entry.
    **F8 LOW, ✅ RESOLVED 2026-09-02 on `fix/427-clock-and-record-default`.** The session clock is now MONOTONIC-ANCHORED and RATCHETED, and every approval expiry site goes through one dead latch. Anchored: `Session._now_ms` samples the wall clock ONCE and derives every later reading from elapsed `time.monotonic_ns()`, so a settimeofday, an NTP step, a DST/timezone change or a restored VM snapshot cannot move the ms an `expiresAt` was computed against relative to the ms it is checked against. Ratcheted: the reading is clamped to a high-water floor, so it never decreases — including through the injectable test clock, which stays injectable but can no longer wind time back (no existing test ever wound it back; they only ever advance). Latched: `Session._expired(record, now)` records expiry as an EVENT on the record (`expiredAt`) the first time it is observed instead of re-deriving `now > exp` per crossing, and short-circuits on the latch thereafter, so an already-dead grant cannot be handed back by any later clock reading. It replaces the bare comparison at all five sites: the ledger entry lookup, the standing-grant lookup, the distilled-rule liveness check, and both deadlines a cache entry carries (its own ttl and its covering approval's, latched under separate keys). Both halves are load-bearing: the anchor makes the reading correct, the latch makes death irreversible, and neither alone closes it. The standalone runtime default (`backends/python/runtime.py` `_default_now_ms`, and the ledger scan in `SessionOwner.find_approval`) got the same treatment, so a raw run is not the weaker path. The latch is in-memory by design and needs no more: grants, ledger entries and distilled rules are session-scoped (invariant 5) and none survives a restart, so there is no expired record for a later process to inherit. SCOPE, stated because this is a guarantee this project states language-wide and the fix is Python-wide: the ledger, the standing grants, the distilled rules and the lease book this latch protects are the Python MCP session's own machinery (items 245/246), and the sibling tiers carry none of it — the go tier, the java tier, the rust tier, the typescript tier and the wasm tier are code GENERATORS with no runtime ledger, no expiry site and no session clock to anchor, so there is nothing on them to bring to parity. That is a measured scope call and not an assumption: `expiresAt`, `expiredAt` and `_expired(` appear zero times in the go, java, rust, typescript and wasm emitter directories and zero times under `selfhost/`, so no approval-expiry site exists on those tiers for a latch to replace. (They do appear elsewhere in the Python tree — `src/revl/liveness_confirm.py`, `src/revl/lower.py`, `src/revl/policy.py`, `src/revl/run.py`, `src/revl/why_runtime.py` — and that is the same Python runtime this fix already covers, not a sibling tier.) Evidence: four tests under the 427 F8 heading in `tests/test_standing_approval.py` covering the latch end to end, the ratchet with no expiry ever observed, the latch mechanism asserted directly, and the default non-injected clock. Original finding: `Session._now_ms` (`src/revl/mcp/session.py:2761-2768`) is `int(time.time() * 1000)` with an injectable override and no monotonic anchor, and every expiry site is still a bare `if exp is not None and now > exp` with no dead-latch (`:2499`, `:2516`, `:2755`, `:3209`, `:3354`). A rewound clock still resurrects a grant for its remaining uses. Original finding: Expiry is a bare wall-clock comparison with no monotonic anchor and no dead-latch (`session.py:2988`, `:2378`, `:3133`). Executed: forward TTL holds, then a rewound clock resurrects the grant for its remaining uses. One-line latch fixes it; the invariant-3 claim is stated as absolute.
    **HYPOTHESIS (unexercised).** `_find_standing_grant` dedupes by `id(g)` and spends one use per distinct grant OBJECT, not per class-(c) capability covered, so a grant covering two capabilities on one call spends one use for two crossings.
    **NEGATIVE RESULTS, attacked and HELD.** The `argsDigest` is inside the ticket hash, so mutating args between issue and consume yields a fresh ticket and a refusal; a ticket for capability A does not satisfy a crossing needing B; per-entry single-use holds; a ticket issued in generation N is refused after a swap (`_tickets` is cleared); replay after abort is unreachable; `uses` exhaustion holds exactly; forward TTL holds; unbounded mints are refused; minting via `effect lease` off-policy is refused; cross-session replay is blocked by the session field. THE FOLD ITSELF: a shape battery (direct emit, emit through a required service, emit through a spawn handle) all classified correctly, and the routed multi-realm seam is closed by G4 because the router's forwarding call must itself be `emit`-marked. Fail-open paths: a malformed cap spelling fails closed everywhere, `restore` rebuilds the class map, `Gate` with a policy and no approver refuses class (c) outright.

428. (private security advisories, see SECURITY.md) **SUPPLY-CHAIN AND ATTESTATION AUDIT (2026-09-02).** Thirteen findings, four HIGH. The two structural ones: the single guard keeping HMAC honest across a trust domain is bypassed by relabelling a field the verifier never reads, and `truc.lock` does not require a pin at all.
    **F1 HIGH, ✅ FIXED 2026-09-02** (`6f5d61ee`, `fix/attestation-truthfulness`). `verify_attestation` validates the ENVELOPE after the MAC (kind, version, verdict, `sign_alg`, `hash_alg`, and the shape of `composition_hash`, `key_id`, `timestamp`, `guarantees`, `checker`) and refuses any `sign_alg` but `attest.SIGN_ALG`, and `deploy.admit`'s cross-domain refusal reads the algorithm off THIS build rather than off the record's own claim about itself (`src/revl/deploy.py:128-133`). The envelope version went to 2.0 because the body changed shape, the MAC changed domain and the guarantee list changed meaning, so a v1 record is refused with a reason naming the version. Original finding: `deploy.py:337` gates cross-domain on `attestation["sign_alg"] == attest.SIGN_ALG`, but `verify_attestation` NEVER checks `sign_alg` and `_sign` is unconditionally HMAC whatever the field says. `sign_alg` is also absent from `SELF_DECLARED_IGNORED`, so the one self-declared member admission reads is the one deciding the trust-domain question. Executed: honest `hmac-sha256` REFUSED; relabelled `ed25519` verified True and ACCEPTED with a receipt; `''`, `'HMAC-SHA256'` and `None` also ACCEPT, so the downgrade needs no forgery skill.
    **F2 HIGH, ✅ FIXED 2026-09-02** (`6f5d61ee`, same branch). THE `G1..G9` CLAIM WAS A CONSTANT AND IS NOW A MEASUREMENT: `attest.run_gate` runs the reference frontend (compile plus the admission gate) and returns a `GateVerdict`; `make_attestation` REQUIRES one, refuses a verdict the gate did not admit, and refuses a verdict whose composition hash is not the hash of the IR being signed, so a real verdict for another composition cannot be laundered onto this one. `bundle`, `registry` and the CLI already held a real compile at the call site and threw it away; they keep it now, so there is no second compile. The recorded codes are `attest.discharged_guarantees()` intersected with the catalogue vocabulary, the signed body carries `checker = {compiler, ruleset}` (ruleset is a sha256 over the frontend ruleset module bytes), and `TrustStore.recheck_source` lets the receiver re-run its own frontend at admission. The docstrings state plainly what the lexical scan does and does not prove. Original finding: `attest.py:206-219` `make_attestation` checks exactly two things before signing all nine codes: the key is non-empty and `ir["holes"]` is empty. It never compiles, never calls the checker, never reads a gate verdict; `attested_guarantees()` is a `sorted()` over the diagnostics catalogue. Executed: `make_attestation({})` signs all nine and verifies; an IR the compiler REFUSES by name for G2 (`provision conflict: key 'db' is provided by both A and B (G2)`) signs all nine, verifies, and `deploy.admit` ACCEPTS it. Auditor's verdict, which I accept: **as stated the claim is not true**, and this is prior to and separate from whether the G-rule theorems are tautologies (item 418). Even fully proved theorems would not make the field mean "this artifact was checked". Fixes in cost order: make the list a measurement (take the gate verdict as an argument and refuse to sign without one; `bundle.py` and `registry.build_evidence` both hold a real compile at the call site and discard it), bind the checker's identity into the signed body, re-run the gate at admission or bind a gate receipt, and validate the envelope at verify time.
    **F3 HIGH, ✅ FIXED on `fix/428-truc-bootstrap-and-pin`; the pin requirement had already landed in `43592006`.** ✅ `plan_drift` no longer skips a truc with no lock row or a blank `sourceHash`: a missing row and a blank hash are refusals with their own message, exactly like drift, and `truc add` (which re-verifies fetched bytes against the registry index row before vendoring) is what mints the pin, so a first add always pins. ✅ **The bootstrap half is now closed too.** truc's own stage-0 lock `src/revl/truc/truc.lock` was `lockVersion: 0`, a bare file list with no hashes, so every boot compiled those eight component paths unverified; three things about it were weaker than the project-side rule this same finding fixed. (1) The lock was OPTIONAL: an absent lock fell back to globbing `components/*.rvl`, so the launcher's own stated posture (a corrupt lock must never degrade to compiling whatever is on disk) did not hold for the easier tamper of removing the file. It is now mandatory and there is no glob fallback — the same rule `plan_drift` applies one level down, that a check which can be turned off by deleting its input is not a check. (2) The paths were UNCONTAINED: rows were resolved with `Path.resolve()`, which follows symlinks and accepts `..`, so stage 0 could compile bytes from outside the installed package. The docstring's own trust argument ("these files ship inside the installed revl package") was therefore an assumption rather than a check; each row is now walked segment by segment, refusing any symlink, any parent reference, any absolute path and any non-regular file, so the claim is enforced. (3) The rows carried NO PINS. The lock is now `lockVersion: 1` with a sha256 per source, verified before the compile; a version-0 lock (no hashes) and any unknown version are refused rather than honoured, as are a blank or malformed hash, a duplicate row, an unparseable lock and one naming no files. Every one of these is a refusal to boot with a message naming the regenerator, never a warning. **What this does and does not buy, stated honestly:** the trust root is still the installed package's own integrity, since anyone able to rewrite these sources can rewrite `registry.py` beside them — but that claim is now checked instead of assumed, nothing outside the package can be drawn into stage 0, and a component whose bytes moved without the lock moving with them refuses. Regenerate with `python -m revl.truc._relock` (new `src/revl/truc/_relock.py`); `tests/test_truc_bootstrap.py` gains a regenerate-or-red check plus twelve refusal tests (deleted lock, unnamed component ignored, hash drift, five unusable pins, path escape, symlink, three bad lock versions, three unusable locks, and the clean CLI exit). `docs/design/truc-architecture.md` §7 updated. Original finding: `truc/components/planner.rvl:163-164` `plan_drift` skips any vendored truc with no lock row or a blank `sourceHash`, and nothing requires that a truc named in `truc.toml` carry a pin; a substituted vendored component was admitted with the lock row removed. The gate was not at fault; the tamper check opted out.
    **F4 HIGH, ✅ FIXED on `fix/428-f4-ceiling-authority`.** `deploy.py:429-445` checked the capability ceiling against `policy.json`, a DERIVED projection, never against the `ir/ir.json` admission re-hashes. Three paths gave `wanted = set()` and passed any ceiling: policy.json absent (facet never bound), policy.json present but malformed (hashed as raw bytes, re-hashes OK, `json.loads` raises and is swallowed to `{}` — this variant carries a fully valid signature and matching binding), and `capabilities` key absent. **Reproduced end to end before the fix**, over a real `build_bundle` whose composition reaches `smtp`, against a receiver ceiling of `frozenset()`: the honest bundle REFUSED at `capability-ceiling`; four tampered variants (policy.json deleted / left as `{not json at all` / `capabilities` renamed to `caps` / the list emptied), each **signed over as staged** so nothing here is a forgery, all four ACCEPTED, every one of them with `verify_attestation` True, the staged IR re-hashing to the signed `composition_hash`, and the artifact re-hashing to its bound facet. The derived-file shape is the whole defect: a projection is writable by the party the ceiling constrains, so REGENERATING it over a narrowed list is as effective as tampering and leaves the chain fully honest. **Fixed** by measuring the surface, not reading the projection: new `deploy.capability_surface(ir)` runs the same `_policy_of(_audit_document(ir))` derivation `build_bundle` uses to WRITE policy.json, over `ir/ir.json` as staged. **Why the IR is the authority:** admission has already re-derived its `composition_hash` from the staged bytes and compared it against the SIGNED one at step (c), so an IR edited to reach fewer capabilities is a different composition and refuses at `source-or-ir-hash` before the ceiling is consulted; the deploying side cannot lower the measured surface without also possessing the receiver's trusted signing key, and even then it would be signing a composition that is not the one it ships. It is also the document every emitter emits from, so the surface it yields is the surface the artifact in hand can actually reach. **On (3), fail-closed:** the ceiling previously fail-OPENED on a missing or unparseable input in exactly item 428 F11's shape (the bare `except: policy = {}`); it now fail-CLOSES — an unmeasurable surface is a `capability-ceiling` refusal naming that it could not be measured, never an empty set. **Also:** `policy.json` is no longer decorative. When the chain binds the facet, a projection that does not agree with what the IR derives (or that will not parse) is refused at `policy`. That is a chain-honesty check and deliberately NOT what the ceiling reads, so it can never become a bypass. Five regression tests in `tests/test_deploy_118.py` pin it: the four tampered variants parameterized, a REGENERATED-and-freshly-signed narrowed policy.json that still does not move the ceiling, the disagreeing-projection refusal, and the fail-closed path. STILL OPEN, filed here rather than fixed: the `conformance/<backend>` facet has the same unbound-means-unchecked shape.
    **F5 MED-HIGH, ✅ FIXED 2026-09-02** (`43592006`, with F3). `Registry.from_dir` no longer believes `index.json`: every row is cross-checked against the entry's own `component.rvl` by RECOMPILING it, the recompiled facts are what the entry carries, and an entry whose row disagrees with its source is refused by `resolve` and listed under a new `refused` key with the reason. Separately, a positive evidence grade now counts only when a cryptographically valid attestation binds the dossier's hash (item 290), or for the publisher facet when the caller's own trust set names it; anything else ranks LEVEL WITH publishing nothing and is marked "(self-reported, unverified)" in the summary, so fabricating a bundle no longer improves rank. `revl_resolve` keeps `verifyRequired` off by default but now passes a configured signing key whenever one exists. Original finding: `registry.py:690-695` `from_dir` reads authority claims off `index.json` and returns `component.rvl` from disk cross-checking NEITHER; `resolve` ranks least-authority on those claims. With `key=None` (the MCP default, `verifyRequired=False`) evidence is graded from publisher-written files and `"present"` outranks `"unavailable"`, so a FAKE attestation improves rank. Executed: a `*`-capability component ranked FIRST with a `why` string asserting a fabricated 9999/9999 fault sweep and trusted publisher. `registry.verify()` catches it but is a CI job neither `from_dir` nor `resolve` calls.
    **F6 MED-HIGH, ✅ FIXED 2026-09-02** (`43592006`, with F3). The attestation tier reads the bundle path `<entry>/evidence/attestation.json` that `build_evidence` actually writes (the root path is kept for pre-293 entries) and also checks the signed dossier bindings, so the tier is live instead of structurally dead. Connecting it surfaced a second bug fixed with it: `reproduce._normalized_ir` normalized only `manifest.components[].file` while `registry._normalize_ir_for_attest` also normalizes `components[].source`, so a reproduce could never have matched a signed attestation; reproduce calls that function now instead of re-implementing it. An unverifiable tier is no longer a silent pass: a rebuild with no mismatch but with tiers that checked nothing reports "partially reproduced", `--strict` exits non-zero, and the JSON carries `fullyVerified` plus the unchecked tiers. The registry-as-adversary half is answered by a new "independent pin" tier reading the project `truc.lock`, which is the one value the registry cannot mint. Original finding: `truc/reproduce.py:361` reads `<entry>/attestation.json` while `registry.build_evidence` writes `<entry>/evidence/attestation.json`, so the tier is structurally DEAD for every published entry, and since `ok = not mismatches` a dead tier is a PASS (`tests/test_truc_reproduce.py:79` pins the wrong behaviour as correct). Separately, `truc reproduce` does NOT detect a substituted dependency when the registry is the adversary: every tier compares the registry against ITSELF, so regenerating the index over substituted source reproduces fully GREEN. The project `truc.lock` `sourceHash` is the only independent anchor, and per F3 it is optional.
    **F7 MED, ✅ FIXED 2026-09-02 on `fix/428-attestation-tail` (`60b1371d`, merged `d126bc34`): freshness is bounded at BOTH ends on the receiver's own clock, so a post-dated attestation is refused.** The paragraph that follows is the original finding, re-verified on `2d2fa07f` BEFORE that merge landed, and item 428's F5-F13 set is written twice because merge `d126bc34` kept both sides of the hunk. `src/revl/deploy.py:641-654` still computes `age = current - signed_at` and refuses only `age > evidence_ttl_seconds`, so a post-dated timestamp yields a NEGATIVE age and is accepted. No future bound, no nonce, no monotonic counter. The 6f5d61ee envelope validation checks the SHAPE of `timestamp`, not its plausibility. Original finding: Freshness is anchored on a signer-chosen field with no future bound (`deploy.py:447-461`): a 2019 timestamp is refused, a 2099 timestamp is ACCEPTED because age is negative. `attest._now_iso` passes an ISO string straight through, so post-dating is a one-argument change, and the receiver has no independent anchor (no nonce, no monotonic counter).
    **F8 MED, ✅ FIXED 2026-09-02 on `fix/428-attestation-tail` (`f6c5f2d0`, merged `d126bc34`): `admit` reads the gauntlet VERDICT and binds the dossier to its composition.** The paragraph that follows is the original finding, re-verified on `2d2fa07f` BEFORE that merge landed, and item 428's F5-F13 set is written twice because merge `d126bc34` kept both sides of the hunk. `FACET_GAUNTLET` is hashed and re-hashed from the staged file (`src/revl/deploy.py:186`, `:557`) and the string `verdict` appears nowhere in `admit`'s gauntlet handling; the only verdict reads in the module are the receipt's own (`:697-704`). A dossier whose verdict is `rejected`, or one produced for a DIFFERENT artifact, still hashes correctly and rides along. Original finding: The gauntlet facet is hashed but its VERDICT is never read (`deploy.py:401-415`): a dossier whose verdict is `rejected` ACCEPTS. The dossier carries no composition identity, so a gauntlet record produced for a DIFFERENT artifact hashes correctly and rides along under an honest signature. `bundle.verify` reads the verdict; `deploy.admit` does not.
    **F9 MED, ✅ FIXED 2026-09-02** (`6f5d61ee`, with F1/F2). `_sign` carries a domain-separation prefix distinct from the deploy receipt MAC, `verify_attestation` checks `kind`, `version` and `verdict`, and `verify_receipt` does the same (`src/revl/deploy.py:697-698`). Neither record verifies as the other, and a guarantee code outside the catalogue is non-verifiable, so the `G10-does-not-exist`/`SOC2`/`FIPS-140-3` record no longer prints VALID. Original finding: Cross-protocol confusion: attestation and receipt MACs are byte-identical constructions with NO domain-separation tag, and `verify_attestation` checks neither `kind` nor `verdict`. Executed: a deploy ACCEPT receipt verifies as a valid attestation and vice versa; `revl attest --verify` prints VALID for a record whose verdict reads `REJECTED-by-the-gauntlet` and whose guarantees include `G10-does-not-exist`, `SOC2`, `FIPS-140-3`.
    **F10 MED, ✅ FIXED 2026-09-02 on `fix/428-attestation-tail` (`a626cbf0`, merged `d126bc34`): an uncanonicalizable record refuses with `(ok, reason)` instead of raising.** The paragraph that follows is the original finding, re-verified on `2d2fa07f` BEFORE that merge landed, and item 428's F5-F13 set is written twice because merge `d126bc34` kept both sides of the hunk. A well-formed v2 envelope carrying a lone surrogate in `signer` still raises `UnicodeEncodeError: 'utf-8' codec can't encode character '\ud800'` out of `verify_attestation`, whose contract is `(ok, reason)` and never raise; the envelope validation 6f5d61ee added runs AFTER the MAC computation that crashes. `deploy._envelope_bytes` (`src/revl/deploy.py:780-786`) has the identical unguarded `.encode("utf-8")` and is reachable from `CorrelationGuard.admit` on any peer-supplied envelope. Original finding: A lone surrogate in `signer` makes `admit` CRASH (`UnicodeEncodeError`) rather than refuse; `verify_attestation`'s contract is `(ok, reason)`, never raise. `_envelope_bytes` (`deploy.py:580`) has the identical shape and is reachable from `CorrelationGuard.admit` on any peer-supplied envelope.
    **F11 LOW/MED, ✅ FIXED 2026-09-02 on `fix/428-attestation-tail` (`ba7e71e5`, merged `d126bc34`): `artifact_digest` refuses a symlink anywhere in the tree and refuses an empty tree instead of digesting nothing.** The paragraph that follows is the original finding, re-verified on `2d2fa07f` BEFORE that merge landed, and item 428's F5-F13 set is written twice because merge `d126bc34` kept both sides of the hunk. `artifact_digest` (`src/revl/deploy.py:145-166`) still folds `sorted(p for p in root.rglob("*") if p.is_file())`, and `Path.is_file()` follows symlinks; an empty `emitted/<backend>/` still folds nothing and digests to the well-known empty hash. Original finding: `artifact_digest` (`deploy.py:135-156`): `p.is_file()` FOLLOWS SYMLINKS so bound bytes can live outside the bundle and change afterwards; an EMPTY `emitted/<backend>/` digests to the well-known empty hash and is a bound artifact that matches; and admit "loads nothing" with nothing re-pinning at load, so the ACCEPT receipt describes bytes that may already be gone. The length-framing itself is sound and resisted reordering and path-boundary collisions.
    **F12 LOW, ✅ FIXED on `fix/428-truc-bootstrap-and-pin`; the silent-pass half had already landed in `43592006`.** ✅ A `@version` that was asked for and cannot be checked is a MISMATCH, so the run reports "not reproduced" and `--strict` exits non-zero instead of `ok=True`. ✅ **`@version` is now a real pin.** It was not one before because nothing recorded a per-component version, which is the registry format change the earlier pass filed as out of scope. A registry entry now declares its release in a one-line `<entry>/version` file; `registry._entry_index_row` records it (INDEX_VERSION bumped to "1"), `_host.commit_add` projects it verbatim into the consumer's `truc.lock` row, and `truc reproduce` gained a live `version` tier: a requested version equal to the recorded one is OK, a requested version the registry does not carry REFUSES the resolution rather than rebuilding another release under the name that was asked for, and a registry recording nothing at all keeps the MISMATCH above. Asking for NO version is now `cannot verify` rather than absent, so an unpinned run is at best "partially reproduced": it reproduced whatever the registry serves right now, which is a real claim but a much weaker one than reproducing a named release, and the two must not print the same. Both directions fail closed: a `version` file present but unusable refuses to publish (a version a consumer can never pin against is worse than none), and an entry declaring nothing stays honestly unversioned with nothing invented for it. **The registry-as-adversary half:** a recorded version is publisher metadata and every tier that reads it compares the registry with itself, so the `independent pin` tier now also compares the version the project locked against the version the registry publishes — a release relabelled under a pinned name diverges there even when the bytes never moved. The five components in `registry/` carry `version` files and the index is regenerated. Seven tests in `tests/test_truc_reproduce.py` (the two that pinned the old behaviour are rewritten); `docs/registry.md` §1.1, `docs/truc.md` and `docs/commands-reference.md` updated. Original finding: `@version` is silently not a pin: `truc reproduce name@99.99.99-totally-different` reproduces whatever `name` is today with `ok=True`.
    **F13 LOW, ✅ FIXED 2026-09-02 on `fix/428-attestation-tail` (`aa225bc6`, merged `d126bc34`): a pinned contract naming a provider outside `plans` is REFUSED, not skipped.** The paragraph that follows is the original finding, re-verified on `2d2fa07f` BEFORE that merge landed, and item 428's F5-F13 set is written twice because merge `d126bc34` kept both sides of the hunk. `federation_admission` (`src/revl/deploy.py:1305-1308`) still does `provider_ir = plans.get(provider_id)` then `if provider_ir is None: continue`, so a pinned contract naming a provider outside `plans` is SKIPPED, in a function whose stated posture is refuse-as-one-unit. Original finding: `federation_admission` skips a pinned contract naming a provider outside `plans` instead of refusing, in a function whose stated posture is refuse-as-one-unit.
    **NEGATIVE RESULTS.** No reachable second preimage in `_canonical_bytes` on the deploy path (the conflations that exist need non-string keys or tuples, unreachable from `ir/ir.json`); NFC/NFD differ; `1.0` vs `1` differ; omitted vs present-and-null differ; injected, dropped or reordered fields all break the MAC; no field is outside the signed body except the signature; HMAC has no length-extension exposure; `hmac.compare_digest` throughout. The re-hash-on-receive DISCIPLINE is sound (`SELF_DECLARED_IGNORED` members really are never read, `composition_hash` and the artifact facet are genuinely recomputed from the receiver's disk, a bound-but-missing facet fails closed). The capability ceiling is a raw string set-difference that fails CLOSED on a spelling mismatch, so no parameter-drop or cone-widening bypass exists; the defect is the fail-open when the projection is missing. `plan_add_report` re-verifies at add time; `plan_drift` refuses drift when a pin EXISTS; `binding_mismatch` treats bound-but-absent as tamper; `verify_required=True` with a key really does filter a tampered attestation.

429. (issue #125) **THE SELF-HOST ORACLES CANNOT CATCH WHAT THE CORPUS DOES NOT EXERCISE, AND THIS RECURRED THREE TIMES IN ONE DAY (2026-09-02).** Item 391 states this in the abstract ("a feature the corpus does not exercise leaves the oracle green while the self-host lacks it") and item 417 records the largest instance (the missing type layer). This item records that it is not a one-off but the dominant failure mode of the byte-agreement gate, with three same-day instances, two of which ran in the OPPOSITE direction from the one 391 anticipates.
    (a) **✅ BOTH DEFECTS FIXED; RECORDED HERE AS A STRATEGY OBSERVATION, NOT AN OPEN FINDING.** **The self-host was RIGHT and the reference was WRONG (twice).** Fixing the callee-subtree defect (`emission_analysis._calls_in` pruning the entire callee subtree, roadmap 421 F1 territory), the implementer found `selfhost/checker.rvl::call_calls` already had the CORRECT shape: `calls_in(tg, acc)` on the target, then `push(cn)` for the name. The reference was the divergent one. Independently, fixing the Unicode-identifier defect, the implementer found `selfhost/lexer.rvl` already REFUSED all three exploits (`ｓend`, `ﬁlter`, `x²`), because `Str.is_alpha`/`is_alnum` are ASCII-only by spec while the reference used Python's full-Unicode `str.isalpha()`. In both cases the oracle was green because both sides agreed on the corpus, not because both sides were right. **RE-CHECK 2026-09-02:** nothing to fix under this letter; it is evidence for the item's thesis, and its exit lives in the numbered Exit list below.
    (b) **✅ PORTED (this is the item-421-F3 port; see 429(c) for the sibling that was NOT closed by it).** **A port the oracle did not demand, and would never have demanded.** The non-injective keyword mangling fix (`lambda` and a distinct `lambda_` collapsing to one emitted name) found the SAME defect in five self-host emitters (`emit_py`, `emit_ts`, `emit_go`, `emit_java`, `emit_rust`). The oracles were green WITHOUT the port because the covered corpus contains no keyword-twin pairs. The implementer checked the sources directly rather than trusting the gate, ported the fix, and PROVED the port was load-bearing by adding an injectivity assertion to the pristine tree and watching it fail. A byte-agreement gate cannot ask for a fix to a case the corpus never spells. **RE-CHECK 2026-09-02:** the injective rule is present in all five self-host emitters, `selfhost/emit_rust.rvl:137-141` among them. Do not read this as closing 429(c), which is a different defect in the reserved-word SET.
    (c) **✅ FIXED 2026-09-05 (#125, exit (4)).** **The missing reserved-word SET, not the already-ported injectivity rule.** Direct inspection confirmed that `backends/rust/emit.py::_mangle` escapes `_RUST_RESERVED | _EMITTER_RESERVED`, while `selfhost/emit_rust.rvl::mangle` listed only Rust keywords. The reference is correct here: `ctx`/`config`/`root`/`plugin` belong to the emitter's raw scaffolding, so user names must move clear of them. Added `tests/fixtures/emit_rust_corpus/reserved_names.rvl` to the oracle FIRST and observed the byte-agreement failure (1300 reference bytes versus 1244 self-host bytes), alongside two failing focused assertions. The document exercises all four names and their underscore twins as record fields, plus function names, parameters, locals, calls, construction and field access; `config` is a revl keyword and is exercised as a record field rather than an illegal local. The port now mirrors both sets without changing the injective rule or raw scaffold tokens. The focused test walks every member of BOTH reference sets through four underscore depths, requires the specified one-rung shift and agreement, and preserves ordinary names. The whole Rust emit corpus and both coverage ratchets remain green; the line ledger did not move, illustrating why line coverage alone could not expose this data defect. This closes (c)/(4), not the remaining corpus-triage work in (3).
    (d) **◑ THE EMIT HALF LANDED, THE STAGE BELOW IT DID NOT.** **A FIFTH instance, same day, found while fixing item 421 F6 (2026-09-02).** The F6 fix marks secrets in the EMITTED program at both ends: `_revl_mark_secret(p)` on a provide-method parameter its service declares `Secret[T]`, and a result marker on a `Secret[T]`-returning extern. `selfhost/emit_py.rvl` can emit NEITHER: `method_lines` (:1067) receives only the method IR node and `provide_lines` never threads the services table, so it cannot see `params[i].secret`, and `_emit_externs` (:687) has no return-side marking. That is a REAL divergence for any `Secret[T]` composition: the self-host emitter produces a program that does not redact. It is invisible because `tests/fixtures/emit_py_corpus/` contains no `Secret[` at all, so the oracle is green and says nothing. This is category (ii) again, and the consequence is a SECURITY one rather than a cosmetic one, which is the argument for (3) below being a real exit condition and not a nicety. **RE-CHECK 2026-09-02:** the two paragraphs headed "(5), what landed" and "(5) found while doing it" below carry this letter's verdict: the self-host emit-side port landed, and the frontend stage below it followed on `fix/selfhost-frontend-secret-marking` — the DECLARATION marking is carried, so a fully native compile of a `Secret[T]` document now matches the reference byte for byte on both wired tiers. What stays open under this letter is not the marking but the reference FLOW analysis, which the self-host still does not have and which this letter never asked for.
    **(5) sibling survey OVERTURNED, and the gate is what overturned it.** The survey above recorded that `secret` appeared 38 times in `backends/python/emit.py` and ZERO times in the ts/go/java/rust/wasm reference emitters, so there was no self-host divergence to find there. That is no longer true and the change was invisible until a gate looked: item 421 F6 crossed tiers while this branch was open, and `backends/typescript/emit.py` now mentions `secret` 17 times and `backends/go/emit.py` 20 times, marking `Secret[T]` at both ends. `selfhost/emit_ts.rvl` and `selfhost/emit_go.rvl` mention it ZERO times, and `tests/fixtures/emit_{ts,go}_corpus/` contain no `Secret[` at all, so BOTH oracles are green over a self-hosted emitter that does not redact — letter (d) recurring verbatim in two more tiers, with the same security consequence. Nobody noticed by reading; the construct ratchet failed on `secret=<true>` and `secret_return=<true>` appearing as new reference-only constructs, and the line ratchet failed on `backends/go/emit.py::_declares_secret` arriving with 16 statements no corpus document executes. This is the first live catch by the mechanism, on the exact defect class the item was opened for, and it was RECORDED rather than fixed: both ledgers carried it under a written reason naming it an open security divergence, and porting it is 421 F6 cross-tier work whose corpus family must go in first and be seen to fail, per exit (3). **AND IT WAS THEN PORTED, WHICH THE LEDGERS PROVE BY GOING STALE.** Re-measured on current main before landing (#141): go carries `tests/fixtures/emit_go_corpus/secrets.rvl` and the port of the marking (#216), ts carries its own corpus family (#209), so the go entry and ts's `secret_return=<true>` now fail the ratchet as STALE and are retired here. Only ts's `secret=<true>` remains recorded. A ledger that can only shrink on its own is the whole design, and this is it shrinking. Two smaller catches in the same pass: item 310's `cache pure` (`class=pure_fn`, and `_emit_memo_wrapper` with 17 unexecuted statements) is in the python reference, absent from `selfhost/emit_py.rvl`, and reached by no corpus document.
    **The gate's own false positives, found by running it against a moved tree and fixed rather than waived.** The first construct extractor counted any `.get("f")` inside a boolean context as a branch on a flag, which swept up the `x = node.get("cond") or {}` default-value idiom and put three phantom constructs in the go table; and it resolved a bound name to the nearest preceding discriminant assignment without noticing that `for index, (kind, value) in ...` rebinds `kind` to something else entirely, which invented `kind=arg` in the java table. Both are now handled — a flag counts only in a real test position (`if`/`while`/ternary/`assert`/comprehension guard, flattened through `and`/`or`/`not`), and a name rebound from a non-discriminant source is invalidated from that line on. The tables lost about 290 phantom entries across the six tiers, which is why `unported` fell from 570 to 290 with no porting. The ablation test caught the over-correction in the same pass: tightening to `if` statements alone dropped `secret=<true>`, which the python reference reads through a comprehension guard, and the 429(d) reproduction test went red until comprehension guards were admitted. A gate measuring the wrong thing is the failure this item is about; it does not get an exemption for being the gate.
    **What this changes about the strategy.** The differential oracles were adopted to prevent SILENT DIVERGENCE, and they do prevent it on the covered corpus. What they cannot do is (i) tell you which side is correct when both agree and both are wrong, (ii) demand a feature or a fix the corpus does not reach, or (iii) notice that two sides mirror DIFFERENT source-of-truth sets. Item 391's standing "does this need a self-host port?" step is necessary but not sufficient, because it fires on new FEATURES and two of these three were BUG FIXES to shared logic.
    **Exit:** (1) ✅ DONE (`fix/429-selfhost-corpus-coverage`) a standing rule that any fix to logic mirrored in `selfhost/*` is checked against the self-host source DIRECTLY, not via the oracle, and the check is recorded in the change; (2) when the reference and the self-host disagree, treat it as an open question about which is right rather than assuming the reference (it lost twice today); (3) ◑ MECHANIZED (`fix/429-selfhost-corpus-coverage`), not yet closed: the blind spot is now MEASURED IN LINES and ratcheted rather than merely asserted, so a gap has to be written down before it can exist; the 8633 uncovered reference statements and 2797 uncovered port statements are recorded, not closed; (4) ✅ DONE (#125, 2026-09-05) fix the `_EMITTER_RESERVED` divergence in (c), with a failing-first corpus case and a full reserved-set ladder assertion; (5) ✅ DONE (`fix/429e-selfhost-secret-redaction`) add a `Secret[` family to `emit_py_corpus/` and port the (d) markings, since a self-host emitter that silently does not redact is a security divergence, not a parity nicety. Relates to 391 (parity), 417 (the type-layer gap), 146 (full self-host).
    **(5), what landed.** Reproduced first on one input: the reference emitted `@_revl_secret_result` above `mint_token`, `_revl_mark_secret(token)` / `_revl_mark_secret(a, b)` at the head of the marked provide methods, and both names in the `from runtime import` line; `selfhost/emit_py.rvl` emitted none of the three. New corpus entry `tests/fixtures/emit_py_corpus/secrets.rvl` covers BOTH ends and the negative cases in one document (a `Secret[Str]`-returning extern beside a plain one, a one-secret method, a three-param method with two non-adjacent secrets, an unmarked method, and an empty-bodied marked method, which is the one branch where the mark REPLACES the `pass`). It was added FIRST and seen to FAIL (`1 failed, 22 passed`) before any emitter change, per exit (3): a corpus entry that was never seen to red proves nothing. The port threads the SERVICES table from `emit_py_src` down through `component_body` -> `emit_body_steps` -> `body_step` -> `provide_lines` -> `method_lines`, which is what the self-host structurally could not reach before (`method_lines` had only the method IR node); the receiver-side stamp is read BY INDEX off the service's declared params (`method_secret_params`) since a method's surface names may differ from the declared ones, and the return-side stamp comes off `externs[i].secret_return`. Both imports are gated on exactly the conditions that emit their call (`body_has_secret_param` walks `if` branches like `body_has_timer`; `secret_result` is gated from the extern list, outside any component, as the reference gates it), so every secret-free document stays byte-identical. **Ablation:** with the corpus entry kept and ONLY `selfhost/emit_py.rvl` reverted, `secrets.rvl` reds again and the other 22 stay green, so the port is load-bearing and not incidentally satisfied.
    **(5) found while doing it, NOW CLOSED for the DECLARATION marking (`fix/selfhost-frontend-secret-marking`): the self-host had NO TAINT STAGE AT ALL, one stage earlier.** Adding `secrets.rvl` to `emit_py_corpus/` also feeds `tests/test_selfhost_lower_ir.py` (which globs the whole directory) and `tests/test_selfhost_compile.py`, and the native LOWERING gate diverged too: it renders `"type": "Secret[Str]"` verbatim and emits neither the `params[i].secret` stamp nor `secret_return`. The cause is bigger than this item: `Untrusted`, `Trusted` and `Secret` appear ZERO times in `selfhost/lower.rvl` and ZERO times in `selfhost/checker.rvl`, while `src/revl/taint.py` strips all three qualifiers and leaves the stamps the backends read. Porting one qualifier's stamps alone would be a partial taint stage, so this is RECORDED rather than patched: the two affected projections carry a STRICT xfail naming the gap (`TAINT_QUALIFIED_GAP`), which is red for a named reason today and XPASSes loudly the day a taint stage lands, rather than the fixture being dropped from the corpus. The emit_py port above is unaffected, since every emitter consumes the reference IR, which carries the stamps; what the gap does mean is that a FULLY native compile (`selfhost/compile.rvl`, item 224) would hand the ported emitter an IR with no stamps and get no redaction, so the native pipeline is not secret-safe until the taint stage is ported. One more incidental: the self-host parser does not accept a scoped `extern emission[cap.op] fn` (`BAD|expected fn after extern` from `test_native_gate_admits_the_whole_emit_surface`), so the fixture declares its `Secret[Str]`-returning extern `pure`; `taint.py` sets `secret_return` off the declared return qualifier alone, so the marking is exercised either way. **CLOSED for the declaration-side marking.** `selfhost/lower.rvl` now carries the qualifier surgery (`taint_strip`, the recursive `taint_mentions_secret`, `taint_secret_witness`) and stamps the four IR positions a backend consumes: `params[i].secret` on an extern, a module fn and a service operation; `secret_return` and `secret_witness` on an extern; and a config field's `secret`. All three qualifiers are stripped, since leaving `Untrusted[T]` standing as a TYPE NAME is wrong the same way `Secret[T]` was. The discipline was followed in order: `tests/fixtures/emit_py_corpus/secrets_nested.rvl` (a nested `Result[Secret[Str], Str]` return, a `Secret[T]` module-fn parameter, an `Opt[Secret[Str]]` service parameter and a `Secret[T]` config field — the four positions `secrets.rvl` does not reach) was added FIRST and the `TAINT_QUALIFIED_GAP` xfail removed, giving 6 named failures across services / component headers / externs / functions before any `.rvl` change. `tests/test_selfhost_compile.py`'s strict xfail then XPASSED and is gone: a `Secret[T]` document now compiles FULLY natively to bytes identical to the reference compile on both wired tiers, so the native pipeline is no longer the hole this paragraph described. **NOT ported, deliberately:** the reference's FLOW analysis — the origin lattice, the `confidential` / `secret` origins, `Untrusted`/`Trusted` sinks and sources, `endorse` and the checked-parser declassifiers, and the G9 refusals. The self-host has a MARKING, not a taint analysis; nothing mints an origin and nothing refuses a program.
        **(1)+(3), the blind spot measured AGAIN, in LINES, because the construct count was a PROXY and it flattered us.** The construct survey above counts DISPATCH ARMS, and an arm the corpus reaches can have almost all of its body unexercised — a green signal that certifies less than it appears to, which is the very shape this item is about, one level up. So `tools/selfhost_line_coverage.py` measures statements. The reference is Python, so `coverage.py` gives that side directly; the self-host side is measured through the python module `selfhost/emit_<tier>.rvl` compiles to, which is how its own oracle runs it, with each uncovered statement attributed back to the `.rvl` FUNCTION whose name the emitted `def` keeps. **The reference emitters: 8633 of 16037 statements (53.8%) are never executed by the corpus their byte-agreement oracle uses** (re-taken on current main; the first measurement on this branch read 8421 of 15690, 53.7%) — py 56.4% covered, ts 65.9%, go 25.2%, java 55.7%, rust 49.8%, wasm 42.8%. **The ports: 2797 of 11217 emitted statements (24.9%) never run, and 59 of 569 self-host functions are NEVER ENTERED AT ALL** while the oracle asserts byte agreement over the file that declares them (py 12 of 100, including `cmatch`, `carrow`, `crecord_update` and `setup_step`). The construct table says 19% blind; statements say 53.8%. It is optimistic by nearly three times, in the direction that matters, and the reason is legible in the split: of the 8633 uncovered statements, 3899 sit in 263 functions no corpus document calls at all, 1740 sit on declared exclusions and named open gaps, and **2994 sit in 318 functions the corpus DOES call and leaves unrun — the mass a construct table structurally cannot see.** The gate now rests on the line ledger (`tests/fixtures/selfhost_uncovered_lines/`: since issue #1768 one file per half and tier, one record per function), keyed by (function, count of unexecuted statements) rather than line NUMBERS, which churn on every edit above them; it ratchets in BOTH directions, so a count that falls fails too and has to be recorded, which is what makes it monotone rather than merely bounded. The construct gate is kept as the cheap fast check and is explicitly no longer what anything rests on.
    **(1)+(3) priced: AUTHOR CASES, OR POINT THE ORACLE AT MORE INPUTS? Neither. TRIAGE.** The cheap hypothesis was that the curated corpus is simply too small, and it is testable. The agreement survey below was taken once, when the tree held 867 `.rvl` documents of which 619 compiled (it now holds 1023 of which 766 compile; the coverage numbers above are re-taken, this split is not, so read it as the shape rather than as today's count): of those 867, and running all six ports over them yields ~2950 (tier, document) pairs the reference emits, of which **1136 ALREADY AGREE byte-for-byte and the rest DIVERGE**. Adopting every agreeing document — a TENFOLD corpus, 1136 documents against today's 112, at zero authoring cost — moves reference coverage from 46.3% only to **51.0%**. The whole tree reaches 80.9% on a re-take, so the remaining ~30 points live ENTIRELY on the diverging pairs. The binding constraint is therefore not corpus size and not authoring: it is triage of those divergences, each of which is either a real self-host gap to port or a declared out-of-slice shape to record. Free adoption is worth taking and buys about five points; the rest is decided divergence by divergence, which is exactly this item's exit (2) and the standing rule, not a corpus-writing exercise. Per-tier the answer differs sharply — ts already agrees on 476 documents against a 31-document corpus, while rust agrees on 71 and go on 131 with coverage that barely moves — so whoever takes this should start with ts and java.
    **(2), the self-host LINE-provenance gap, and what closing it costs (NOT DONE, deliberately).** The self-host measurement above is per-FUNCTION, not per-line, and that is a hard limit rather than a shortcut: `src/revl/lower.py` drops the parser's `.line` for every IR node kind except `break`, `continue` and `hole`, so there is nothing to thread from `.rvl` source to emitted output. Closing it is three pieces, in order. (i) Carry the AST's `.line` into the IR — as a SIDE CHANNEL off `compile_files`, not as new keys on the IR dicts, because `tests/test_selfhost_lower_ir.py` compares the reference IR to `selfhost/lower.rvl`'s byte-for-byte and new keys would force a line-stamping port into a 5697-line self-host file before anything could be measured. (ii) Have `backends/python/emit.py` record, per emitted output line, the IR node that produced it. This does NOT have to be ported: the self-host module under measurement is built by the REFERENCE python backend, so only the reference needs the side table, and it can be threaded at the dozen dispatch entry points (`_fn_stmt`, `_expr`, `_body_step`, `_method_step`, ...) with a current-node stack rather than at each of ~2000 append sites. (iii) Map emitted line -> IR node -> `.rvl` line and re-key the self-host half of the ledger. Worth doing on its own merits — it makes every future self-host diagnostic locatable, which is a standing complaint — but it is a separate piece of work with its own risk to the IR goldens, and half-building it would have produced a provenance table nothing consumed. Recorded here rather than started.
    **Not affected: the `_infile_programs()` truncation bug.** That harvester in `tests/test_selfhost_lower.py` scans a plain string literal to the next `"` without honouring `\"`, so a harvested program containing an escaped quote is silently cut short and any coverage taken through it would understate itself in a way that looks like a real blind spot. Neither gate here goes through it: both read the tier's `CORPUS` list for FILENAMES and compile those `.rvl` files off disk. Checked at the source regardless — 50 programs are harvested there today and none is currently truncated, because every `\"` in that section sits inside a `\"\"\"` literal the harvester scans correctly. The bug is real and latent; it belongs to whoever owns `test_selfhost_lower.py`.
    
    **(1)+(3), what landed: the blind spot, MEASURED.** The item argued from five anecdotes; `tools/selfhost_coverage.py` turns the argument into a number, and `tests/test_selfhost_coverage.py` into a gate. It extracts a CONSTRUCT TABLE from both sides of each of the six mirrored emitter pairs — every discriminant the emitter branches on, as `field=value` (`kind=optfield`, `step=let-effect`, `op=??`, `method=join`, `name=Some`) plus the boolean IR flags a branch tests directly (`secret_return=<true>`) — reads the tier's corpus from the oracle's OWN `CORPUS` list rather than the fixture directory (a fixture the oracle does not run proves nothing), compiles it, and collects the pairs the corpus IR actually exhibits. **The measurement: of 363 constructs implemented on BOTH sides, 70 (19%) are never reached by any corpus document** — py 12/71, ts 15/71, go 5/44, java 27/72, rust 9/55, wasm 2/50 — and a further **290 are implemented by the reference alone with no corpus document that would notice**, which is the shape of the (d) gap exactly. The sharpest single finding is not the totals but a claim: `test_selfhost_emit_py.py`'s docstring lists `len`, `maplit`, `optfield`, `assert` and bare `expr` as COVERED, `src/revl/lower.py` emits all five, and no document in the 20-file py corpus — or the 31-file ts corpus — spells one. The oracle was never asked about them. (One entry is a genuine dead branch rather than a gap: the ts and java references dispatch on `op == "==="` / `"!=="`, which `src/revl/parser.py::_CANONICAL_OPS` rewrites to `==`/`!=` before lowering, so no IR can carry it.) Both populations RATCHET against `tests/fixtures/selfhost_blind_spots.json`: a blind construct needs a WRITTEN reason, a reference-only construct needs a deliberate baseline entry, and an entry that coverage has caught up with fails as STALE, so the ledger shrinks by itself and grows only when a human writes the sentence. **Ablation (the proof it is load-bearing):** remove `secrets.rvl` from the py corpus list and the gate names `secret=<true>` and `secret_return=<true>` as blind — it reproduces (d) from a standing tree, with no diff to inspect. The three options weighed and NOT taken: a generator deriving corpus cases from the construct table (a synthesized case both sides handle identically-wrongly still proves nothing, and it must invent surface syntax per construct); and a diff gate demanding a corpus case for any edit to a mirrored reference function (it needs history, fails open on a fresh clone, and — decisively — says nothing about logic nobody edits, which is how the `Secret[T]` gap survived for months and how (c) survived with no edit at all). The chosen gate is static, runs on any checkout, and measures the standing state. **What it CANNOT do, and does not claim:** it cannot see category (i) at all; it cannot see a defect in the DATA of a branch the corpus already reaches, which is what (b)'s keyword twins and (c)'s `_EMITTER_RESERVED` both are — (c) therefore remained open after that gate-only change; its corpus-first port is recorded above. A green run means "no NEW silence", never "the port is correct"; the standing rule in `docs/process.md` is the instrument for the rest.
    **(5) sibling survey (the (b)-shaped question: is any OTHER tier blind the same way?). ❌ FALSIFIED ON 2026-09-02 BY THE COVERAGE GATE — see the paragraph below it.** The survey's answer was No. `secret` appears 38 times in `backends/python/emit.py` and ZERO times in the ts/go/java/rust/wasm reference emitters, and zero times in `selfhost/emit_{ts,go,java,rust,wasm}.rvl`. Checked by running each tier's reference emitter and its self-host port over the same `Secret[Str]`-receiver composition: neither side of any non-py tier emits anything mentioning a secret, so there is no self-host DIVERGENCE there and those oracles are honest. What the survey does show is a different gap, one tier up and NOT of the 429 class: the F6 marking (and item 256's `_revl_secret` bound-secret injection with it) is PYTHON-TIER ONLY, so an emitted ts/go/java/rust/wasm program does not redact at all. That is reference-side coverage, not self-host parity, and it belongs to whoever takes 421 F6 cross-tier. (Incidentally observed and NOT touched: go and wasm already diverge from their references on this component shape, identically with the `Secret[...]` qualifier removed, so it is a pre-existing out-of-corpus shape unrelated to secrets.) **SURVEY REDONE ON SOURCE 2026-09-02 (#234), AND THE COUNT ABOVE IS NOW OUT OF DATE.** The cross-tier work the paragraph handed to "whoever takes 421 F6 cross-tier" happened: `secret` is no longer zero in the non-py reference emitters. ts carries `host.secretResult` / `host.markSecret` / `, secret: true`; go carries `revlSecretResult`, `revlMarkSecret`, a `_declares_secret` mode gate that reads a config field, and a `secret_witness` WAL referent; rust, java and wasm each carry the `secret_witness` WAL referent. Re-diffing every self-host emitter against its reference counterpart site by site, rather than trusting a green byte-agreement oracle, found ONE emitter with a reference site inside its own covered surface and no code for it: `selfhost/emit_py.rvl` consumed `secret_return` and a method's `params[i].secret` but had no config-field site at all, so `_revl_ConfigSchema([..])` went out without the `secret=[..]` argument the runtime redacts the `<name>.config` trace line from. Ported in #234, corpus first: `secrets_nested.rvl` was in neither the emit_py oracle corpus nor the fully-native compile corpus, was added to both, and was seen to red on exactly that argument before the port. Every other non-py site is out of its self-host emitter's covered surface rather than missing from it — `revlMarkSecret` and the `_SECRET_MODE` config scan sit in the live-component and placement worlds `selfhost/emit_go.rvl` lists as deferred, and every `secret_witness` site is `--record` WAL emission, which no self-host emitter emits at all. #234 also found and fixed a second, unrelated defect the same corpus addition reddened: `uses_builtin_result` in `selfhost/emit_py.rvl` mirrored only the `adt`/`match`/checked-division walk and neither of `_uses_builtin_result`'s two extern clauses, so a document whose only Result is an extern's declared return emitted a module that references `Ok`/`Err` and never defines them.
    **THE RATCHET'S FIRST LIVE CATCH ON A MERGE, AND WHAT IT COST TO CLOSE (2026-09-03).** Merging #234 into this branch moved two numbers in opposite directions, and both gates named them before CI did. `class=witnessed` became MIRRORED, because #234's new extern clauses in `uses_builtin_result` branch on it, while no corpus document reached it; and `uses_builtin_result` went 2 to 3 uncovered on the self-host side while `_uses_builtin_result` went 2 to 1 on the reference side. Closed with a CORPUS CASE, not a ledger entry: `tests/fixtures/emit_py_corpus/witnessed.rvl` declares a `witnessed[fs]` extern with its `undo`, which is the only construct that reaches the FIRST clause both sides test when they gate the built-in `Ok`/`Err` classes on the EXTERN TABLE rather than on a surface `match`/`adt`. It is byte-identical on both sides, so the oracle now asserts something there: the reference's `_uses_builtin_result` is at 0 uncovered, and the py ledger fell from 938 to 936 uncovered statements (reference) and 298 to 295 (self-host). The stale `class=witnessed` line was DELETED from the `unported` baseline rather than rewritten, which is the only direction that ledger moves.
    **What the corpus case deliberately did NOT close, TRIAGED rather than left untriaged.** The witnessed EFFECT STEP is unported, and the gap is real rather than cosmetic. Measured on `component Keeper { effect stash() }`: the reference emits the transactional registration (`_revl_wit1 = stash()`, then `if isinstance(_revl_wit1, Ok):`, then `yield _revl_frame.transactional(...)`), and `selfhost/emit_py.rvl` emits the bare call plus `yield lambda: <<UNSUPPORTED-CEXPR:>>`, a bracket-shaped nothing where a transaction belongs, and a bracket reverts the deliverable on a CLEAN unload. It is not closed here because it is a PORT and not a corpus case: `body_step` carries no externs table, so the witnessed lookup has to be threaded through five signatures alongside item 309's register kwargs, and a corpus document spelling it would only red the oracle. It stays named where the line gate already names it, `_ComponentEmitter._witnessed_step` (9 statements) and `_method_witnessed_step` (8), NEVER ENTERED on the reference side of `tests/fixtures/selfhost_uncovered_lines/`, and it is now triaged rather than untriaged: the verdict is PORT, and it belongs to item 391's parity catch-up.
    **AND THE COVERAGE GATE PAID FOR ITSELF ON THE WAY IN: the corpus case reddened two gates nobody was aiming at, both in the self-host FRONTEND.** Adding `witnessed.rvl` was supposed to close one construct. It also found that `selfhost/lower.rvl` cannot read the extern declaration surface past the shape `externs.rvl` happened to spell (two `pure` externs, no `undo`, no capability tag). Two defects, measured, not read off the source: (i) the admission gate FALSELY REFUSES a program the reference compiles, `BAD|expected fn after extern`, because the extern grammar knows `pure`/`acquire`/`emission` untagged and knows neither the `witnessed` class (item 243) nor a capability tag on ANY class, so `emission[net]` is refused too; and (ii) worse, `lower_to_ir` SILENTLY drops the whole `externs` section to `null` for any extern carrying an `undo` clause or a capability tag. Not a refusal: a document with a full extern table lowers to one with none, which reads downstream exactly like a document that declared no externs, and every extern the reference would have emitted disappears. Neither is reachable from the pre-429 corpus, which is the item's thesis arriving on schedule: the gate demanded a case for one blind construct, the case spelled a legal program, and the program found two frontend defects that had nothing to do with the construct. Both are recorded as ratchets rather than skips: `NATIVE_GATE_GAPS` in tests/test_selfhost_compile.py pins the exact refusal string, and `EXTERN_DECL_GAP` in tests/test_selfhost_lower_ir.py marks the document strict-xfail, so the day the grammar lands both go red on the stale entry instead of outliving it.

432. (issue #113) **WASM CODEGEN PERFORMANCE AUDIT: THE CANONICAL-ABI BOUNDARY COPIES EVERY STRING BYTE THROUGH AN INTERPRETED LOOP, AND 85% OF EVERY EMITTED MODULE IS UNREACHABLE (2026-09-02).** Audit of the code the wasm backend GENERATES, against what a competent wasm author writes for the same semantics. Harness committed at `bench/codegen/wasm/` (`run.py`, `probe_heap.py`, `harness.py`, `programs/`, `results.json`) and re-runnable standalone. **No timings were taken and none should be quoted from this item**: the audit machine was carrying about a dozen concurrent agents, so every number below is load-independent by construction. The executed measure is wasmtime **fuel**, an exact count of executed wasm operations found by bisecting `-W fuel=N`; the rest are module byte sizes, reachability, and static allocation and copy counts. Executed against wasmtime 47.0.3 and `wasm-tools` through the component model on macOS arm64; the emitter was NOT edited, each proposed fix was A/B'd as a hand-written rewrite of the emitted WAT, and `run.py` refuses to report a variant that does not return exactly what the baseline returns.
    (a) **`$__canon_lift_str` copies a `Str` byte by byte in a wasm loop, and the copy is not needed at all.** Every `Str` entering the module through the canonical boundary goes through a five-instruction-per-byte loop. Measured slope: **19 fuel per argument byte**. Swapping the loop for the `memory.copy` the same module already uses in `$str_concat`, `$str_slice` and `$list_push` gives **1 fuel/byte** (`echo` of a 4 KiB string: 77900 to 4174 fuel, 73726 saved per call). Better, the copy is removable: a canonical `string` param reaches a linear-memory callee in memory the callee itself handed out through `cabi_realloc`, so if `cabi_realloc` reserves 8 bytes of headroom and returns `p+8`, the incoming bytes already sit exactly where the internal `[u32 len][bytes]` layout wants them and lifting is one store of the length. Measured: **0 fuel/byte, 58 fuel flat at every string length from 16 B to 4 KiB**, and the component still round-trips `"hello"` correctly. Emitter line: `backends/wasm/canonical.py:680` (`base_helpers`). BLAST RADIUS: the four `golden/*.wat` byte-identical oracles; `canonical.py` is not mirrored in `selfhost/emit_wasm.rvl`, so no self-host port. The zero-copy form must be gated on the argument having come from this module's own `cabi_realloc`, which is the ABI's contract for an indirect param but should be asserted in the fix rather than assumed. **FIXED 2026-09-02** (`fix/432acf-wasm-canonical-copies`, 11ba908a, `backends/wasm/canonical.py`). `cabi_realloc` now reserves 8 bytes below every buffer it hands out and `$__canon_lift_str` is one store of the length. RE-MEASURED on the committed harness, baseline before vs baseline after: `echo` **19.000 to 0.000 fuel/byte**, flat **49 fuel** at 16 B, 256 B and 4 KiB alike (77900 to 49 at 4 KiB, 77851 saved per call); `greet` 21 to 2 fuel/byte; `concat6` 58 to 20. The `cabi_realloc` provenance is CHECKED, not assumed: the zero-copy path is gated on `$ptr` lying at or above this module's `$alloc` floor, and every other pointer, including the any-aligned-address a host may pass for a zero-length string, takes a bulk `memory.copy` instead, so the lift can never write below a buffer it was not given room under. That guard is 4 fuel flat per lift, which is why `echo` lands at 49 rather than the audit's unguarded 45. Cost: +20 B core.wasm on the string programs, the fallback keeping `$alloc_str` reachable; (b) is where module size gets fixed. Goldens regenerated: the three that carry the boundary (`canonical_echoer`, `canonical_aggregates`, `canonical_service` `.core.wat`); the `.wit` files did not move, the interface being unchanged. SELF-HOST: verified in the source, not through the oracle (item 429). `cabi_realloc`, `__canon_*` and the canonical return area occur ZERO times anywhere under `selfhost/`; `emit_wasm.rvl:1365` mirrors emit.py's `$__hp`/`$alloc_str`/`$str_concat` prelude, which is (b)'s and (d)'s blast radius, not this one's. No port.
    (b) **The linear-memory helper prelude is emitted unconditionally, so 27 to 30 of every 32 to 34 emitted functions are unreachable from any export.** `emit.py:2732` `_helper_funcs()` returns a fixed list of 16 memory helpers, and `emit.py:2750` appends 10 arithmetic and 3 list helpers, all of them whenever the module needs a memory at all. Only `$f64_to_str`, `$str_index_of`, `$str_split` and `$str_join` are gated on use (`emit.py:2129-2143`), and that gating is exactly the pattern the rest should follow. Measured core module sizes, baseline versus dead-code-pruned: `echo` **3483 to 503 B (85.6% smaller)**, `scalar` (an `Int` doubler, which still drags in `$str_concat`, `$str_to_int`, `$int_to_str`, the codepoint walkers and the list helpers) **3567 to 445 B (87.5%)**, `greet` 3527 to 645 B, `listid` 3723 to 555 B. With every fix in this item applied together: `echo` **3483 to 436 B**, `listid` 3723 to 516 B. Fuel is unchanged, as expected: this is pure size, and on this tier size is the deliverable. BLAST RADIUS: all four goldens, and `selfhost/emit_wasm.rvl:1365` embeds the same preamble VERBATIM, so this needs a matching self-host port under `tests/test_selfhost_emit_wasm.py` byte agreement. **FIXED 2026-09-03** (`fix/113-wasm-perf-remainder`, PR #259, `backends/wasm/emit.py` `prune_unreachable_funcs`, wired in `emit()` and `canonical.py:_canonical_module`). **The proposed shape was NOT the one that landed, and the difference matters twice.** (1) Per-helper token gating — "the pattern `$str_split` already follows" — cannot see that `$alloc_str` is wanted only because `$str_concat` is, so it needs a hand-maintained dependency table forever; what landed is a single reachability sweep of the finished module: keep every export, keep everything an export reaches through `(call $…)`, drop the rest. (2) The sweep must run on the ASSEMBLED module, not on the core: `$alloc_str` is reached by NO core export and IS called by the canonical lift wrapper, so a sweep inside `_helper_funcs` would have emitted a module that does not link. `emit(prune=False)` is what the canonical path asks for, and `_canonical_module` sweeps after splicing. Soundness is CHECKED, not assumed, in #133's posture: the sweep declines any module carrying `ref.func`, `call_indirect`, a top-level `(table`, `(elem` or `(start` — the constructs that put a call-graph edge where a `(call $…)` scan cannot see it — and that module keeps the whole prelude rather than losing a function something could still reach. This tier emits none of them today. RE-MEASURED on the committed harness, baseline before vs after, core.wasm bytes: `echo` **3698 to 719 B (-80.6%)**, `scalar` **3765 to 652 B (-82.7%)**, `listid` **3830 to 669 B (-82.5%)**, `constfold` 3675 to 612 B, `greet` 3742 to 914 B, `concat6` 3798 to 1193 B (the last two also carry (d)'s k-ary helper). Live/defined functions: `echo` 7/34 to 7/7, `listid` 6/36 to 6/6, `scalar` 6/35 to 6/6 — zero dead functions in every bench program. Fuel is unchanged at every point of every sweep (`echo` 67, `listid` 136 and 8296, `scalar` 11), which re-confirms the audit's negative result (5): this is purely size. A dropped function's own comment paragraph is dropped with it, so a swept module carries no prose about a helper it does not have. SELF-HOST: PORTED. `selfhost/emit_wasm.rvl` renders the function bodies BEFORE placing the preamble and sweeps the verbatim preamble block by the same rule (`prune_preamble`); byte agreement holds across the whole corpus, which is the oracle that proves the two implementations agree on the set AND the order.
    (c) **`List[Int]` crosses the boundary element by element, twice, with a scratch buffer that is never needed.** `$__canon_lift_list_Int` (`canonical.py:400`) walks the list with an `i64.load`/`i64.store` loop, although for `Int` the canonical element layout (8-byte `s64`) is byte-identical to the internal 8-byte slot, so one `memory.copy` is provably equivalent. `$__canon_lower_list_Int` (`canonical.py:425`) is worse: it allocates a scratch buffer and refills it element by element, when the internal list body at `r+8` **already is** exactly the canonical array and can be pointed at directly, with zero copies and zero allocations. Measured on an identity `fn ids(xs: List[Int]) -> List[Int]`: **46 fuel per element, down to 8** with bulk copy plus an aliasing lower (1024 elements: 47197 to 8269 fuel, 38928 saved per call), allocations on the call path 3 to 1, and reachable functions 4 to 2. BLAST RADIUS: `golden/canonical_aggregates.core.wat`, `golden/canonical_service.core.wat`. The layout-identity test must be a real check, not an `elem == "Int"` special case, or a later element type will silently get a wrong copy. **FIXED 2026-09-02** (same commit). RE-MEASURED baseline before vs after on `listid`: **46.000 to 8.000 fuel per element** (n=1024: 47197 to 8260, 38937 saved per call; n=4: 277 to 100), `$alloc` calls on the path **3 to 1**, `memory.copy` sites 0 to 1, core.wasm 3723 to 3635 B. The identity test is structural: an aggregate is rejected because it is internally a POINTER and canonically the DATA, and what remains must be an 8-byte 8-aligned canonical element whose generated per-element move is a bare `i64.store` of a bare `i64.load`. `List[Person]` keeps the loop in the regenerated golden, which is the check doing its job. CORRECTION to the measurement above: the `reachable functions 4 to 2` was an artifact of the variant rewrite, whose `_replace_func` re-indented the two helpers out of the reachability scanner's view; the emitted fix leaves both reachable and the fuel, allocation and size numbers are unaffected.
    (d) **A k-part template literal emits a k-1 deep pairwise `$str_concat` chain, so the byte cost is quadratic in the arity.** `emit.py:4696-4698` left-folds the interpolation pieces, and each step allocates a new buffer and recopies the whole accumulated prefix. A competent author sums the lengths once, allocates once, and does one `memory.copy` per part. Measured on `` `<<${a}|${b}|${a}|${b}>>` `` (9 parts): **58 fuel per argument byte, down to 42** with a k-ary concat alone, and to **6** with (a) as well (1024-byte arguments: 76377 to 20735 fuel). On a 3-part `greet` the fusion alone is worth little (21 to 20 fuel/byte), which is the point: the win is entirely in the arity, so fix this for the long templates, not for `` `Hello, ${x}` ``. BLAST RADIUS: `golden/canonical_echoer.core.wat`, `golden/canonical_service.core.wat`, `golden/functions.wat`, and `selfhost/emit_wasm.rvl:837` emits the same fold. **FIXED 2026-09-03** (`fix/113-wasm-perf-remainder`, PR #259, `backends/wasm/emit.py` `_interp_expr` + `_helper_str_concat_n`). A k-part template now emits ONE `(call $str_concat<k>)` for k >= 3: one length pass, one `$alloc_str`, one `memory.copy` per part, and the helper is demand-driven per arity the way `$str_split` is. k=2 already IS that shape, so it keeps calling `$str_concat` and every two-part golden is byte-unmoved. RE-MEASURED, baseline before vs after: `concat6` (the 9-part `` `<<${a}|${b}|${a}|${b}>>` ``) **20.000 to 4.000 fuel per argument byte**, 1024-byte arguments **37604 to 20797 fuel, 16807 saved per call**, 16-byte arguments 17444 to 16765 (679 saved); `greet` (3 parts) 2.000 to 1.000 fuel/byte, 4096-byte argument 24805 to 20647 (4158 saved). Executed `banner("ab", "cd")` returns `"<<ab|cd|ab|cd>>"` before and after. Size before the sweep in (b) lands: +374 B on `concat6` and +151 B on `greet`, the now-dead pairwise `$str_concat` still being emitted; with (b) both are smaller than they started. **NEGATIVE RESULT on this finding's own BLAST RADIUS.** `selfhost/emit_wasm.rvl:837` does NOT emit this fold: that line is the `.concat()` BUILTIN, a single pairwise call. The self-hosted wasm emitter implements no template interpolation at all (`interp` occurs nowhere in the file; the header lists it as out of subset), so (d) needed NO self-host port — verified in the source, not inferred from a green oracle.
    (e) **GROWTH ✅ FIXED 2026-09-02 (`fix/432e-wasm-heap-growth`); RECLAIM ✅ FIXED 2026-09-02 (`fix/432-wasm-reclaim`), see below.** `$alloc` now computes the end of the bump, refuses a round-to-8 or a bump that wraps i32, and calls a new `$__heap_grow` when the end crosses the current memory limit. `$__heap_grow` does its page arithmetic in pages (`end >>> 16` plus a ceiling bit, so nothing can overflow), grows by exactly the shortfall, and on `memory.grow` answering `-1` calls `$__heap_exhausted`. `cabi_realloc` is backed by the same `$alloc`, so the host's own placement of an incoming string grows the memory too and needed no change; `canonical.py` was not touched. **RE-BASELINED ON (a)/(c)/(f), because both audit numbers had moved and neither cliff had closed.** The audit's 40000-byte trap no longer reproduced: (a)'s zero-copy lift roughly doubled what one call can carry, so the single-call cliff was re-BISECTED rather than quoted, and sat at **65520 B ok, 65521 B refused**, with wasmtime's own canonical check (`realloc return: beyond end of memory`) in place of the pre-(a) `memory fault at wasm address 0x10000`. The sustained-call cliff moved the same way and no further: **64 calls with a 1 KiB string ok, 65 trapped** at `memory fault at wasm address 0x1020c` (the audit said 63; (a)/(c)/(f) shrank what a call allocates, they did not stop it accumulating). After this fix both are gone: 65521 B, 200000 B and 1000000 B arguments all round-trip, and 65536 calls with a 1 KiB string leave ONE instance alive with the heap at 67.6 MB. `bench/codegen/wasm/probe_heap.py` carries the re-bisected ladder. **What growth failure looks like.** There is no error channel out of `cabi_realloc` (the canonical ABI requires an allocator to return a usable pointer or not to return), so a capping host is answered with a deliberate trap at a NAMED site rather than an unchecked write. An operator sees `wasm trap: wasm 'unreachable' instruction executed` over a backtrace reading `__heap_exhausted` / `__heap_grow` / `alloc`, which says out of memory, instead of an address in a fault message, which says nothing. The two i32-wrap guards refuse at the same site, so a hostile `new_size` through `cabi_realloc` cannot buy a pointer with no bytes behind it. **RECLAIM ✅ FIXED 2026-09-02** (`fix/432-wasm-reclaim`, `backends/wasm/canonical.py`).
