Item **433**: `REVL_CROSS_TIER_SLOW` was set nowhere, so the rust and java halves of the cross-tier semantic floor executed nowhere, and riders R1 (`Int / Int` doing Java integer division) and R2 (`==` on Float going through `doubleToLongBits`, inverting NaN and `-0.0`) survived under `IEEE_FLOAT` assertions that had covered them since Float landed. Java has never executed that file. The skip counted as a pass.

    **THE AUDIT, verified rather than assumed.** Genuine environment gates in `tests/`, cross-referenced against `.github/workflows/`, `Makefile` and `ci/`. `REVL_CROSS_TIER_SLOW`: **17 `skipif` decorators across TWO files, not one**, 16 in `tests/test_cross_tier_execution.py` and a 17th in `tests/test_time_coeffect.py` (`test_rust_async_timer_lifecycle_runs_end_to_end`), which every prior count of this switch missed because it sits in an unrelated file; NEVER SET. `REVL_CONFORMANCE_TS`, `REVL_CONFORMANCE_PY`, `CORDIS_WASM`: never set, but all three are **OVERRIDES, not gates**: each falls back to an in-repo path and the real gate is a filesystem probe, so "never set" is the correct state for them and the audit's headline count of four dead switches is really ONE dead switch plus three overrides. `REVL_PY` and `REVL_REQUIRE_WASMTIME` are genuinely set, each in exactly ONE place, both in `.github/workflows/ci.yml` (the `conformance` and `backend-wasm` jobs respectively); `ci/placement_smoke.sh` only READS `REVL_PY` as a `${REVL_PY:-...}` fallback, which proves nothing about the variable being set, and the guard below deliberately does not count a read as a set for that reason. `REVL_ORDER_LOG`, `REVL_WIT_TARGET` and `REVL_SECRET_API_KEY` are set by the suite itself (`monkeypatch.setenv`, or handed to emitted code) and are not switches at all.

    **A THIRD GATE, FOUND WHILE MEASURING, AND IT IS NOT AN ENV VAR.** `RUNNERS["ts"]` requires `backends/typescript/node_modules/.bin/vitest`, and **no CI job that runs `tests/` executes `npm ci`**, neither `frontend`, not `frontend-cordis`. So the TypeScript tier of the cross-tier suite has skipped in CI exactly as hard as java did, while the file's header, `docs/contract-errata.md` and `docs/arithmetic.md` all described it as executing "by default". This is the same class with a filesystem probe in place of an environment variable, which is why item 433's sweep for dead env switches could not see it, and why the guard below states plainly that it does not catch this half.

    **A THIRD INSTANCE, AND THE VARIANT MOST LIKELY TO SURVIVE A REVIEW: RIGHT VARIABLE, WRONG JOB.** `REVL_CORDIS4J_CLASSES` IS set in `.github/workflows/ci.yml`, so every audit that asks "is this switch set anywhere in CI" answers yes and moves on. It is set in the `backend-java` job, which runs `backends/java/test_emit_java.py` and nothing else. The test that READS it is `tests/test_realm_conformance.py::test_cordis4j_realm_conformance`, and every job that collects `tests/` (`frontend`, `frontend-cordis`, and this item's new `conformance` step) leaves it unset. So that probe has skipped in CI as completely as the `REVL_CROSS_TIER_SLOW` ones, while looking fully provisioned. This is item 430's shape exactly, a third time. NOT CLOSED: closing it means cloning and compiling cordis4j-core in `conformance` the way `backend-java` already does, and that step could not be executed on the audit machine to prove it green first. It is enumerated instead, in the `conformance` job comment and in the guard's `_KNOWN_WRONG_JOB` registry, so it cannot go quiet.

    **MEASURED, on an arm64 laptop, `tests/test_cross_tier_execution.py` alone.** Default gates: **30s**, 93 pass, 94 skip. With `node_modules` present: **3m53**, 128 pass. Plus `REVL_CROSS_TIER_SLOW=1` so rust executes: **4m53**, 156 pass. Plus a working JDK so java executes: **6m31**, 180 pass, 5 skip. **87 tests newly execute** across those three gates. The file was NOT narrowed to make that fit: a smaller green is what created this item.

    **THE FULL MATRIX WAS RUN, AND IT PRODUCES EXACTLY ONE FAILURE.** `/opt/homebrew/opt/openjdk` (JDK 26) is installed on the audit machine but keg-only, so it is absent from `PATH` and `_java_tool`'s `shutil.which("javac")` finds only the macOS shim. **That is why item 433 states "no JDK on this machine" and took no java measurement at all; that premise was wrong, and putting the keg-only bin directory on `PATH` is the whole fix.** With it, the complete five-tier matrix runs, item 433's R1 and R2 fixes execute green on a real JVM for the first time, and the single failure is `test_probe_executes_identically_slow[java-structural equality]`: `_emit_v3_types` (`backends/java/emit.py`) emits a v3 record as a bare `public static final class` with no `equals`/`hashCode`, so `Objects.equals` on two equal records is Object identity and false. That is the gap item 433's rider section already recorded, landed via `fix/433-java-record-equality` (`c98b826c`: v3 records and variant cases got a structural `equals`/`hashCode`, primitives comparing with primitive `==` and references through `revlEq`, hashing through `revlHash`). That closes the one failure blocking this flip and nothing wider: the rest of item 433's java findings stay open under issue #70. **Nothing else fails.** The flip is therefore proven safe the moment that lands, which is a stronger statement than "it should work".

    **DONE.** (a) The `conformance` job, the one place node, rust, java, go and wasmtime all exist at once and the only job that runs `npm ci`, now runs `tests/test_cross_tier_execution.py`, `tests/test_realm_conformance.py` and `tests/test_time_coeffect.py` under the cordis-py venv. That alone closes the TypeScript hole and the `REVL_CONFORMANCE_TS` / `REVL_CONFORMANCE_PY` holes, and needs no java fix. (b) The `REVL_CROSS_TIER_SLOW=1` flip sits in that same step **commented out, with the failing probe named**, staged behind the java record fix. The probe is not deselected and the file is not narrowed. (c) The false prose is gone: `tests/test_total_field_surface.py` claimed "CI runs the full matrix" and `docs/contract-errata.md` claimed TypeScript executes by default; both now say what actually runs where. (d) **The systemic guard**, `tests/test_env_gated_skips_run_somewhere.py`: it AST-scans `tests/` for every environment name the suite READS but never SETS (resolving indirections like `_TARGET_ENV = "REVL_WIT_TARGET"`, and not counting a subprocess `env` dict as ownership), and fails unless each one is either assigned somewhere in `.github/workflows/`, `ci/` or the `Makefile`, or listed in an `_INTENTIONALLY_LOCAL` registry with a reason. Commented-out lines do not count as set. The registry may only shrink: a separate test fails if an entry stops being read or starts being set in CI, and a third rejects a reason under 80 characters or one that amounts to "it is slow", because slow is what a dedicated job is for. A fourth check, `test_a_ci_set_gate_is_set_in_a_job_that_actually_runs_it`, closes the right-variable-wrong-job variant: for every BARE read (no default, so nothing but CI can supply a value) it parses the pytest targets out of each workflow step that sets the variable and requires one of them to actually collect a file that reads it. Verified non-vacuous by clearing the registry and watching it name `REVL_CORDIS4J_CLASSES`.

    **THE GUARD IS ITSELF STAGED, AND ON PURPOSE.** Its main assertion is marked `xfail(strict=True)` today, since `REVL_CROSS_TIER_SLOW` really is still unset. The moment somebody uncomments the two lines in `ci.yml` the test PASSES, which under `strict` is a FAILURE, so whoever lands the java fix cannot avoid returning to delete the marker. Parking the name in `_INTENTIONALLY_LOCAL` with an excuse would have gone quiet instead, which is the precise failure mode this item exists to stop. **Verified by simulating the flip: uncommenting makes it red with `[XPASS(strict)]`, and reverting makes it xfail again.**

    **EXIT, for whoever lands `fix/433-java-record-equality`.** Uncomment the two `env:` lines in the `conformance` job, delete the `xfail` marker in `tests/test_env_gated_skips_run_somewhere.py`, confirm the full file is green on the runner, and tick this item. **Until then, and generally: a `*_slow` skip means UNMEASURED, never passing.** Relates to 430 (the same shape, one job away) and 433 (the two live defects it hid).

    **A FOURTH GATE, AND IT LEFT THE REGISTRY INSTEAD OF JOINING IT (`CORDIS_WASM`, PR #197).** `_INTENTIONALLY_LOCAL` carried `CORDIS_WASM` with the reason that the cordis-wasm substrate is a separate repository and a prototype, so pinning its revision would be "a decision, not an oversight". Widening `backend-wasm` from one named file to the whole root made the cost of that decision visible: NINETEEN newly-collected tests — router, spawn and accessor execution, str-literal length, the lifecycle driver — skipped on "cordis-wasm runtime not found", and the job's own no-skip guard reddened on them, correctly. The decision was taken rather than deferred: the job now clones cordis-wasm at the pin `f5c0562` and builds the venv `tests/test_wasm_backend.py` resolves by absolute path, declares `CORDIS_WASM` as a JOB env so `test_a_ci_set_gate_is_set_in_a_job_that_actually_runs_it` can check that the job setting it is the job collecting those files, and the registry entry is DELETED — `test_no_stale_local_declarations` would fail otherwise, which is the registry-may-only-shrink rule doing exactly what it was written for. TWELVE of the nineteen now execute. The other SEVEN are VERSION gates, not switch gates: the pinned runtime predates the instance accessor (`_instance_get`, 2 tests) and the `route:<key>` primitive (item 173, 5 tests), and no change in this repo can make them run — the primitives have to reach the cordis-wasm repo's `main` and the pin has to be bumped. Both reasons are enumerated verbatim in the guard's `ALLOWED` list, so bumping the pin makes them fail as stale rather than keeping a dead exemption alive, and nothing else can hide behind them. The guard also had to stop comparing raw XML: a reason carrying `<key>` reaches the junit report as `&lt;key&gt;`, so an exemption spelled in plain text would silently never have matched.

451. (issue #378) **A MERGE THAT KEEPS BOTH SIDES OF A ROADMAP HUNK IS INVISIBLE, AND IT HAS HAPPENED THREE TIMES (2026-09-02).** Found while attaching owners to the orphan findings. Git resolves a conflicting roadmap hunk by keeping both sides; prose has no syntax that makes that a build error, so the duplicate just sits there and the FIRST copy is the one a reader believes. Three instances on main, all from the same week: **(a) ✅ FIXED HERE.** Merge `2d2fa07f` kept a stale `106. 🚧` header line above the `106. ✅` entry that `53988312` had replaced, identical apart from the marker and with no body of its own. That one line was the only reason a landed item read as in-progress, and it is what `--check-duplicate-headers` (added in the same commit, `tools/check_roadmap_markers.py::duplicate_header_findings`) now catches. **(b) ✅ FIXED 2026-09-05 (`#406`, landed via batch `#414`); item 428's duplicated F5-F13 F-blocks are deduped and the extended `--check-duplicate-headers` F-block check now guards it.** Merge `d126bc34` had kept both sides of item 428's finding set, so F5-F13 were written TWICE: the reconciliation pass `eb6be729` (re-verified at `2d2fa07f`, before the fixes landed) and `fix/428-attestation-tail`'s own updates. Both sets now carry the same verdict and the same shas after the ownership sweep, so no reader is misled, but the item still says everything twice. **(c) ✅ FIXED 2026-09-05 (`#406`, landed via batch `#414`); item 433's duplicated F1-F10 F-blocks and the doubled correctness-riders panel are deduped.** Same shape as (b) had been: F1-F10 appeared once with `❌ STILL OPEN, RE-VERIFIED IN SOURCE ON 2d2fa07f` heads and once as the original audit text, plus the correctness-riders panel twice at 8.9 kB and 13.9 kB. **THE GAP THE NEW CHECK DOES NOT CLOSE.** `--check-duplicate-headers` asks the question per ITEM NUMBER, so it sees (a) and is blind to (b) and (c), which duplicate F-BLOCKS inside one item. The same question one level down — one F label carrying two blocks in one item — is decidable from the file alone and is the natural extension. **WHY IT MATTERS BEYOND TIDINESS.** Item 428's stale copy said F7/F8/F10/F11/F13 were STILL OPEN, re-verified in source, with line numbers; every one of them had been fixed and merged three hours later. A reader who trusted it would have re-audited five closed findings, which is the same cost item 99 and item 107 paid, and item 100 and item 112, and 101/113, 102/114, 103/115. UNOWNED: no branch, no issue.

452. (issue #379) **THE ISSUE MIGRATION SKIPPED EIGHT OPEN ITEMS, AND `--require-issue` CANNOT BE TURNED ON UNTIL IT IS FINISHED (2026-09-02).** `python3 tools/check_roadmap_markers.py --require-issue` reported 59 open/partial items citing no GitHub issue on 2026-09-02, and 56 on `c0387514` (2026-09-05) before the citation pass that carries this note. Most of those had an issue on the tracker and simply did not cite it in their own text; that mechanical fix is done in the same change (50 citations added: 47 to existing `<N>:`-titled issues, three to the new issues named next). Of the EIGHT that had no issue at all (items 5, 10, 416, 421, 422, 425, 427 and 428): "item 5" was entry 5 of the 1d conformance gap list, not an item, and is now ticked with its siblings; 10 has issue #377; 427 has #174 (closed); this item has #379 and its sibling 451 has #378; 416, 421, 422, 425 and 428 still have no issue and are the whole of what `--require-issue` should report once this lands. **The live cost named on 2026-09-02 is gone:** `--check-orphan` reports nothing on main and already runs in the `lint` job beside `--check-contradiction`, `--check-delegation` and `--check-duplicate-headers` (`.github/workflows/ci.yml:176-181`, mirrored by `make roadmap-check`). Remaining exit: issues for the five, then `--require-issue` joins the lint line.

453. **A DEMO THAT IS TYPECHECKED BUT NEVER RUN IS PROSE, AND THE TS TIER'S HAD BEEN WRONG SINCE AUGUST (2026-09-02, FIXED IN PR #243).** `node demo.ts` failed one check on main: `R2 - reactivated cache runs its effects afresh (new store)`. The assertion read `ctx.cache.get('alice') === null` and it was CORRECT when written - at `07ec35bd` the ts runtime's `MapHandle.get` answered `this.data.get(key) ?? null`. `9124c918` dropped that `?? null` deliberately and correctly, because `Opt` is bare `value | undefined` on this tier, `emit.py` lowers `match` over an Opt by discriminating on `=== undefined`, and a `null` answer made `None == None` false through `revlEq`. That commit updated `emit.py`, `runtime.ts` and `tests/test_lifecycle.py`. It did not update the demo, which has asserted something impossible ever since.

    **THE DIVERGENCE WAS NOT CROSS-TIER, WHICH IS WORTH RECORDING BECAUSE THE FAILURE LOOKED LIKE ONE.** All six tiers agree on an absent key and each answers its own native `Opt` None: python `None`, ts `undefined`, rust `Option::None`, java `Optional.empty()`, go a nil `*T` ("Opt lowers to a pointer `*T` (nil == None)"), wasm a tag-0 cell. The source is `fn get(key: Str) -> Opt[Str]`; nothing in the emitter or the runtime was wrong and the fix belonged in the demo. It was made `=== undefined` and NOT `== null` on purpose: on this tier `null` is not None, so the loose spelling would wave through precisely the regression `9124c918` fixed.

    **THE TRAP IS THAT THE TYPECHECK DID COVER THE FILE.** `backend-typescript` ran vitest, `scripts/typecheck-generated.mjs` and `tsc --noEmit`, and `tsconfig.json` includes `demo.ts`. So the demo was type-correct and green for the whole period its assertions were never evaluated. Typechecking a demo proves it compiles; only running it proves it still demonstrates anything. `backend-python` runs `.venv/bin/python demo.py`, and that is the entire reason the python demo has not rotted - not that it is better written. The ts tier now has the same property via an unconditional `node demo.ts` step (no `if:`, no env gate, no optional toolchain; the demo already exits non-zero on a failed check), held there by `tests/test_ts_demo_runs_in_ci.py` in the shape of `test_ts_typecheck_gate_runs_in_ci.py` - static, no toolchain, running in `frontend`, because a runtime probe would live inside the very job that might stop running the step. Verified non-vacuous by deleting the step and watching three of its four checks go red.

    **CONFORMANCE DOES NOT COVER THE QUESTION EITHER, AND THAT ABSENCE IS THE SECOND FINDING.** `tools/conformance.py` emits every case per tier and hands the artifacts to `tools/validate.py`, whose validators stop at compile/typecheck depth - `tsc`, `cargo check`, `javac + cordis4j stubs`, `go build`, `wasmtime compile`, and the empty depth for python. Not one of them EXECUTES. So `Map.get`'s null/undefined answer is not a conformance claim: the matrix proves a construct emits and the artifact compiles, never what it evaluates to. A cross-tier value question cannot be answered by that corpus as it stands.

    **THE SURVEY, since the same question was owed to the other five tiers.** There are exactly three tier demos. `backends/python/demo.py` runs in `backend-python`. `backends/typescript/demo.ts` ran nowhere and now runs here. `backends/wasm/demo.py` is invoked by `tests/test_wasm_backend.py::test_demo_runs_on_the_real_substrate` under `skipif(not CORDIS_WASM_PY.exists())` - and `"cordis-wasm venv not available"` is the single string in the `ALLOWED` exemption of the `backend-wasm` job's own "no wasm test may skip in CI" guard, so CI never provisions the substrate and that demo has never executed there. go, rust and java have no demo at all. **LEFT OPEN: the wasm half**, which is this item's shape sitting inside a deliberate allowance rather than an oversight, and which needs the cordis-wasm substrate in CI to close. Relates to 430 and 446 (a skip and a pass are the same colour), issue #183 (one named test file per job, so whole suites never executed) and issue #191 (the release path never exercised).

## Web-platform maturity — "make ordinary web development boring; reserve the complexity for the guarantees revl uniquely provides"

_38 closed item(s) of this section (✅ landed or ➖ declined) are archived in [roadmap-archive/14-web-platform-maturity-make-ordinary-web.md](roadmap-archive/14-web-platform-maturity-make-ordinary-web.md)._

Filed 2026-09-07 from revl-harness developer feedback (built from the harness, not a benchmark). The recommended milestone is **not another feature list** but a small, exemplary revl web application (item 462) that drives items 456–461; each item's exit criterion is that the exemplary app needs it and stops needing a workaround once it lands. Where an item belongs in a standard web library or in tooling rather than the language, say so in its design doc — the harness's size and historical workarounds do not prove revl inherently requires them.

456. **FIRST-CLASS HTTP CONTRACTS (2026-09-07, from harness feedback #1).** Routing passes bare strings and reconstructs status from body prose; the harness distinguishes unhandled routes, empty responses, errors and application output through conventions like `""` and `"::empty::"`. Provide a standard typed `Request`/`Response` with headers, typed body variants, and an explicit `Handled`/`NotHandled` outcome so status and execution outcome never require interpreting output text. Exit: a route in the exemplary app (462) sets and reads status/outcome through the typed contract with **zero** `""`/`"::empty::"` sentinels, and a mishandled route is a typed `NotHandled`, not an empty string. Owner: TBD (issue #719). Design doc TBD.

457. **ONE DEFINITION FOR ROUTES AND WIRE TYPES (2026-09-07, from harness feedback #2).** Route conditions, method tables, JSON schemas, validators, browser clients and capability descriptions are maintained in coordinated pieces today. Define an endpoint once and derive routing, request validation, response serialization, an OpenAPI description, and a TypeScript client from that single declaration. Authorization stays explicit — generated metadata must never grant access. Exit: one endpoint declaration in 462 generates routing + validation + serialization + OpenAPI + a TS client, and removing the endpoint's explicit auth step denies the request (generated metadata alone grants nothing). The explicit authorization step binds to a real auth service (Cordis SSO, `ctx.sso.validateSession`); generated route metadata never substitutes for it. Design doc: docs/design/529-sso-auth-alignment.md. Owner: TBD (issue #720).

459. ◑ **FIRST-CLASS FRONTEND ASSET INTEGRATION (2026-09-07, from harness feedback #4).** The legacy console page is ~4,800 lines of HTML/CSS/JS carried inside revl source strings, with browser tooling extracting scripts back out. Support external asset files, reusable templates with contextual escaping, source maps, and normal JS/TS tooling — revl should sit behind a clean typed boundary next to an existing frontend stack, not replace it. Exit: 462's UI is built from external assets and a real TS frontend behind a typed boundary, with no JS extracted from revl strings and source maps pointing at the original files. Binds to Cordis WebUI (`ctx.webui.addEntry`, Vue/Vite client) with the reactive-state/RPC channel expressed as a typed contract. Design doc: docs/design/526-webui-asset-alignment.md. Owner: TBD (issue #722).

460. **PREDICTABLE LIFECYCLE APIS (2026-09-07, from harness feedback #5).** Startup, readiness, cancellation, disposal, restoration and hot replacement each require substantial app-specific integration and careful interpretation today. Publish a small documented lifecycle contract — when a service is serving, what cancellation requests versus completes, what remains owned after a failure, and how recovery resumes — with actual native-runtime integration coverage, not just unit tests. Extends the liveness/admission/recovery guarantees just landed (items behind #96/#476/#622/#624). This is where revl is genuinely differentiated, so the guarantees must be dependable: a documented refusal with retained ownership beats an apparently-successful replacement whose cleanup/recovery is uncertain. Exit: a written lifecycle contract + conformance tests exercising serving → cancel-requested vs cancel-completed → owned-after-failure → recovery-resume on the native runtime, referenced by 462. Owner: TBD (issue #723).

461. **A REPRODUCIBLE DEVELOPER WORKFLOW (2026-09-07, from harness feedback #6).** Reproducing behavior has repeatedly required pinning exact compiler/runtime/cordis bindings and the correct nested interpreter. Provide one supported development command and reproducible toolchain resolution, with diagnostics that point at the original revl source and name the failing lifecycle stage. Ties to the single-binary tooling arc (#102/item 336) and revl doctor. Exit: one command runs 462 end to end; an induced error names the `.rvl` source line and the lifecycle stage that failed. Owner: TBD (issue #724).

463. **SECURITY FOLLOW-UP — THE A1 ASYNC-COLOUR FALSE-ADMIT FAMILY (2026-09-07, GHSA-p5c5-fhrh-6mqp, private).** PR #692 ported one A1 predicate hole (the `await`-in-sync-provide check gated on name resolution), but GHSA-p5c5-fhrh-6mqp describes A1 as a broad, uninvestigated false-admit family (~2289 corpus hits) where the gate admits programs the reference refuses. Exit: a full-corpus before/after verdict diff proving the A1 false-admit count reaches zero (or an enumerated, justified residual), at which point GHSA-p5c5-fhrh-6mqp can be published RESOLVED. Do not publish the advisory until this is proven. Owner: TBD.

464. **SECURITY FOLLOW-UP — THE G1 `is_upper_head` DECLARED-WIRING RESIDUAL (2026-09-07, GHSA-79rp-2wj4-5jf6, private).** PR #228 closed four of the G1 statement-position holes, but a residual `is_upper_head` false-admit (~15/335 minimals) is deliberately unfixed: the gate still admits some declared-wiring programs the reference refuses. Exit: close the `is_upper_head` residual (or record it as a permanently-accepted, documented gap), at which point GHSA-79rp-2wj4-5jf6 can be published RESOLVED with or without a caveat. Owner: TBD.

465. **PERSISTENCE BOUND TO A DRIVER-INDEPENDENT MODEL (2026-09-08, Cordis ecosystem alignment).** The exemplary app (462) persists through a revl service bound to Cordis Database (`ctx.model` / Minato), declaring its tables via the typed-model boundary (design 257), with every persistence mutation carrying a derived inverse that reverts within `database.transact` on teardown or divert, and drivers chosen at composition time. The DESIGN.md §3.1 `query(sql: Str)` sketch predates Minato and is not the app's surface. Exit: 462 persists real data with **zero** raw-SQL strings; a driver swap (memory to sqlite) requires no change to model or query code; an injected fault during a write reverts residue-free. Design doc: docs/design/527-database-persistence-alignment.md. Owner: TBD (issue #752).

466. **CORDIS TS FORK: RE-ROOT ON UPSTREAM RC.9 + RE-APPLY SCOPED HARDENING (2026-09-08, cordis sync step 2).** The TS tier vendors a flattened `packages/core` snapshot of `cordiverse/cordis` (rc.8 era) as `inso1337/cordis`, carrying a scoped UNLOADING teardown guard (a G5/A8/G7 runtime backstop). Step 1 (repin to fork HEAD `f45630e` for the scoped guard, replacing the lagging broad-guard root commit) landed separately. Re-root the vendored core on upstream rc.9 (`ed8a775`) and re-apply the scoped hardening, adopting the guarantee-bearing core fixes revl lacks: #98 lifecycle re-entry-from-failure and #109 fiber-failure-not-unhandled (both sharpen A8), plus #44/#51. Do not retire the hardening (upstream still has the G5 gap); do not vendor hmr/loader (that is 459-461 scope). Exit: fork re-rooted on rc.9 core with the scoped guard re-applied, the full `backends/typescript` vitest + `test_selfhost_emit_ts` + conformance + adversarial suites green, and the A8 teardown/conformance sequence unchanged or improved. Design doc: docs/design/cordis-upstream-sync-2026-09.md. Owner: TBD (issue #756).

467. **COUNTERFACTUAL INCIDENT REPLAY (2026-09-09, product ideation batch).** `revl replay incident.wal --under policy/new.toml` and `revl counterfactual --candidate patched.rvl --no-external-effects` re-run a recorded WAL under a different policy or against a candidate component with NO live effect authority (mocks plus recorded host responses), answering whether the incident would still happen, which effect first crossed the boundary, whether the replacement would be admitted, and how many downstream actions were causally dependent on it. It extends `revl recover`, `revl trace`, the WAL, and the admission gate, reusing the recorded run rather than re-executing against live systems. The honest scope is offline analysis over one captured incident: no new effects fire, and the report is only as complete as the recorded host responses. Exit: a recorded incident WAL replays under an alternate policy and a candidate component with zero live effects, and the report names the first dangerous crossing and whether the candidate is admitted. Owner: TBD (issue #819).

469. **ENFORCED POLICY-DRIFT CONTROL (2026-09-09, product ideation batch).** A continuously-running monitor detects when the LIVE system no longer matches the ADMITTED one — model, tool, policy, dependency and peer hashes — and on drift can suspend authority, downgrade to read-only, require re-admission, or roll back to a pinned artifact. It extends attestation (item 127) and audit, turning a one-time admission check into an ongoing invariant. The scope is hash-level identity drift between admitted and live artifacts, not semantic equivalence of a changed component. Exit: an induced hash drift between the admitted and live artifacts is detected and triggers the configured response (suspend, re-admit or rollback) with a WAL record. Owner: TBD (issue #821).

470. ◑ **INTENT-VS-ACTION REFINEMENT VERIFICATION (2026-09-09, product ideation batch).** A typed link binds a declared intent (object, verb, ceiling, tenant) to the requested authority, so the checker verifies that the executed action is a permitted refinement of the approved intent: the same or a related object, an amount within the stated ceiling, an allowed verb, the correct tenant, no hidden extra capability, and no broader recipient scope. It is a confused-deputy and goal-drift defense that sits on top of the existing capability and approval machinery. The scope is refinement checking against a declared intent, not inferring an intent the caller never stated. Exit: an action that exceeds its declared intent — a wider verb, a higher amount, a different tenant, or an extra capability — is refused with the intent it violated. Owner: TBD (issue #822). Landed: the exit sentence is met in the language, for all four excess kinds, in both stating slots. `within { object, verbs, ceilings, tenant, related, scopes }` on a service operation is the declaration, `acting { verb, tenant, scopes }` on an `emit` step or a `let` binding is what one crossing does, and `lower._check_intent_refinement` / `_check_intent_completeness` refuse a crossing that exceeds the declaration or states nothing about itself, naming the declaring operation, the declared value and the `within` clause's line (#855, #971, #1007, #1031, #1058). The runtime half compares a standing grant as a declaration at the class-(c) gate and bounds what an operator may mint against a `may mint` line (#1061, #1073). `tests/test_470_exit_criterion.py` pins the sentence as one 4x2 matrix with both halves shown non-vacuous (#1097): the check disabled reds 16 of 26 with both controls green, and the NAMING dropped while still raising reds exactly the 8 naming cases, so "is refused" and "names the intent" are pinned independently. Issue #822 CLOSED 2026-09-15. Open, and not part of the exit sentence: the lease path, and a verb vocabulary for the class-(c) gate (docs/design/470-intent-refinement.md §4). The `calls` ceiling compares two SPELLINGS, so a crossing spelled `calls=1` inside a loop is bounded at runtime by `remainingUses` (§3.6), not by this check -- "a higher amount is refused" is a statement about declarations.

472. ◑ **TYPED DATA-RETENTION AND ERASURE RECEIPTS (2026-09-09, product ideation batch).** A `Retained[T]` type carries a retention deadline, a geographic residence, legal-hold exceptions, the set of allowed deleters, and whether derivatives (summaries, indexes, embeddings) are in scope, and a deletion request produces a signed erasure receipt over each known replica and derivative. It extends `Secret[T]` and the taint machinery (items 249/256) with a time-and-residence dimension the type system enforces at persistence sinks. The scope reaches the replicas and derivatives the system knows about; it cannot attest to copies made outside its boundary. Exit: a `Retained` value past its deadline is refused at persistence sinks, and an erasure request emits a signed receipt naming each in-scope replica and derivative. Owner: TBD (issue #824).

473. ◑ **SLO CONTRACT + ROLLOUT GATE (2026-09-09, product ideation batch).** A composition-level `slo { p95_latency, success_rate, recovery_time, approval_wait, max_pending_tasks }` contract the runtime monitors, refusing a risky rollout when predicted budgets or SLOs would fail, diverting to fallback providers, or triggering a safe pause or e-stop before a full incident, with SLO receipts tied to the running generation. It extends budgets (item 260), lifecycle (item 460) and e-stop (item 443). The scope is the declared budget set and the predicted or observed values the runtime can measure, not arbitrary external service-level indicators. Exit: a rollout predicted to breach a declared SLO is refused, and a live breach triggers the declared fallback or pause with an SLO receipt on the generation. Owner: TBD (issue #825).

475. ◑ **CONFIDENTIAL / ATTESTED-TEE PLACEMENT (2026-09-09, product ideation batch).** A placement that requires an attested trusted-execution environment (`requires attested_tee`, a region, `outbound_network = forbidden`): a worker proves via remote attestation that it runs an approved bundle inside a permitted enclave or sandbox, and the composition receives only signed results and receipts. It extends the verifiable private peer-pool design (docs/design/461-verifiable-private-peer-pool.md) and sandbox placement (item 411). The scope depends on the attestation root and the enclave technology available; a host that cannot produce a valid attestation simply cannot satisfy the placement. Exit: a `requires attested_tee` placement admits a worker only on a valid remote-attestation of the approved bundle and enclave, and refuses an unattested or wrong-bundle worker. Owner: TBD (issue #827).
Landed: the placement-file spelling of the demand. `[processes.<p>.attest]` with `requires = "attested_tee"`, `bundle`, `measurements`, a region and `outbound_network = "forbidden"` is a grammar word in the placement surface (src/revl/placement.py: `parse_tee_requirement`, `render_tee_requirement`, `process_placement_slot`, `admit_peer_for_process`, `tee_placement_diagnostic`). It parses into tee_attestation.py's `TeeRequirement`, lands in `PlacementSlot.attested_tee`, and defers its verdict to `tee_admits` through `peer_offer.offer_eligible`, so the file spelling cannot disagree with the verifier. A malformed table is refused by name (unknown word, no word, unknown key, empty measurement or region set, a posture other than forbidden, `region` and `regions` together) and a well-formed demand is refused by `run_placement` before anything spawns, because this build has no peer transport and no attester root: an attested placement is refused rather than run unattested. docs/attested-tee-placement.md, 54 tests in tests/test_placement_attested_tee.py plus 3 in tests/test_tee_attestation.py. Landed: the attestation root and real quote formats. src/revl/tee_quote.py parses an Intel TDX Quote v4 (48-byte header + 584-byte TD quote body + the ECDSA-P256 authentication data, with all three chain hops checked: the PCK key signs the QE Report, the QE Report's report_data is SHA-256(attestation key || QE auth data), the attestation key signs the quote body) and an AMD SEV-SNP ATTESTATION_REPORT v2/v3 (fixed 0x4A0 bytes, VCEK over the first 0x2A0, R and S as 72-byte LITTLE-endian P-384 scalars), and verifies each with ECDSA over NIST P-256/P-384 written in hashlib and integers because the package ships no runtime dependency (each prime is the standard's FORMULA, not a transcribed constant, and the tests re-derive the generator's membership and order). The chain terminates in a `HardwareRoot`: platform keys the OPERATOR pinned, or a one-hop `PlatformEndorsement` signed by a pinned vendor root, and the root is never read out of the record. Two bindings make the hardware signature cover the whole claim: the 64-byte report_data carries SHA-512 of the evidence's canonical body (`evidence_report_data`), so peer, bundle, region, posture, window and CHALLENGE are covered and a captured quote cannot be rewritten around; and the measurement the record STATES is compared against the register the hardware REPORTED, because report_data binds what the workload said and only the register is what the platform measured. The symmetric MAC stays as `DevMacRoot`, explicitly not a production root: `is_production = False`, construction needs `acknowledged_dev_only=True` by name, every verdict it reaches INCLUDING the admission carries `DEV_ROOT_NOTE` in its reason, and `require_hardware_root=True` refuses it outright; the two verifiers cannot be crossed and supplying both refuses. Single verdict path preserved: the file spelling's reason stays byte-identical to `offer_eligible`'s on the hardware path. Refusals pinned by name: forged root (unpinned platform key, a re-signed internally-consistent quote, an unattached attestation key, an endorsement by an unpinned/expired/altered/wrong-vendor/wrong-subject root, the peer's own offer key named as the platform), wrong measurement (outside the permitted set, and a record whose claim disagrees with the register), replay (a consumed challenge, a quote reused under a new challenge, and every single-member rewrite around a valid quote), malformed structure (truncated, unknown version, SGX tee_type, wrong attestation-key type, non-Intel QE vendor, signature-data length short or long, oversized P-384 scalar, non-canonical ECDSA scalar, off-curve key), expired/stale/future-dated, no replay ledger, and a debuggable TD or guest policy. docs/tee-attestation-root.md; 69 tests in tests/test_tee_quote.py + 56 in tests/test_tee_hardware_root.py + 23 committed quote fixtures under tests/fixtures/tee/ (regenerable by generate.py and asserted byte-equal to a fresh generation). Landed: the delivery half, so the item's other sentence ("the composition receives only signed results and receipts") is enforced rather than only expressible. `receipt_admits` had no caller anywhere in src/, which left three fail-OPEN shapes: no delivery gate at all, a correlation the CALLER re-supplied (`peer_id`, `bundle` and `nonce` were arguments, so a delivery was only as good as the caller's memory of what was admitted), and a replay check a caller defeated by handing it a FRESH delivery ledger each call. An admission now PRODUCES the only object a result can arrive through: `tee_attestation.open_attested_run` returns an `AttestedRun` and `run.accept_result(receipt, result)` is the delivery, with `peer_offer.offer_admission` and `placement.admit_run_for_process` the same path at the pool and placement-file tiers. The run carries the admission's own peer, bundle and challenge and owns its delivery ledger, and `accept_result` has no parameter for any of them, so the correlation is structural rather than a convention. The admission verdict stays `tee_admits`' own reason text verbatim at every tier, so no surface grows a second opinion. One new gate rather than plumbing: `EnclaveEvidence` grows an optional `receipt_key_id`, the fingerprint of the key the enclave signs results with, which rides in the body so the attester's signature covers it and the quote's report_data covers it on the hardware path; `open_attested_run` refuses a composition holding a different key and, by default, evidence that names none, because an unnamed receipt key is the ambiguous case. The member is additive, so every committed quote fixture is unchanged, and the pre-binding shape works only by name and labels every verdict it reaches with RECEIPT_KEY_UNBOUND_NOTE. Refusals pinned by name and all in the refuse direction: no receiving key, the peer's own offer key as the receiving key, a receipt key the attestation did not name, an unnamed one, a present-but-unreadable one, a receipt_key_id rewritten on a MAC record (signature) or on a quoted one (the root refuses the quote), every admission refusal yielding no run, an offer admitted by the attestation but failing a later slot gate yielding no run, a slot or process demanding no attested TEE yielding no run, a missing or non-record receipt, a receipt signed by the peer or another enclave, one covering another result, run, peer or bundle, a stale or future-dated one, and a second delivery for the same run. Two asymmetries are deliberate and pinned: a REFUSED delivery does not consume the run's one delivery (or junk presented first would deny an honest worker its result), and a run refused on the composition's own misconfiguration does not burn the peer's challenge, while one refused on the receipt-key binding does, because that gate runs after a valid proof. docs/attested-result-delivery.md; 49 tests in tests/test_tee_result_delivery.py. Still open on this item: the vendor DER chain (Intel PCK / AMD KDS certificates in place of the one-hop endorsement) and an attestation root in the lawful_retry dispatcher; quorum is item 471 and drift control is item 469.

477. ◑ **SECURITY FOLLOW-UP — THE `revl-gate` FLAT-SIBLING STACK ABORT (2026-09-11, GHSA-m3fg-gp7f-6cwr, private).** The advisory is right that `MAX_SOURCE_BYTES` does not bound the recursion it compensates for, and wrong about which recursion: `p_stmts` is not what aborts. Measured on the committed crate, the aborting class is FLAT SIBLING recursion at any one bracket level — `g(1, 1, ...)` with 11,386 arguments is a 34 KB source (13% of the byte bound) that aborts a stock 8 MiB stack, `[1, 1, ...]` and a run of `let` statements go down at the same counts, and blank or `//`-comment lines between those statements cost no frames at all. The `nesting_depth` pre-scan cannot see it by construction: its `sibs`/`up_sibs` accumulators exist precisely so siblings do NOT accumulate depth, and the worst shape is one line at depth one. Exit: a pre-descent bound on items at one bracket level, refusing through the EXISTING `outside_frontier` arm. ✅ FIXED 2026-09-11 (`fix/gate-level-items-bound`). `MAX_LEVEL_ITEMS = 1024` (`crates/revl-gate/src/frontier.rs`, generated from `tools/build_gate_crate.py`) counts siblings per bracket level over the literal-stripped source — a level's first item is counted when the level opens, and `,`, `;` and a newline each separate two items, so a statement run is counted by its line ends (the conservative direction: a comment-heavy source can be declined, a dense one cannot be waved through). Measured before choosing it: only 4 of the 648 census programs exceed 512 items at one level and only 1 exceeds 1024 (`selfhost/lower.rvl` at 3,691, already refused by the byte bound at 481 KB), the largest program the gate actually decides is `selfhost/checker.rvl` at 604, so the bound changes no verdict on the whole corpus — re-run confirms `no change from the baseline`. Boundary verified against the built crate, not asserted: 1,024 items decide, 1,025 refuse, and the four previously-aborting shapes (11,386 args at 8 MiB, 2,900 `let`s and a 2,900-element list at 2 MiB, 3,072 args at 1 MiB) all return `outside_frontier` with exit 0. Regression: `frontier::tests::too_many_items_at_one_level_is_a_gap` + `::the_level_bound_is_neither_a_byte_nor_a_depth_bound`, `tests/admit.rs::a_source_over_the_level_bound_is_declined_rather_than_risked` + `::a_source_at_the_level_bound_is_still_decided` (the over-bound case runs on a 1 MiB thread so the test cannot itself abort), and the Python mirror in `tools/gate_reference_census.py` held against the rust by `tests/test_gate_reference_census.py`. The advisory's own reproduction and root cause need correcting to this shape before it is published. **The advisory's SECONDARY finding is NOT fixed and is not this bound's business:** the per-level `ts.clone()` in the emitted recursion leaves the gate O(n²) in statement count where the reference is linear, which is a codegen shape (emit the accumulator/tail-recursion idiom as a loop, as the reference does) and stays open here. Owner: TBD.

485. **SECURITY FOLLOW-UP — `json_parse` DECODED A LONE SURROGATE ESCAPE FOUR DIFFERENT WAYS (2026-09-12, GHSA-x29m-48mh-j9jx, private).** `stdlib/json.rvl`'s `json_parse(s: Str) -> Any` is `pure`, and `docs/strings.md` pins a `Str` as a sequence of Unicode scalar values, so a JSON string escape that denotes a UTF-16 code unit rather than a scalar — an UNPAIRED `\uXXXX`, e.g. the document `"\ud800"` — has no value it can legally produce. `docs/syntax-2.0.md` section 0.1 already refuses an unpaired surrogate "wherever it appears ... reachable from every JSON-carried source path (the LSP, the MCP verbs, `gate.admit(str)`)", and the frontend enforces that (`src/revl/lexer.py:361`, `src/revl/lower.py:5944`, `src/revl/attest.py:148`); `json_parse` did not, and the four tiers gave FOUR answers, measured on `origin/main` with the agreement probe (assert a literal predicted observable per tier, because rust and go print no failing value): py's `json.loads` returned the RAW lone surrogate, which `stdlib/str.rvl`'s `utf8_encode_scalar` (`stdlib/str.rvl:164`) spelled `ED A0 80` — the WTF-8 encoding of a surrogate, and NOT valid UTF-8, so a hash, a signature or a wire write over the parsed value produced bytes no UTF-8 decoder accepts; the @ts body returned the six-character escape TEXT `\ud800` verbatim, a silently different value; `serde_json` rejected the escape, which is the one correct answer; and Go's `encoding/json` silently substituted `U+FFFD`, indistinguishable from a document that genuinely spelled a replacement character. The parameter is `Any`, so the frontend's literal check structurally cannot see it — the same blind spot item 481 recorded for a computed non-finite `Float`. Exit: refuse on every tier, which is what `serde_json` already did, so rust is the reference tier and its bodies are UNCHANGED. ✅ FIXED 2026-09-12 (`fix/json-nonfinite-refusal`). The technique is forced by each runtime rather than chosen: py and ts check the DECODED value (the @py body walks `str`/`list`/`dict` with an `isascii()` fast path and raises `ValueError`; the @ts body iterates the decoded string by code point — `for...of` plus `codePointAt` — so a well-formed pair reports its astral scalar and passes), while the two @go bodies must scan the INPUT TEXT (`revlHex4`/`revlLoneSurrogate`), because `encoding/json` has already replaced the code unit by the time a value exists and a substituted `U+FFFD` is indistinguishable from a spelled one. The scanner pairs a high escape with an immediately following low one and skips an ESCAPED backslash, so the document `"\\ud800"` (a literal backslash followed by `u`) is NOT refused — that false positive is a pinned control. `json_parse` keeps the split it already had (total on rust/go, raising on py/ts) and `json_try_parse` routes all four into `Err`. **Regression:** `tests/test_json_cross_tier_bytes.py`, 60 checks across all four tiers — ten unpaired documents (lone high, lone low, high-then-ASCII, high-then-`\u0041`, high at EOF, low at start, pair-then-lone, object key, inside a list, nested) refused on every tier, the escaped-backslash, valid-pair and plain-ASCII documents still ACCEPTED on every tier as the controls that this is not a blanket ban on `\u`, the byte observable of the pair (`34,240,159,152,128,34` = U+1F600 in UTF-8) pinned on every tier so the astral path is proved to still decode, and the py tier pinned at the byte level: the probe that pipes the parsed value through `str_utf8_bytes(json_stringify(v))` returned `34,237,160,128,34` (WTF-8) before the fix and `REFUSED` after. `docs/stdlib-json.md`'s ts-parse section carries the rule. Owner: TBD.

509. 🚧 **MULTI-PARTY QUORUM: BIND THE IDENTITY OF A CAST INSTEAD OF BELIEVING IT (item 471 residual, #979).** `require N of {a, b, c}` exists so an irreversible crossing needs N distinct operators to agree, but `Session._cast_vote` took the voter from `as_token`, a string the caller chose (`voter = as_token if as_token is not None else self._operator_token()`), and counted distinct NAMES. Reproduced on `bf14852f`: one session bound to `alice` (the proposer), `approve_ticket(hash, as_token="bob")` then `approve_ticket(hash, as_token="carol")`, `counted: 2`, `approved: True`, crossing admitted. The same held for `escalate_ticket`, `revoke_ticket` and `override_ticket`, so an override could be recorded against a name the caller merely typed. Documented as a bound in Decision 5 of `docs/design/471-quorum-approval.md`, not a regression. Fix: the profile declares a per-operator vote credential as a SHA-256 digest (`operator bob key sha256:<hex>`, `"key"` in JSON); a new pure `revl.mcp.quorum.resolve_cast` attributes a cast to the session's serve-time operator or to an operator whose declared credential the caller proved (`asSecret`), and to nothing else; `Session._bind_cast` records the unbound case as a `quorum-refused` row before raising it. Fail closed on every axis: `unbound-identity` (no profile to check against), `unknown-operator`, `unkeyed-identity`, `unproven-identity`, `unnamed-credential`. Distinctness is counted over a DERIVED principal (the session binding, or the credential digest proven), so two operators issued one secret are one principal and supply one vote between them (`same-principal`) — a refusal a count of names cannot make. The `quorum-vote` row carries the principal (a hash OF the digest, never the digest) and `boundBy`; the receipt shape is unchanged, so `RECEIPT_VERSION` stays 1. **The residual, stated plainly:** N counted votes required N distinct secrets, which is NOT proof that N humans consented. A credential is a bearer token, it can be shared, delegated or stolen, the deployer who writes the profile can hold all of them, and every cast still arrives over ONE session's wire, so an operator who has collected two secrets satisfies a two-of-M rule and the graph reads as two principals because to this boundary it was. Closing that needs the per-caller authenticated transport (items 39 / 55): each cast on its own authenticated connection, signed over the question's binding so a captured credential is not replayable. Also not taken here: checking the named voter's own operator grants, because who may vote is the rule's approver set and a second membership test could disagree with it. Regression: 18 cases in `tests/test_979_quorum_identity_binding.py`, with `test_the_self_quorum_is_refused_on_a_session_with_no_profile` driving the pre-fix configuration exactly (it admits on `bf14852f`) and `test_two_distinct_principals_still_satisfy_the_quorum` the non-vacuity control. `revl.mcp.quorum` is not a frontend checker module and does not belong in `attest.RULESET_MODULES`: that digest identifies what the COMPILER refuses (`src/revl/*.py`), and this decides what an MCP session admits at a crossing; `tools/build_gate_crate.py --check` reports in sync. 146 passed across the two item-471 suites and the new one, 692 across the operator/approval/MCP slice. Owner: TBD.

## Model portfolio, computer-use, and the operator surfaces

_2 closed item(s) of this section (✅ landed or ➖ declined) are archived in [roadmap-archive/15-model-portfolio-computer-use-and-the.md](roadmap-archive/15-model-portfolio-computer-use-and-the.md)._

Filed 2026-09-19 from an external review of the 2026-09-09 batch and of what has landed since. The review names **seven remaining frontiers** and, separately, a cluster of model-native ideas. Each of the seven was checked against this file before a number was minted, because four of them are already owned and a fifth is not a public item at all:

- **Frontier 1 — native self-hosting closure with no silent defer.** Owned: items 146/391 (issues #84/#106) and the requires-resolution layer (#346). The 2026-09-18 residual set this review names had **no item on this file at all** — it existed only as open issues — so it was not "already owned" and is numbered below as 526-531 (#1148, #1151, #1153, #1157, #1172, #1175).
- **Frontier 2 — formal L2 must match runtime admission.** Owned: items 132/133 with the A9 rows (#1161, #1166, #1169). Not re-filed.
- **Frontier 3 — finish A2A push and stream transport.** Owned: the residual of item 439 (issue #118); `docs/design/439-a2a-task-lifecycle.md` and `docs/design/439-a2a-transport-binding.md` are already written. Not re-filed.
- **Frontier 4 — close the private peer pool into a usable product.** Not owned anywhere; this is **item 524**.
- **Frontier 5 — finish the deployable web application story.** Owned: items 456-462 (issues #724/#725, `docs/design/525-530`); `docs/webapp-competitiveness-report.md` exists, so item 462's remaining report gate may now be met. Not re-filed.
- **Frontier 6 — resolve the runtime security residuals rather than add security features.** Audit-surface work, which CONTRIBUTING rule 4 routes to a **private security advisory** and never to a public item; the Go tier module cache and the shell-quote surface have already gone that way. Deliberately not filed as an item.
- **Frontier 7 — true portable runtime parity.** Split by nature. The gap-closing half is owned by the Stream items, the Rust native compilation frontier (items 332/334) and the self-host items. The half the review actually asks for in its own words — *"publish a support matrix with hard guarantees … honest refusal is already a strength; unclear tier scope is the risk"* — was owned nowhere and is **item 523**.

The review's own shortlist of five high-impact moves therefore needed **one** new item (the peer pool), and its closing thesis — that the project *"does not need another large list of speculative features"* and should connect what exists into one demonstration — is recorded as **item 525**. The typed model boundary is item 257 (`docs/design/257-typed-model-boundary.md`) and the confinement argument is `docs/prompt-injection-resistance.md`; several of the items below build on both, and neither is re-filed.

The cluster has one recurring shape, and it is the shape item 161 established for providers: **the author leaves the choice open until execution, and the choice is checked at admission.** Items 512-519 apply it to a model, 521-522 to a GUI, and 524 to a peer set. The review's eight model-native ideas map onto 512-519 one-to-one — routing, grammar-constrained tool calls, a local/cloud taint router, a device capability profile, a local council, a signed decision record, shadow promotion and per-model attenuation. The two operator-surface items (520, 523) are the ones this review judged closest to load-bearing already: 520 because the pieces are all landed and only the lifecycle is missing, 523 because an adopter cannot currently answer "what is guaranteed on the tier I ship".

512. (issue #1186) **🚧 PARTIAL, EXIT MET, SLICES 1 TO 4 LANDED (2026-09-20, merged `d205c2cca`, `99b159d33`, `1377792ca`): the declaration is checked under `G-MODEL-PLACE` with no IR written, a confidential value placed off the device is refused (item 514), a crossing on a role its block does not name is refused, and the self-host decides the declaration half under a `MODEL` tag. Open: the crossing refusal is checked over the block's whole role set rather than per origin arm, so it does not name an origin as this item asks, and slice 5, the role in the manifest, is PR 1532 in review. MODEL PLACEMENT AS A CHECKED ROUTE CONDITION (2026-09-19, external review).** An action that needs a completion routes to a model the way it already routes to a provider (item 161, `docs/router.md`): a `route model on <action>` clause selecting a role by origin or class, with the role a declared placement rather than a host detail. A crossing that reaches a role outside the permitted set is refused at admission naming the action, the origin and the role, in the same shape as item 421's placement refusal. Note the ordering: 421's F6 is one of the two open `--check-tier-parity` findings, so it lands first. The scope is placing a call, not scheduling it: which of two admissible models is cheaper is a policy question, and the route is a permission. Exit: a component whose action is declared confidential and routed to a cloud role does not compile, and the diagnostic names the action, the origin and the role. Owner: TBD (issue #1186). SLICE 1 LANDED: `docs/design/531-model-placement.md` designs the whole item and its five slices; the first is the DECLARATION half, checked. `model role NAME <residence>` and a `route model on <action>` component-prelude clause, both contextual identifiers so `KEYWORDS` is untouched, with eleven refusals under a new `G-MODEL-PLACE` code and the exit test's diagnostic naming the action, the origin and the role. The catch-all `*` is DEFINED not to cover a confidentiality origin, so `* -> cloud` can never be the sentence that sends a confidential input off the device; routing the `secret` origin is refused at every residence and cites `G-SECRET-FLOW`, which already named an LLM prompt a disclosure sink for a bound secret. The clause writes NO IR, because 512 is a permission checked at admission and not a runtime selection, so no emitter changed and an admitted program is byte-identical to the same program without the block. What slice 1 left open has since landed (updated 2026-09-29). The value side is item 514 (merged `99b159d33`), and it reads the route table `model_route.check` returns. The crossing side (slice 4, merged `1377792ca`) refuses a crossing whose `model.<role>` token names a role no arm names, over the block's whole role set rather than per origin. The self-host port (slice 3, same merge) decides the declaration half under a named `MODEL` tag; the value and crossing sides read a flow position that gate does not walk.
    The review's own sketch of this is a route block keyed on the step's risk and shape rather than on a model's name — a low-risk structured decision, a local tool call and an extraction going to three different local models while a high-risk action goes to a human — and its last line is the one that decides this item's design: **the router may recommend a destination, and revl still decides whether the destination is allowed.** That split is why 512 is a permission rather than a scheduler, and it is the boundary 515 has to schedule inside.

513. (issue #1187) **🚧 PARTIAL, SLICES 1, 2 AND 4 LANDED (merged `23e94c274`, `b90515fbc`): the grammar, derived from the same schema object item 257's validator reads and pinned by `tests/test_decode_grammar_513.py`; a python-tier provider seam, where a provider that claims the constraint and breaks it gets a named `GrammarNotHonouredError` and one that ignores the grammar is still not refused; and a JSON-Schema-shaped dialect. Slice 3, the `model.json[T](prompt)` surface the exit names, was measured and deferred (PR 1364), so the exit is unmet. The five other tiers now refuse a `validated` crossing by name (PR 1413). Wiring the grammar into real providers' structured-output modes is PR 1524 in review. COMPILE THE TYPED BOUNDARY INTO GRAMMAR-CONSTRAINED DECODING (2026-09-19, external review).** When a component asks a model for a structured value the compiler already knows the type (item 257), but the model is asked in prose and its output is validated and repaired, so a schema-valid answer is probabilistic where the decoder could make anything else unemittable: compile `T`'s schema into a decoding grammar and pass it with the call. The gain is not only accuracy, it is the emission budget (item 260) — every invalid sample is a wasted crossing and a retry multiplier against a ceiling. The refusal half is the guarantee and must not degrade: a `T` the boundary cannot express (item 257's `fully_expressible`) is refused at compile time, never silently repaired at run time. Exit: `model.json[T](prompt)` admits only grammar-valid samples for an expressible `T`, and refuses at compile time for an inexpressible one with the position named. Owner: TBD (issue #1187).

514. (issue #1188) **🚧 PARTIAL, EXIT MET (2026-09-20, merged `99b159d33`): a `confidential` value reaching a `model.*` crossing whose `route model` block does not place it on an `on_device` role is refused under `G-MODEL-PLACE`, naming the origin, the role and its residence, pinned by `tests/test_model_ceiling_514.py`. Not as written: a role declares a residence, not a list of origins it may receive; `secret` stays with `G-SECRET-FLOW`; and a component with no `route model` block is not checked. AN ORIGIN CEILING ON THE MODEL ROLE (2026-09-19, external review).** Item 249 makes a completion an `Untrusted[T]` source and gives the lattice origins; item 343 scopes an emission by the origins it may carry. Nothing joined the two, so app code and not the checker decided which model saw which input, and a value already carrying a confidential origin could be sent to a cloud model. A model role declares the origins it may receive, and a value whose origin is above that ceiling is refused naming both. This is confinement rather than instruction: a value whose origin cannot be admitted is never a candidate for the call, which is a stronger statement than adversarial-input prompting (the argument in `docs/prompt-injection-resistance.md`). Exit: a value carrying a confidential origin reaching a role declared for public origins does not compile, and the diagnostic names the origin and the role. Owner: TBD (issue #1188).

515. (issue #1189) **🚧 PARTIAL, SLICES 1, 2, 4 AND 5 LANDED (2026-09-26 to 2026-10-02, merged `6a80e1fe4`, `b6b85f39f`, `fe631d52a`, `99900efa7`, `7a6081c84`): a role's device profile and ordered candidate sets are checked, and a scheduler places routed actions on a host's DECLARED devices, refuses when none fits, and hands the decision to the child, which re-derives it or does not boot. Open: slice 3's reader landed via #2106 (merge `2435f7c5a`), reading the recorded `timeline` purely, but its ranking rule is deferred to #2118 and its declared cost is out of scope, and the conductor-side consumption of residency remains blocked behind #2118; the device check runs when a placement is run, against declared supply, not at compile time. THE MODEL PORTFOLIO AS A SCHEDULABLE, DEVICE-PROFILED RESOURCE (2026-09-19, external review).** A model is not placed like a provider: it has a device profile (GPU/CPU/NPU, memory, quantisation), a load and unload cost, and a lifetime that should be shared. Model providers declare the resource they need and are provisioned by key, so the existing lifetime machinery supplies sharing and teardown (the item-243 witness pair, the G5/G7 teardown contract, the handoff rule) instead of a parallel scheduler. The device profile is config-as-data, so admission refuses a placement the target cannot satisfy. Exit: two consumers injecting one model key share a single provision, teardown unloads once and `no_residue` holds, and a placement onto a host whose declared device cannot satisfy the profile is refused. Owner: TBD (issue #1189).
    **The roster the review actually names**, recorded here as CANDIDATES rather than as a shopping list, because the item is satisfied by any set that meets the device-profile contract: a typed-decision and routing model (NanoJev), a tiny tool-automation model (Needle 3), a local embedder for retrieval and peer matching, a general edge tool-calling and light-reasoning model (MiniCPM5-2B), and a heavier local planning and multimodal model (Bonsai 2) — with revl as the execution kernel rather than one model asked to be good at everything. The role split is itself the evidence that the device profile is a real dimension and not a formality: those five differ in quantisation, memory and load cost by orders of magnitude, which is what makes one shared provision per key worth having instead of a load per call. Written down so the next agent evaluates a concrete roster rather than rediscovering the design; none of them is a dependency, and a portfolio of one satisfies this item.
    **Ornith-1.5 is the candidate this roster was actually read against**, and it is recorded here as the expert-tier member rather than as the router. The material supplies three device profiles for it — a 9B mobile-class build, a 35B mixture-of-experts with roughly 3B active, and a 397B frontier build — and that spread is this item's design argument arriving as a concrete case: one model at three quantisation and memory points is three placements and three load costs, not three models, which is exactly why the provision is keyed rather than per-call. What it is placed for is the one role no small member of this roster can hold: repository-grounded work on the compiler itself, which is the subject of the section that follows this one. Recorded on the same terms as the other five — a candidate, not a dependency, and subject to the licence and provenance check every placement needs before it is a placement rather than a name.

516. (issue #1190) **🚧 PARTIAL, SLICES 1 TO 5 LANDED (2026-09-20 to 2026-09-24, merged `13bc22a15`, `1377792ca`, `49337029b`, `553540543`, `b800456c5`): `on_tie allow`, `quorum answered` and `aggregate first` are refused by name under `G-COUNCIL-SPLIT`; a `route model` arm may name a council, and an off-device member is refused on a confidential value; a match on `Aggregate[T]` must name `Split` and `Inquorate`; members may take per-member inputs under a per-member ceiling; the self-host decides the declaration. Both exit clauses hold at compile time. Open: there is no runtime aggregator, so nothing asks the members at run time, which design note 543 lists as a non-goal. A MODEL COUNCIL WITH ROLE-BOUND MEMBERS AND TYPED DISAGREEMENT (2026-09-19, external review).** Asking one model to be confident is not evidence; where an action is consequential the useful shape is several models with declared roles (proposer, adversary, verifier) and a typed aggregation, each member placed by 512/514 so a local adversary can read an origin the cloud proposer may not. The checked property is that disagreement can never be silently resolved toward allow: an aggregation that admits on a tie, or that ignores an unreachable member, is refused. This is the model counterpart of item 471 and the two must not be conflated — a council's members are models and carry no identity, while a 471 vote comes from a bound operator and is a statement about consent. Exit: a two-member council that disagrees does not admit, and an aggregation written to admit on a tie is refused at compile time. Owner: TBD (issue #1190).

517. (issue #1191) **🚧 PARTIAL, EXIT MET ON THE PY TIER (2026-09-20, merged `b573911d6` and `96bcba5b5`, CI step `053db3734`): a sealed, MAC-covered decision record in `src/revl/model_evidence.py`, sealed at the crossing onto the WAL by the py runtime; `revl replay` rebuilds the decision from the WAL alone and an edited member yields no reading, pinned by `tests/test_model_evidence_replay_552.py`. Open: only the py tier writes the record, `revl canary` does not read it, and nothing emits it where the crossing is irreversible. THE MODEL DECISION AS A SIGNED EVIDENCE OBJECT (2026-09-19, external review).** When this was filed, the one step of a revl execution with no evidence attached was the model's choice: the WAL recorded effects and emissions, so a replay could detect that two worlds differ (item 496) but could not say what the model was given, which candidates existed, which policy was in force, or how deep the fallback went. A `ModelDecision` object binds the model hash, the prompt hash, the candidate set, the sampling parameters, the input origins (item 249), the policy in force and the fallback depth, and is written beside the crossing and emitted where the crossing is irreversible, so a decision artifact is subject to the rules of any other evidence. It must not retain raw untrusted prompt text by default: hashes are the binding, and retaining content is an explicit decision that carries the taint. Exit: a recorded run replays a model decision from the artifact alone, and an edited field fails its digest. Owner: TBD (issue #1191).

518. (issue #1192) **🚧 PARTIAL, EXIT MET ON THE PY TIER (last 2026-09-27, merged `7030dd080`): agreement over item 517's records (`7a1f2793b`), the shadow schedule and world comparison (`5e29acf22`), the py completion seam (`ce53dbe3c`, PR 1432), and `src/revl/shadow_register.py`, which lands a threshold promotion with its comparison attached and reverts it on an induced divergence, naming the step. Open: no tier routes by the register yet, and ts, java, rust, wasm and cordis have no shadow seam. SHADOW ROUTING WITH EVIDENCE-GATED, REVERTIBLE PROMOTION (2026-09-19, external review).** Moving an action from one model to a successor (cheaper, faster, local, newer) has no gate. `revl canary` already serves one realm on a successor, compares the two recorded worlds, attributes the first differing step to an exact (component, realm), and derives the revert with the survivors proved; what was missing when this was filed is the scheduling half, which slices 2 and 3 added on the py tier. Serve a declared fraction of a model action in shadow (the incumbent's answer is used, both worlds are recorded), accumulate agreement per action class, promote on a stated threshold, and revert on the first attributed divergence, naming it. Deliberately reuses item 496's rationale, not just its machinery: the verdict is a replay comparison, NEVER a metric threshold, so no latency or cost metric can promote on its own. Exit: an induced divergence in shadow reverts the promotion and names the step that diverged, and a promotion on an agreement threshold lands with the comparison attached. Owner: TBD (issue #1192).

519. (issue #1193) **🚧 PARTIAL, SLICES 1, 2, 3 AND 6 LANDED (2026-09-26 to 2026-10-07, merged `6a80e1fe4`, `fb46f872f`, `1bb7e86f4`): a `model role` may declare `reaches [...]`; a component whose role reaches past what it holds is refused under `G-MODEL-PLACE` naming both sets, an undeclared reach is refused rather than read as empty, and a narrower role is admitted with the dropped capabilities recorded, pinned by `tests/test_model_attenuation_519.py`. A crossing placed on a role by its `model.<role>` token is an edge of the product with or without a `route model` block (slice 2, `tests/test_model_reach_crossing.py`), the role's reach is folded into the spawn closure so a spawner cannot grant a child a reach it does not hold (slice 3, `tests/test_model_reach_spawn.py`, both engines), and the fold's predicate is the exemption itself (a component that reaches ANY boundary is in the product) rather than the token spelling it read first (slice 6), so a component holding `net.request` that routes through a model reaching `shell.exec` is refused and no longer accounted for what it held alone. The exit asks for attenuation to the intersection; what shipped refuses the wider program instead. Open: a council's own reach rather than its members' (slice 5, partial), and the predicate measures that the held set is EMPTY, never that it is COMPLETE: a component that consults a model with no `route model` block and no `model.`-spelled token is still unaccounted, and `reaches [...]` remains an unverified author claim checked at compile time only. PUT THE MODEL ROLE IN THE ATTENUATION PRODUCT (2026-09-19, external review).** The product in `docs/capability-attenuation.md` accounts for services, realms, origins and budgets. A model is an authority surrogate — it chooses which capability a component reaches for — and it was not in the product, so a component holding `net` that routed through a model able to reach `shell` was accounted for what the component held rather than for what the pair could reach. Add the role with a declared reachable-capability set so the effective ceiling is the intersection, and a widening is refused naming both sets. This is the same shape as item 344's request-scoped grant applied to the model seam, which is also why it is not a security escape: the item adds a checked property and narrows nothing that is currently enforced. Exit: a component whose model role reaches a capability the component does not hold attenuates to the intersection, and a candidate asserting a wider reach is refused. Owner: TBD (issue #1193).

520. (issue #1194) **🚧 PARTIAL, SLICE 1 LANDED (2026-09-20, merged `7b75cb9bd`): `tools/evolution_controller.py` walks four preconditions and three measured stages to one recorded verdict citing each stage's supplier, and refuses a widened authority at admit, before any measurement is read, pinned by `tests/test_evolution_controller.py`. Open: stage records are supplied rather than produced by the real tools, the authority diff is declared by the caller rather than computed, and termination is bounded per proposal, not proved. GOVERNED SELF-EVOLUTION: ONE LIFECYCLE, AND AN AUTHORITY THE SYSTEM MAY NOT EVOLVE (2026-09-19, external review).** The pieces exist and are separate: the repair loop (item 62), the operator cockpit (63), derived semver (64), generation history and undo (65), and the self-extending runtime's propose/swap/rollback with its EDGE-1 health gate (item 334, issue #100). What did not exist when this was filed is the controller, one lifecycle (propose, admit, activate, observe, decide) whose verdict is recorded once and which cites for each stage the mechanism that supplies it, nor the invariant it must state: **a system may evolve its behaviour, never the rules that govern its authority.** That half is partly enforced already, by item 334's `FORBIDDEN_GRANT` refusal of a granted set naming the gate or the admit decider; the item's job is to make the boundary a stated property of the lifecycle rather than a property of one refusal, and to guarantee that no stage can widen G8. The lifecycle is event-driven (gaps trigger it, not a schedule) and its termination is a proof obligation: a proposal that cannot reach a verdict halts rather than looping. Exit: a proposal is carried through all five stages with one recorded verdict, and a proposal that would grant itself a wider authority is refused at admission rather than at the decide stage. Owner: TBD (issue #1194).
    **The item's scope is two lists, and the review supplies both.** What evolution MAY do is behave differently inside declared bounds: rebalance workloads, update caches, choose a better model, adjust bounded thresholds, replace a component with an admitted equivalent, learn which peers are reliable, and optimise a workflow within its declared limits — across seven surfaces the review enumerates as policies, routing, components, workflows, memory, runtime, and device placement. What stays outside the lifecycle because it is the authority rather than the behaviour: granting a new capability, disabling a taint check, widening secret access, removing an approval requirement, changing rollback semantics, replacing the admission kernel, and altering what "committed" means. Those seven are not "hard to reach" — they ARE the boundary the invariant names; recording them as a set means the lifecycle states *which* mechanism refuses each one (the grant refusal of item 334, the taint checker, the approval authority, the commit semantics of item 245) rather than leaving the boundary to be inferred from whichever refusal a given proposal happens to hit first. The proposal kinds are enumerated likewise, and none of them is an authority change: a candidate component, a candidate policy, a candidate routing rule, or a candidate tool adapter. The gate each stage cites is then the review's own checklist, every entry of which this file already owns — declared capabilities (the G8 boundary audit), secret and taint flows (G-SECRET-FLOW), resource ceilings, component and service compatibility (the §5 admission gate), teardown and recovery behaviour (G5/G7 and the R4 residue proof), model and output schema compatibility, policy and approval requirements (item 55), shadow and canary evidence (518), and whether rollback remains possible (item 65).
    Two entries on that list are named by the review as requirements with **no owner on this file**, and are recorded here so they are not lost: a **control plane separated from the data plane**, so a proposal cannot be published over the same channel that serves traffic; and an **externally governed root of trust**, which is the standing question that item 475's attestation and item 55's operator authority only partly answer, because a root of trust the system administers itself is not a root of trust.
    **Three modes, and one of them may not act.** The review splits the work this lifecycle governs three ways, and the split is a rule rather than a label: a **repair** mode fixes a fault the trace has already localized, which is item 62's shape and needs no new authority; a **feature** mode implements a declared gap against the tests that gap names; and an **evolution** mode proposes a behaviour change inside the two scope lists above — and evolution mode **produces a proposal only**. It may not merge, admit, activate or deploy, because a loop that can activate its own proposal is this item's lifecycle with the verdict removed, which is the one thing the invariant forbids. The three modes share a single evidence discipline, which the next section's items own rather than this one: the curriculum a mode draws its tasks from (535), the reward that scores its output and the retention rule (536), and the scoring set it is not allowed to have read (537). Recorded here so this item is not rebuilt when those three land — 520 owns the lifecycle and the authority boundary; they own what makes a candidate's pass believable.

522. (issue #1196) **🚧 PARTIAL, SLICES 1 TO 5 LANDED (2026-09-20 to 2026-09-25, merged `21c1cc5cd`, `8a41acdc0`, `4cbd9de8c`, `90bb7f0d5`, `7ac39d915`): the reversibility classification in `src/revl/ui_family.py`, refused at the declaration site under G4 and pinned by `tests/test_ui_transaction_classification_522.py`; five disjoint residue states printed by `revl erase-report`, the aggregate being the WEAKEST part; the LIFO run `compensation_run` in `src/revl/ui_transaction.py`, keyed on the failing step (PR 1386, completed by PR 1412, which closed issue #1327); a postcondition bound to its step by target provenance rather than by position (issue #1370); and a target that must come from a crossing that resolved it (PR 1416, issue #1371), while that it is this step's target and still fresh needs item 539's substrate. STILL NOT BUILT is execution: no compensation is performed (PR 1536 in review runs them on the python tier, toward issue #1369, with PR 1528 and PR 1522 stacked on it), there is no `transaction` construct so the unit is inferred, and a compensation that FAILS has no residue word. UI TRANSACTIONS WITH VERIFIED POSTCONDITIONS AND HONEST NON-INVERTIBILITY (2026-09-19, external review).** A sequence of UI actions has no failure semantics: if the third of five steps fails, the external UI is left in a state no inverse describes and the residue proof has nothing to measure. Declare the transaction with explicit phases (observe, plan, stage, preview, confirm, commit, verify, compensate) and a typed postcondition per step; compensations run LIFO when a postcondition is unmet; preview and confirm reuse the existing approval authority rather than inventing a second one. The item-243 witness pair is being extended to a target whose state revl does not own, so the honest limit belongs in the item: a UI action is frequently NOT invertible, and the transaction must be able to report `uncompensated` rather than claim a clean teardown. Exit: a mid-transaction failure runs the registered compensations in order, and a transaction over a step with no inverse reports `uncompensated` instead of `no_residue`. Owner: TBD (issue #1196).
    The classification this item must state is the review's five-way one — **reversible, compensatable, requires confirmation, irreversible, unknown** — because it is what decides whether a step may run at all: an action classified irreversible or unknown is not silently executed, and for those classes the honest answer is the confirmation the approval authority already owns rather than a compensation invented for a target whose state revl does not own. Classification and the phase list are one mechanism rather than two: `commit` is where a classified-irreversible step is permitted to cross, `compensate` is where a classified-compensatable one is undone, and a `reversible` claim that turns out false is exactly what the residue report exists to make visible.

524.
